This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. <note>A book in the "public domain" is no longer owned by an individual or corporation, meaning it belongs to the public and can be used by anyone for any purpose.</note> A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia <note>Marginalia refers to the marks, notes, or scribbles made by previous readers or owners in the margins of a page, often providing clues to how the book was used in the past.</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying. <note>Automated querying involves using software scripts to rapidly download or search through large amounts of data without human intervention.</note>

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR (Optical Character Recognition) is the technology that "reads" the shapes of letters on a scanned page and converts them into searchable digital text.</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* <note>Attribution is the practice of acknowledging the source or creator of a work.</note> The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

->[The page is filled with a dense, repeating floral pattern. It consists of finely line-drawn flowers resembling daisies, arranged in a continuous, interlocking design that covers the entire surface.]<-

<note>In traditional bookbinding, an endpaper (or "pastedown") is the leaf of paper used to line the inner surface of the book's cover. These were often decorated with marbled, block-printed, or floral patterns to add elegance and to hide the raw edges of the leather or cloth covering. This specific daisy motif is characteristic of nineteenth-century decorative stationery and bookbinding aesthetics.</note>

# GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA
<note>Giordano Bruno (1548–1600) was an Italian philosopher, poet, and cosmological theorist known for his theories on the infinite universe. He often referred to himself as "The Nolan" because he was born in Nola, Italy.</note>

<note>original: "Opera Latine Conscripta"</note>

->EDITED BY<-

->**V. IMBRIANI and C. M. TALLARIGO**<-
<note>Vittorio Imbriani and Francesco Saverio Tallarigo were prominent Italian scholars and literary critics of the late 19th century.</note>

### VOLUME II.

->CONTAINING:<-

1. **On the Shadows of Ideas.**
<note>original: "De Umbris Idearum." This is one of Bruno's most important works on mnemonics (memory systems) and Neoplatonism.</note>
2. **The Art of Memory.**
<note>original: "Ars Memoriae." A treatise on the "Method of Loci" and other techniques used to organize knowledge within the mind.</note>
3. **The Incantation of Circe.**
<note>original: "Cantus Circaeus." A work presented as a dialogue on the nature of human vices and the transmutation of characters, using the mythological figure of Circe.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the archival library markings of the previous page to the formal editorial foreword of the book. It introduces the scholars responsible for this 19th-century reprint of Giordano Bruno's 16th-century Latin texts.</meta>

# FOREWORD

Following the death of Professor **Francesco Fiorentino**, those who continued the reprinting of the **Latin Works** of Giordano Bruno—**Vittorio Imbriani** and **Carlo Maria Tallarigo**—adhered strictly to the standards by which that illustrious philosopher and man of letters had undertaken this newest edition. These standards were extensively detailed in his letter to the Minister **Francesco De Sanctis**, which served as the preface to the first volume of these works, published between 1879 and 1884 and divided into two parts. In fact, especially regarding punctuation and general spelling, they applied those standards with even greater rigor than their original author, for it was their firm opinion that a reprint—especially of an extremely rare book surviving in only a few copies—should be, as far as possible, a facsimile. Since Imbriani has also passed away, with only the first ten sheets of the present volume having been printed, the undersigned considered it a sacred duty to continue the path traced by his two illustrious friends and masters.

This second volume of Bruno’s **Latin Works** is, therefore, a faithful copy of the Paris Edition of 1582. This edition was, as everyone knows, overseen by Giordano Bruno himself, who, in a section titled *Regarding Errors that disturb the understanding* <note>original: "Pro Erratis, quæ intellectum turbant"</note> placed at the end of *On the Shadows of Ideas* <note>original: "De Umbris Idearum"</note>, corrected several errors of

<summary>This foreword explains the editorial history of the collection, noting that it is a faithful facsimile of the 1582 Paris edition of Giordano Bruno’s Latin works, continued by successive scholars after the deaths of their predecessors.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Francesco Fiorentino, Vittorio Imbriani, Carlo Maria Tallarigo, On the Shadows of Ideas, Latin Works, Renaissance philosophy, editorial history, 19th-century scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the editorial foreword from the previous page, detailing the specific typographic choices and corrections made in this 1886 reprint of Giordano Bruno's works.</meta>

— IV —

press. And these errors, naturally, have been amended, each in its place, in the text of the present reprint. But this was not done for a few other typographical errors not recorded in the *Brunian Correction List* <note>original: "Errata-Corrige Bruniano"</note>: as, for example, on page 25, line 18, *edoremur* <note>a likely typo for "we were being taught"</note>, which it seems should say *edoceremur*; on page 42, line 25, *inexis-tentemque*; on page 59, line 14, *in timiusque*; on page 79, line 15, *aptasunt*; on page 80, line 11, *offerunti*, which, evidently, stand there by a printing mistake in place of *inexsistentemque* <note>"and non-existent"</note>, *intimiusque* <note>"and more inward"</note>, *apta sunt* <note>"they are fit"</note>, and *offerunt* <note>"they offer"</note>, and which the reader will correct for themselves.

Such scrupulous fidelity to the original edition might, perhaps, seem excessive. It is certain, however, that whoever has not seen that edition (and, undoubtedly, there will be many, so rare is it), will be able to say, seeing this one, without fear of being mistaken:
> “This reprint is exactly the same; if you except the changed characters <note>The font or typeface used in the 19th century versus the 16th century.</note>, the omitted abbreviations, and the different paper, I have the original before me.”

As for the printing corrections, the greatest diligence possible has been applied. Once the volume was printed, reread, and compared with the text, no other error was found except, on page 25, an *adsimilitam* for *adsimilatam* <note>"assimilated" or "made similar"</note>. And if this is the only one, as is to be hoped, the surviving editor of the reprint of this volume will have reason to call himself content with his work.

->Naples, June 15, 1886.<-

->**Carlo Maria Tallarigo.**<-

<summary>In this concluding section of the foreword, editor Carlo Maria Tallarigo explains the decision to preserve minor original typos to maintain the reprint's status as a faithful facsimile, dating the work June 1886.</summary>
<keywords>Carlo Maria Tallarigo, Naples, typography, editorial standards, facsimile, Giordano Bruno, Latin Works, 19th-century publishing</keywords>

<meta>This page is the original title page of Giordano Bruno’s "De Umbris Idearum" (On the Shadows of Ideas), originally published in 1582. It follows the editorial preface of the 1886 reprint which described the typographical fidelity of the new edition to this original source.</meta>

# GIORDANO
## BRUNO OF NOLA <note>original: "Nolanus"; Bruno consistently identified himself by his birthplace, Nola, near Naples.</note>

### ON THE SHADOWS OF IDEAS.

->Involving the art of Seeking, Dis-<-
->covering, Judging, Ordering,<-
->and Applying:<-

->*Explained for internal writing, and for no ordinary*<-
->*operations through the memory.* <note>"Internal writing" refers to the "Art of Memory," a technique where information is "written" onto mental images rather than paper.</note><-

->TO HENRY III, THE MOST SE-<-
->rene King of the French and of the Poles, etc.<-

### PROTESTATION. <note>A formal declaration or warning to the reader.</note>

> *We are deep shadows; do not trouble us, you fools.*
> *This weighty work seeks not you, but the learned.* <note>original: "Vmbra profunda sumus, nè nos vexetis inepti. / Non vos, sed doctos tam graue quærit opus." This couplet warns that the book contains difficult, esoteric knowledge not meant for the uninitiated.</note>

->PARIS,<-
->At the house of Gilles Gourbin, under the<-
->sign of Hope, opposite the College<-
->of Cambrai. <note>original: "gymnasij Cameracensis"; the Collège de Cambrai in Paris, where Bruno often lectured.</note><-

->1582.<-

---

->WITH ROYAL PRIVILEGE. <note>A legal notice indicating the King granted the printer the exclusive right to publish this work.</note><-

<summary>This is the title page for Giordano Bruno's seminal 1582 work on mnemonics and Neoplatonism, dedicated to King Henry III of France. It introduces his "Art of Memory" as a sophisticated system of "internal writing" intended for a scholarly audience.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of Giordano Bruno’s "De Umbris Idearum" (1582). Having established the work's dedication to King Henry III and its scholarly nature, Bruno now addresses the reader directly with a poetic warning and encouragement about the difficulty of the text.</meta>

# GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA <note>original: "PH. IORD. BR. NOL."; an abbreviation for Philippus Iordanus Brunus Nolanus.</note> TO THE FRIENDLY
## AND STUDIOUS
### Reader.

*It is set in high relief,*
*the face of Diana in Chios,* <note>Bruno references a famous statue on the island of Chios mentioned by the Roman naturalist Pliny. The statue was sculpted such that its expression seemed to change based on the viewer's position.</note>
*Which seems sad to those entering the temple,*
*But joyful to those leaving.*

*And the letter of Pythagoras,* <note>The Greek letter 'Y' (Upsilon). Pythagoras used it as a symbol for the path of life, which forks into two directions: the easy path of vice and the difficult, narrow path of virtue.</note>
*Driven by a two-pronged division,*
*Which shows a grim face on the right-hand path:*
*Yet bestows the finest end.*

*Of the Shadows, which from the deep*
*Darkness have emerged,*
*In the end they shall become pleasing, though now harsher*
*Both the face and the letter.* <note>Bruno is warning the reader that his book, "On the Shadows of Ideas," will be difficult and "grim" at first, much like the path of virtue or the entrance to Diana's temple, but will prove rewarding to those who persevere.</note>

---

<summary>In this poetic preface, Bruno uses classical metaphors—a shifting statue of Diana and the Pythagorean fork in the road—to warn readers that his work is difficult at first but ultimately rewarding. It sets a mystical and demanding tone for his treatise on memory and ideas.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Diana of Chios, Pythagoras, Pythagorean Y, Shadows of Ideas, Neoplatonism, Classical Allusions</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page contains the formal dedication of the work "On the Shadows of Ideas" (1582) to King Henry III of France. Following his introductory poem to the reader, Bruno here addresses his royal patron, justifying the importance of his mnemonic system and seeking the King's protection and judgment.</meta>

# TO HENRY III, MOST SE-
# RENE KING OF THE FRENCH, AND OF THE PO-
### les, etc. Phi-
### lotheus Giordano
### Bruno of Nola. <note>Bruno uses the name "Philotheus," which means "Lover of God," a common pseudonym in his writings to emphasize the spiritual or philosophical nature of his work.</note>

<insert>BRITISH MUSEUM LIBRARY</insert>

->Heartiest Greetings.<- <note>original: "S. P.", an abbreviation for "Salutem Plurimam," a standard formal salutation in Latin letters.</note>

*Who is unaware (Most Sacred Majesty) that principal gifts are due to principal men; greater gifts to those who are greater; and the most principal gifts to the greatest of men? Therefore, let no one doubt why this work—accounted among the greatest because of the nobility of the subject with which it deals, the uniqueness of the invention upon which it relies, and the weight of the demonstration through which it is communicated—has looked toward you: an extraordinary spectacle for the nations, most distinguished by the virtue of an outstanding mind, most celebrated for the height of a sublime intellect, and therefore most rightly to be honored by the devotion of all the most illustrious, magnanimous, and learned men. It is yours to accept it with a gracious spirit, to protect it with your great favor, and to examine it with mature judgment, as you appear eminently noble, powerful, and wise.* <note>Henry III (1551–1589) was a noted patron of the arts and was particularly interested in the "art of memory," which Bruno claimed to teach. This dedication was a strategic move to secure the King's protection during Bruno's stay in Paris.</note>

->FAREWELL.<-

<vocab>Henry III, Giordano Bruno, Philotheus, dedication, art of memory</vocab>

<summary>Giordano Bruno formally dedicates his treatise on memory and ideas to King Henry III of France, praising the King's intellect and requesting royal patronage for his "unique invention." The dedication frames the work as a gift worthy of a king due to its philosophical nobility and scientific weight.</summary>
<keywords>Henry III of France, Giordano Bruno, royal patronage, Art of Memory, Renaissance philosophy, De Umbris Idearum</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues from the formal dedication to King Henry III. Following the prose address, Bruno transitions into a series of cryptic, allegorical poems. This specific section introduces the persona of "Merlin," a figure Bruno uses to deliver satirical warnings about the nature of true versus false knowledge.</meta>

## MERLIN THE ARTIST <note>original: "MERLINUS ART." Merlin is a recurring character in Bruno’s dialogues, often representing a mysterious or ancient source of wisdom. "Art." is likely an abbreviation for "Artifex" (Artist) or "of the Art" (referring to the Art of Memory).</note>

*There is one who painted farmyard roosters,* <term>farmyard roosters: "gallos gallinaceos," literally domestic poultry. In this allegory, they represent superficial or fake creations.</term>
*Who, since he is not entirely unwise:*
*So that his clumsy lines might not be more easily detected,*
*The inept strokes of an inept artist:*
*He has arranged little servants and little friends;*
*Through whom he wishes the natural roosters to be driven far away.* <note>Bruno is satirizing rival teachers or philosophers who surround themselves with "yes-men" or "servants" to prevent their flawed theories (the "painted roosters") from being compared to the actual truth (the "natural roosters").</note>

*Since you are not ignorant of this, take heed*
*When you bring a true rooster near to the painted ones,*
*Which cause the long-eared ones to marvel:* <note>original: "auriculatos." This refers to those with long ears, a classical allusion to asses or fools (like King Midas) who lack the discernment to tell the difference between artifice and reality.</note>
*Lest, driven away by a troublesome servant, you grieve.*

<summary>This page contains an allegorical poem featuring Merlin, who warns against "inept artists" (false scholars) who use deception and assistants to hide the poor quality of their work from those seeking the truth.</summary>
<keywords>Merlin, allegory, satire, art of memory, farmyard roosters, imitation vs reality, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical sequence of "Merlin," shifting from the warning about inept artists and their cronies to a specific warning for a "Sober Judge." While the previous page dealt with the creation of false knowledge, this page addresses the superficial consumption of knowledge and the danger of judging things one has not truly mastered.</meta>

# MERLIN TO THE SOBER JUDGE <note>original: "MERL. IVDICI SOBRIO." The title addresses a "Sober Judge," a person who considers himself moderate and sensible, but whom Merlin is about to expose as shallow.</note>

->*There is a river in Phrygia called the Gallus,*<- <note>original: "Gallus." A river in Asia Minor. According to classical legend, its water, if drunk in moderation, was medicinal, but if drunk in excess, it caused madness.</note>
->*From which, if you drink but a little,*<-
->*It heals the ailments of the body.*<-

->*But if you swallow it immoderately, it will swallow you up,*<-
->*Until you breathe your last breath,*<-
->*Never to drink again.*<-

->*So also the letters of wisdom, when touched but slightly:*<- <term>wisdom: "sophia." In this context, it refers to deep philosophical or occult knowledge.</term>
->*They contribute to the civic life,*<-
->*And provide the greatest delight.*<-

->*If you gorge yourself on them too much, they will trouble you,*<-
->*And drive you into madness,*<-
->*Or toward a headlong pursuit of glory.*<- <note>Bruno often warned that the study of deep philosophy could be dangerous for those without the right temperament, leading to intellectual vanity or a break from reality.</note>

->*Since, therefore, you have until now been made prudent,*<-
->*Lest you should incur such great harm,*<-
->*By the recommendation of your masters,*<-

->*It has pleased you only to sip at wisdom,*<- <term>to sip: "pitissare." A rare term meaning to taste or sip wine, usually used to describe a superficial sampling rather than drinking deeply.</term>
->*Only to touch it with your lips,*<-
->*And to catch its scent with your nose.*<-

->*Therefore, I declare that you do not act well,*<-
->*While you hasten here as a judge,*<-
->*To shake the ears of Midas.*<- <note>original: "Midæ auriculas." In Greek mythology, King Midas was given the ears of a donkey (an ass) as a punishment for his poor judgment in a musical contest between Apollo and Pan. To "shake the ears of Midas" means to display one's own foolishness or lack of discernment while attempting to act as a critic.</note>

<summary>In this poem, Merlin compares the study of wisdom to a river that heals in small doses but causes madness in excess, ultimately mocking a "judge" who has only superficially studied and yet presumes to critique others.</summary>
<keywords>Merlin, Phrygia, Gallus river, wisdom, Midas, satire, moderation, intellectual vanity</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of "Merlin," the prophetic voice often used by Giordano Bruno. Following the previous page's warning to the "Sober Judge" about the dangers of merely "sipping" at wisdom, this page addresses the "Apt Judge"—or perhaps an ironically "self-appointed" judge—regarding the natural limitations of the mind and the necessity of the right method (the thread) when navigating the labyrinth of knowledge.</meta>

# MERLIN TO THE APT JUDGE <note>original: "MERL. APTO IVDICI." The title addresses an "Apt" or "Fit" Judge, potentially a sarcastic contrast to the "Sober Judge" of the previous page, or a warning to those who believe they are naturally prepared for deep philosophy without proper training.</note>

*It is as when a dog comes to the plow;*
*Or as when a Camel wishes to climb to the stars;*
*It is as when, with a frog leading the way, a shrew-mouse swims the river;* <note>A reference to Aesop’s fable where a frog ties a mouse to its leg to help it cross a river, only to dive down and drown it. It signifies a dangerous or deceptive guide.</note>
*As when slow donkeys hurry to the hunt,*
*Or a cuckoo attempts to snare wolves,*
*It is as when pigs desire to fly—*
*There is something here poorly suited by nature.* <note>original: "natura malè appositum." Merlin lists "adynata" (impossibilities) to show that some people attempt tasks for which they have no natural "aptitude," despite the title's address.</note>

*However, this is not a fault of the Instrument,* <term>Instrument: "Organele." This likely refers to the "Organon" or the logical/mnemonic system Bruno is teaching. He argues that if the student fails, it is due to their own nature, not his method.</term>
*Nor of the one inviting you to dig,*
*Or of those carrying out fishing expeditions,*
*Or of the one who must fly through the air with suitable wings,*
*Or of the teacher of the hunt and the snare.*

*If you feel yourselves to be apt diggers,*
*And not at all unsuited for flying,*
*For fishing, for hunting, and for snaring,*
*And if, therefore, there are no grounds for lamentation:*
*I will grant this to you, provided that you grant me this:*
*That you have entered the labyrinth without a thread.* <note>original: "labyrinthum sine filo." A reference to the myth of Theseus, who needed Ariadne’s thread to find his way out of the Minotaur’s labyrinth. Merlin suggests that even a "fit" person will be lost in complex philosophy without the proper "thread" or method.</note>

<vocab>Merl (Merlin), Camelus (Camel), Organele (The little Instrument/Method), labyrinthum (labyrinth), natura (nature), malè (poorly), minimè (not at all), aucupari (to snare/fowling), effosores (diggers/excavators)</vocab>

<summary>Merlin uses a series of absurd animal metaphors to argue that failure in deep study results from a lack of natural aptitude or a lack of proper method ("the thread"). He defends his "Instrument" (his philosophical system) against claims that it is at fault for a student's confusion.</summary>
<keywords>aptitude, nature vs. art, the Labyrinth, Ariadne's thread, logical method, animal allegories, Organon</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the cryptic warnings of "Merlin" to the formal opening of Bruno's "Apologeticus" dialogue. While the previous page used the persona of Merlin to warn about the necessity of having the right nature to learn, this page introduces Philotheus (Bruno’s philosophical alter-ego) and the god Hermes. The solar imagery here continues the theme of light and shadow: the "Shadows of Ideas" are the means by which the human mind perceives the light of the divine intellect.</meta>

# OF PHILOTHEUS JORDANUS

## BRUNO THE NOLAN’S Preliminary
## Apologetic Dialogue for the
## Shadows of Ideas concerning his
## invention of memory.

---

->INTERLOCUTORS:<-

->HERMES. PHILOTHIMUS. LOGIFER.<- 
<note>Hermes represents the messenger of the gods and wisdom; Philothimus translates to "Lover of Honor" or "Lover of Value"; Logifer means "Word-bearer" or "Logic-bringer." These three represent different aspects of the soul's engagement with knowledge.</note>

**HERMES.** — *Go forth, my book. For you are not unaware that it is the same sun, and the same art. The same sun makes known the deeds of one person to their honor, yet brings forth the actions of another to their reproach. At the sun's presence, the screech-owls of the night, the toad, the basilisk, and the horned owl are saddened—creatures that are solitary, nocturnal, and sacred to Pluto.* <note>Pluto is the Roman god of the underworld; here he represents the realm of darkness and ignorance.</note> *But the Rooster, the Phoenix, the Swan, the Cygnet, the Eagle, the Lynx, the Ram, and the Lion rejoice. As the sun rises, the workers of darkness huddle together in their dens, but man and the animals of light go forth to their work. The sun invites the latter to labor; it pushes the former into idleness. The lupine and the Heliotrope* <term>Heliotrope: "Elitropia." A plant famous in ancient and Renaissance lore for turning its face to follow the sun; a symbol of the soul following the light of Truth.</term> *turn toward it, but the herbs and flowers of the night turn away from it. It lifts up thin vapors into the appearance of a cloud, yet casts down to the earth those condensed into water. To some it grants perennial and continuous light; to others, light that comes only in turns. The unerring intellect teaches that this sun stands still; while Sense* <note>original: "Hunc intellectus non errans stare docet; Sensus..." Bruno is making an early, coded reference to the Copernican theory that the sun is stationary at the center of the system, contrary to what our physical senses tell us.</note>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the "Apologeticus" dialogue, using a monologue by Hermes to establish the Sun as a metaphor for Truth, which illuminates some while blinding others. It introduces Bruno’s philosophical pseudonym, Philotheus, and hints at his heliocentric views through the metaphor of the "standing sun."</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Hermes, Sun metaphor, Shadows of Ideas, Heliocentrism, Memory Arts, Neoplatonism, Philotheus</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Hermes's speech from the previous page. Having established that the sun affects different creatures differently, Hermes now explains that the apparent movement and changes of the sun are actually due to the position and rotation of the Earth. This transition moves from biological metaphors to cosmological arguments used to support Bruno's philosophical "Shadows."</meta>

...but a deceptive sense suggests that it moves. This sun rises for that part of the **rotating earth** <term>rotating earth: "terræ girantis." Bruno was a passionate early supporter of the idea that the Earth moves, which was a radical and dangerous idea in the late 16th century.</term> exposed to it: yet at the same time, it sets for the part situated otherwise. The same sun apparently circles the horizons which they call **arctic** through the differences of right and left; yet to no others does it seem to traverse the upper and lower arc.

This sun appears larger to one occupying the high point of the earth's circuit: but to one holding the lowest point (as being further distant from it), it appears smaller. In some portions of the semicircuit it withdraws slowly; but in others, it is absent swiftly. To one on the earth leaning toward the South, the sun becomes more Northern: but to one hurrying toward the North, it is made to appear more Southern. To those having a **straight horizon** <note>original: "Rectum... Orizonta." Referring to those at the equator where the sun's path is perpendicular to the horizon.</note>, it receives its width into equal scales on either side; but to those holding an oblique one, it falls into unequal ones. 

The same sun grants darkness perpetually measured against the light to those inhabiting the space between the two middle parallels of this mass <note>The region between the Tropics.</note>: but to others, it does so only for a defined time. If the divine earth, nourishing us on her back, should cast our face toward it, she will obtain for us its oblique rays: but for those whose crown of the head she has placed directly beneath it, she obtains straight rays. 

Certain bodies of the world moved near to it—which many understand to be **living beings and gods** <note>original: "animalia deòsque." Bruno believed the planets and stars were giant living animals or "star-gods" swimming through an infinite ether.</note> second only to the one prince—receive light from its peak or **apogee** (as they call it); while others have it in the opposite position, or in the middle latitudes and intervals. Of this same whole, the **moon** (which many philosophers understand to be another earth) receives free light in its hemisphere turned toward the sun: but this [our earth], by the interposition of its globe, shows to that same sun a saddened, shadowed face on the moon's opposite hemisphere. 

Therefore, the **Sun** remains and persists as one and the same perpetually; yet to different people, and to those situated in different ways...

<summary>Hermes explains that the Sun is a constant, singular light, and all its perceived changes—rising, setting, size, and eclipses—are actually the result of the Earth's rotation and the observer's relative position.</summary>
<keywords>rotation of the earth, perspective, arctic horizons, apogee, moon as another earth, solar constancy, planetary life</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous discussion on the sun's relative effects. Hermes is reflecting on the nature of his own work, "On the Shadows of Ideas," which he carries as a manuscript. The conversation shifts from cosmology to the danger and necessity of publishing radical philosophical ideas.</meta>

...one is made one way, and another another way. In no different a manner should we believe that solar art to be one thing for some, and another for others.

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — Why is it, Hermes, that you are speaking to yourself? What is this little book you hold in your hands?

**HERMES.** — It is a book *On the Shadows of Ideas contracted for internal writing* <note>original: "De vmbris Idearum ad internam scripturam contractis." This refers to Bruno's system of mnemonics (the art of memory), where "internal writing" means storing information within the mind using organized images.</note>, about which I am undecided: whether it ought to come forth, or whether it should remain forever in the same darkness in which it once lay hidden.

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — Why is that?

**HERMES.** — Because its author offers himself up (as they say) as a target at which armed archers of more than one kind are aiming. <note>Bruno often used the metaphor of a target to describe how his controversial ideas—such as the infinite universe and the motion of the earth—made him a mark for critics and the Inquisition.</note>

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — If that were something to be feared and avoided by everyone, no one would ever have attempted worthy works; nothing good or outstanding would ever have come into view. The **providence of the gods** <note>original: "providentia deorum."</note> does not cease (as the **Egyptian Priests** <term>Egyptian Priests: Bruno was deeply influenced by "Hermeticism," a philosophical tradition he believed originated in ancient Egypt, which emphasized divine wisdom hidden in nature.</term> said) to send certain **Mercuries** <term>Mercuries: "Mercurios," referring to messengers of divine wisdom or intellectual reformers like Hermes Trismegistus.</term> to men at ordained times, even if they foreknow that these messengers will be poorly or barely received. Nor does the intellect, or that sensible sun, ever cease to shine simply because we do not all perceive it, nor do we perceive it at all times.

**LOGIFER.** — I would easily agree with those who believe that such things should by no means be made public. I hear Philothimus is doubtful on this matter; if he had heard with his own ears the things we have heard, he would surely rather throw these things into the fire to be burned than see to their publication. For these things have thus far brought their teacher a harvest that is hardly pleasant; now, I do not know what he can hope for in the future, except...

<sig>2</sig>

<summary>Hermes expresses hesitation about publishing his work on the "Shadows of Ideas" due to potential persecution, while Philothimus argues that divine messengers must share their wisdom regardless of the risk. Logifer, the skeptic, warns that the author has already suffered for his teachings and should consider burning the manuscript instead.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes, Philothimus, Logifer, internal writing, art of memory, Egyptian Priests, Mercuries, providence, censorship, persecution</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding Hermes' controversial book on the Art of Memory. On the previous page, Hermes expressed hesitation about publishing his work, fearing the "archers" (critics) who might target him. The dialogue now brings in Logifer, a personification of pedantic logic, to debate the potential reception of the book.</meta>

...for the very few who are able to understand these things by themselves will be least able to bring a right judgment concerning them.

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — Do you hear what that man says?

**HERMES.** — I hear, but so that I may hear more, discuss it amongst yourselves.

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — I will debate with you then, **Logifer** <term>Logifer: A name meaning "word-bearer," used by Bruno to represent a pedantic scholar or a logic-chopper who values words over actual wisdom.</term>, and I would say this first: your statement has no power of persuasion; rather, the very strength of your reasoning serves to confirm the opposite opinion. For those few who have attained an understanding of this invention—among whose number Hermes and I belong—would extol it with no small praise. Those, however, who have understood it not at all, will be able neither to praise nor to criticize it.

**LOGIFER.** — You speak of what *ought* to be, not what *will* be, what *is*, or what *has been*. Many, since they do not understand, for the very reason that they do not understand—and moreover out of the wicked spirit by which they are driven—heap many slanders upon the author himself and his art. Have you not heard with your own ears **Doctor Bobus** <note>original: "doctorem Bobum." A satirical name derived from the Latin "bovis" (ox), implying the doctor is a dullard.</note>, who said there is no **art of memory** <term>art of memory: A system of mental techniques used to organize and recall vast amounts of information using vivid images and structured mental locations.</term>, but that it is acquired only through habit and the frequent repetition of mental paths—by seeing again and again what was seen, and by receiving again and again what was heard?

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — If this man had a tail, he would be a **long-tailed monkey** <note>original: "cercopitecus." Philothimus is mocking the "Doctor" as a mere mimic or an unintelligent beast.</note>.

**LOGIFER.** — What will you say to **Master Anthoc** <note>Another satirical name, likely implying someone superficial or "flowery" but lacking substance.</note>, who thinks that those who perform memory feats beyond the common sort are **sorcerers or possessed by spirits** <term>possessed by spirits: original "energumenos." In the Renaissance, exceptional intellectual or mnemonic feats were sometimes viewed with suspicion as being the result of demonic intervention or forbidden magic.</term>, or men of some such kind? You see how much he has grown old in his study of letters <note>A jab at scholars who study for a lifetime but never gain true insight.</note>.

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — I would not doubt that this man is the grandson of that **donkey** who was preserved in **Noah's Ark** <note>original: "Archa Noe." A humorous insult suggesting the critic is a "pure-bred" fool whose lineage traces back to the original animal saved from the flood.</note> to ensure the survival of the species.

<summary>Philothimus and Logifer debate how the public will receive Hermes' work on memory, contrasting the praise of the wise with the slanders of pedantic "doctors" who mistake mental art for either simple repetition or dark magic.</summary>
<keywords>Logifer, Art of Memory, Doctor Bobus, Master Anthoc, Noah's Ark, mnemonics, pedantry, sorcery</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Logifer and Philothimus. Following the previous page where Logifer expressed skepticism about the Art of Memory being merely a habit, he now cites several academic and professional authorities who dismiss Hermes’ work as either useless, confusing, or outright nonsensical.</meta>

-> — 11 — <-

**LOGIFER.** — *And Master Roccus, a grand master of arts and medicine, who prefers practical experience to a taught system of memory, would consider these things nonsense rather than skillful instructions.*

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — *He should not look beyond his own chamber pot.* <note>original: "Non ultra matulam." A biting idiom. Since physicians of the era diagnosed patients by inspecting urine in a glass vessel (matula), Philothimus is telling the doctor to stick to his specialty and stop commenting on philosophy.</note>

**LOGIFER.** — *One of the ancient doctors said that this art cannot be accessible to everyone, except for those who already possess a powerful **natural memory**.* <term>natural memory: The innate ability to remember things without the use of specific techniques, as opposed to the "artificial memory" developed through the mnemonic arts.</term>

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — *A worthless, outdated opinion.* <note>original: "Depontana sententia." Literally "a sentence of the bridge-tossed." In ancient Rome, men over sixty (sexagenarians) were called "depontani," implying they were past their prime and should be cast off the bridge. Philothimus uses it to mean the idea is obsolete.</note>

**LOGIFER.** — ***Pharfacon*** <note>Likely a satirical pseudonym for a contemporary critic.</note>, *a doctor of both civil and canon law and a philosopher of letters, feels that this art burdens rather than relieves the mind. For whereas things should be recalled without any art, we are now bound by art to recall things, places, and many images; there is no doubt that the natural memory is more confused and entangled by these.*

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — *The "sharpness" of **Chrysippus** <term>Chrysippus: A Stoic philosopher famous for his intricate, often frustratingly complex logic.</term>! That is an opinion that needs to be carded with a massive iron comb.* <note>Philothimus mocks the "sharpness" of the logic, suggesting it is so tangled and rough it requires a heavy-duty tool to straighten it out.</note>

**LOGIFER.** — ***Doctor Berling*** *said that even the most learned can harvest nothing from Hermes’ speech—I believe this is because he harvests nothing himself.*

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — *Is there any chestnut under those prickly husks?* <note>original: "Sub illis echinis ulla ne castanea?" He compares the difficult language to a chestnut’s spiky outer shell (echinus); he is asking if there is actually any nourishment inside the difficult exterior.</note>

**LOGIFER.** — ***Master Maines*** *says: "Even if it pleases everyone, it will never please me."*

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — *Nor will the wine he never tastes.*

**LOGIFER.** — *What do you think that friend of yours—whom you know well—will feel about this matter?*

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — *Adding cuttlefish ink to a lamp makes men appear as dark as Africans; so too a mind corrupted by envy judges even clearly beautiful things to be foul.*

**LOGIFER.** — *Even the lofty Master Scoppet among...*

<summary>Logifer lists various academic critics who believe the Art of Memory is either redundant for those with good memories or too burdensome for those without. Philothimus dismisses these critics as biased, envious, or out of their depth.</summary>

<keywords>natural memory, artificial memory, Roccus, Pharfacon, Chrysippus, Berling, Maines, envy, mnemonics, satire</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Logifer and Philothimus. They are discussing and refuting critics of the Art of Memory, specifically focusing on physicians who dismiss the art in favor of physical remedies or who lack the intellectual capacity to understand it. Logifer has just finished describing the views of "Pharfacon."</meta>

-> — 12 — <-

...of our own time, easily the foremost among doctors, they say told the author: that he should show him his memory before his art, as it is doubtful whether he, out of disdain or incompetence, was unwilling to perform. <note>The text suggests a physician challenged the author to prove his own memory worked before teaching the system, but the author implies the doctor was either too arrogant or too dull to appreciate it.</note>

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — If he had said to him, "show me your urine before I consider more solid excrement," <note>original: "ostende mihi urinam tuam priusquam solidiora contempler excrementa." Philothimus is returning the doctor's insult with interest, using the language of 16th-century medical diagnosis—inspecting waste—to suggest the doctor's mind is equally foul.</note> perhaps our author would have accommodated him; for he would have received him more hospitably and politely, and in a manner more fitting to his own dignity, duty, and art.

**LOGIFER.** — What shall we say of Master Clyster, <note>A "clyster" was an enema; the name is a satirical jab at a doctor obsessed with physical purging rather than mental cultivation.</note> a doctor of medicine, who must not be outdone by the one just mentioned? For he differs in no way from that man who—following Arnald and the Tiber—wants instead to confer a most tenacious memory upon the forgetful by placing the tongue of a hoopoe upon them. <note>original: "ex Aknaldo & Tiberide... linguam vpupæ impositam." Refers to Arnald of Villanova, a famous medieval physician. The use of a hoopoe's tongue was a folk-magic remedy for memory, which the author here mocks as superstitious nonsense compared to the "Art."</note>

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — Aristotle said: "By playing the lyre, one becomes a lyre-player." <note>original: "cytharizando fit cytharædus." From the *Nicomachean Ethics*, meaning that skills are acquired through practice. Philothimus uses it to mock the doctor's lack of practice in thinking.</note> If someone were to place another brain upon this wretched man (having extracted the one he has), perhaps by practicing medicine he might become a doctor.

**LOGIFER.** — Doctor Carpophorus, following Proculus and Sabinus of Ithaca, <note>likely references to classical or legal authorities used here in a pseudo-medical context.</note> also said that the seat of the mind and memory is divided into three parts. For between the stern and the prow <note>Anatomical metaphors: the "prow" is the anterior (front) of the brain, and the "stern" is the posterior (back).</note> is the middle pineal gland; when we persist in recalling something with the memory, this opens up, providing a passage for the **animal spirit** <term>animal spirit: In early modern medicine, these were "spirits" (a very fine, subtle fluid) that traveled through the nerves to facilitate sensation and movement.</term> from the prow to the stern. Furthermore, the animal spirit never passes through unless it is serene, lucid, and clear. Hence, being dulled by excessive cold, it renders our memory blunt and sluggish. Indeed, if this cold is joined with dryness, it brings on excessive wakefulness and insomnia; if with humidity, lethargy. To drive these away, these things have been devised through art: Exercise that recalls and rouses the senses,

<summary>This page features a satirical debate where the characters mock medical critics of the Art of Memory, contrasting "superstitious" remedies like hoopoe tongues with a pseudo-scientific explanation of how "animal spirits" move through the pineal gland to facilitate recall.</summary>
<keywords>satire, Master Clyster, Arnald of Villanova, Aristotle, pineal gland, animal spirits, medical theory, hoopoe tongue, memory physiology</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Logifer and Philothimus regarding the physical and intellectual requirements for a good memory. The preceding section listed remedies and behaviors recommended by traditional physicians to aid the mind, which the speakers are now critiquing as purely physical compared to the "Art of Memory."</meta>

-> — 13 — <-

...and awakening the vital spirits <note>In early modern medicine, "spirits" were subtle fluids that circulated through the body to facilitate movement, sensation, and thought.</note> as if from a shameful sluggishness and sleep-inducing leisure. Moderate sexual intercourse. Warding off sadness and calling back joy through pleasure. The cleansing of all the body's passages. Rubbing the head with an ivory comb and a rough cloth. The use of lighter or watered-down wines, lest the open veins burn up the blood through the violence of the wine. Closing off the stomach with naturally or artificially <term>styptic: substances that contract tissues or "tighten" the stomach to prevent vapors from rising</term> things, lest the smoke evaporating from the stomach due to the boiling of food—which darkens the mind and intellect—should excite sleep. Abstaining from cold and damp foods, such as fish in general, brains, and marrow, no less than from sharp and "smoky" leeks, radishes, garlic, and onions that have not been softened by fire. The use of aromatic things. Washing the head and feet with a decoction of water in which *Lemon Balm*, *Laurel leaf*, *Fennel*, *Chamomile*, *Reeds*, and similar things have been boiled. The *Pythagorean* exercise performed at twilight, as it is most beneficial for memory, mind, and intellect. <note>The "Pythagorean exercise" involved mentally reviewing everything one did, said, or heard during the day before going to sleep to strengthen the memory.</note> These are the things that can support the memory, along with those which *Democritus*, *Archigenes*, *Alexander*, and *Andronicus the Peripatetic* <note>A group of ancient Greek philosophers and physicians cited here as authorities on the physical soul and memory.</note> handed down in their literary monuments; not those trifles of "arts" which boast that they forge a solid memory through I-know-not-what images and figures.

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — He concluded someone else's speech with his own braying; the venerable doctor acted like both a parrot and an ass. <note>Philothimus is mocking a doctor who merely recites ancient medical advice (like a parrot) but lacks true understanding (like an ass).</note>

**LOGIFER.** — Master Arnophagus, <note>likely a satirical name meaning "Lamb-Eater"</note> skilled in law and statutes and highly esteemed, said that there are many learned men who do not have that skill <gloss>the Art of Memory</gloss>, and they would certainly have it if such a thing actually existed.

**PHILOTHIMUS.** — Reason is a young girl who has not yet grown her teeth: therefore we do not bring a "tooth-breaker" <note>original: "denti frangibulum," a metaphor for a difficult problem or a tool to crack something hard</note> to her.

<summary>This page lists traditional medical and lifestyle remedies for improving memory, such as diet, hygiene, and the Pythagorean evening review, while the dialogue mocks physicians and lawyers who dismiss the formal Art of Memory as non-existent.</summary>
<keywords>medical remedies, hygiene, diet, Pythagorean exercise, vital spirits, legal scholars, Philothimus, Logifer, ancient authorities</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 13. Having discussed the physical preparations for memory, the characters Logifer, Philothimus, and Hermes now begin to critique existing mnemonic systems (the "Art of Memory") and introduce a new method by opening a specific book on the subject.</meta>

-> — 14 — <-

**LOGIFER.** — *The most learned Theologian and most subtle patriarch of letters, Master Psicoteus <note>A symbolic name, likely derived from the Greek for "Soul of God" or "Divine Mind."</note>, declares that he has seen the Art of Tully <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator whose work "Ad Herennium" was the foundation of the Art of Memory.</note>, of Thomas <note>St. Thomas Aquinas, who integrated memory techniques into ethics and theology.</note>, of Albert <note>Albertus Magnus, a medieval scholar known for his work on the soul and memory.</note>, of Alulidis <note>Possibly a reference to an obscure medieval commentator or a corruption of another name.</note>, and of other obscure authors, and he protests that he could draw no juice <note>Meaning he found no practical substance or benefit.</note> from them.*

**PHILOTHIMVS.** — *The judgment of the first tonsure.* <note>The "tonsure" was the shaved patch on a cleric's head. "First tonsure" refers to a beginner or someone with only a superficial, introductory education.</note>

**LOGIFER.** — *And to finally embrace everything in one word: different people feel differently, various people say various things; as many heads, so many opinions.*

**PHILOTHIMVS.** — *And so many voices. Hence crows croak, cuckoos call, wolves howl, pigs grunt, sheep bleat, oxen low, horses neigh, and donkeys bray. It is shameful, said Aristotle, to be anxious about answering every questioner; let oxen low to oxen, horses neigh to horses, and donkeys bray to donkeys; our task is to test something through conversation regarding this man's invention.*

**LOGIFER.** — *Rightly indeed. Let it please Hermes, then, to open the book, so that we may consider the author's own opinions.*

**HERMES.** — *I shall do so most willingly. Behold, I read the prologue <note>original: "prophœmium"</note> of the work.*

> I believe (he says) that it escapes no one that many **arts of memory** <term>memorative arts: systems of mental techniques, like the "Method of Loci," used to store and recall vast amounts of information</term> have been published by others, all and each of which, using the exact same rules, are caught in almost the same difficulty. For this reason, we have decided that it would be better to present the fruits of this invention, through which this illustrious business—for an art so desired—might be handled more seriously, easily, and quickly...

<summary>The characters dismiss traditional memory systems by Cicero and Aquinas as unproductive "animal chatter" and prepare to examine a new, more efficient "Art of Memory" by reading from a new text.</summary>
<keywords>Art of Memory, Cicero, Thomas Aquinas, Albertus Magnus, Master Psicoteus, Mnemonic Rules, Prologue</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>Continuing the dialogue from page 14. Logifer and Philothimus are discussing a specific book or author and contrasting his work with the "herd" of unoriginal compilers who simply aggregate the works of others without understanding.</meta>

-> — 15 — <-

...was being discussed for attaining <note>The sentence likely began on the previous page with a word like "mirabili" (wonderful/remarkable).</note> remarkable things. By seeking out older instructions through long-term practice, they used to drive away more fortunate talents from their pursuit and study with too much persistence; for minds, the more subtle and prompt they are, the less they endure and (to put the matter more clearly) the more impatient they are; for some of these, it is advised to touch on everything rather than to grasp one single thing.

**PHILOTHIMVS.** — *What pleases me about this author is that he does not make himself one of that herd who, gathering the opinions of others from here and there into one place, enroll themselves among the authors laboring for posterity at the expense of others to achieve immortality. For the most part, they set themselves up as teachers of those things of which they have no understanding or logic at all. They often cannot help but—after they have somehow fitted the lion’s skin <note>A reference to Aesop’s fable of the donkey in the lion’s skin, used to describe someone pretending to be more important or learned than they truly are.</note> from the inventions of others onto themselves—burst out more frequently into their own true voice. This happens when they ejaculate something from their own spineless Mars <note>original: "delumbi Marte." A metaphor for weak, unmanly, or effortless work, as Mars was the god of strength and war.</note> (because it is easy to add to things already invented) or vomit from the poverty of their stupid sense. Those, those are the battering rams of infancy, the engines of error, the cannons of ineptitude, and the thunder, lightning, flashes, and great storms of ignorance.*

**LOGIFER.** — *Do you not feel the same about our poem-pickers <term>poem-pickers: carminilegis, those who "harvest" lines from existing poems to stitch together new ones</term> and versifiers <note>original: "versificatoribus." A derogatory term for someone who writes mechanical verse without true poetic inspiration.</note> who sell themselves to us as poets by using the inventions, half-lines, and verses of others as their own?*

**PHILOTHIMVS.** — *Dismiss the poets. For just as we know according to the locations...* <note>The text cuts off here. The mention of "locations" (locis) likely refers to the "Method of Loci," the classic mnemonic technique of placing mental images in specific architectural locations.</note>

<summary>Philothimus and Logifer critique unoriginal scholars and poets who plagiarize the work of others to appear learned. They praise a specific author for his original contribution to the art of memory, contrasting him with those who use "battering rams of ineptitude."</summary>

**LOGIFER.** — *Not about the poets.*

**PHILOTHIMVS.** — *But let us follow what has begun; read on.*

### Hermes

From here (he says), when I applied my mind to comply with certain friends of mine—after other arts of memory <note>term: "memoriæ artes." Systems or techniques used to improve the mind's ability to retain and recall large amounts of information, often using mental imagery.</note> of various kinds, which I directed privately to different people and shared with others along various paths according to their status and capacity—I have compiled this one. It is to be preferred above all others due to the power of the principles contained in it, and to be second to none in its results. In this work, I certainly promise an easy art and a knowledge that is not at all laborious for practice. However, I have made the book itself, with its maxims, by no means accessible to everyone. This is contrary to the habit of those who have handed down books on this art that are easy and brief, but the art itself they made difficult and lengthy. Let only a few of the learned understand this one, but for all who do understand it, let it become useful in practice. Let it be something that everyone, whether unlettered or learned, can easily know and exercise—and yet, which no one can understand without a teacher, except those well-versed in metaphysics and the doctrines of the Platonists <note>Followers of the philosopher Plato. In the Renaissance, Platonic thought was deeply linked to the idea that knowledge is a form of "remembering" what the soul once knew.</note>. For this art has this quality: while it embraces difficult terms and the power of speculative faculties...

<meta>Continuing from the text of Hermes on page 16, the author is discussing the nature and accessibility of his "Art." ...[re]stated regarding suppositions, it can nevertheless be explained to anyone (provided they are not entirely dull-witted), for it contains the most precise <term>terms</term> <note>original: "terminos." In the context of logic and mnemonics, these are the specific words or concepts used to build the system.</note> and those best suited for signifying the things themselves.

This **Art** does not contribute to a simple art of memory alone; rather, it opens the way and introduces the <term>invention</term> <note>term: "inuentionem." From the Latin 'inventio,' this refers to the discovery or creation of new ideas and arguments, a key part of classical rhetoric.</note> of many faculties. Therefore, let those who are granted the ability to perceive its inner workings remember: they must not make it common to everyone without distinction, out of respect for its majesty. They should share its <term>canons</term> <note>original: "canones." The fundamental rules or principles of the mnemonic system.</note> with each of those to whom it is communicated according to the merit and capacity of their ability, explaining them more intensely or more sparingly as appropriate.

Beyond this, let those into whose hands this art falls know this: we are not of such a mind <note>original: "ingenij." Refers to one's natural character, talent, or intellectual disposition.</note> that we are bound to any specific school of foreign philosophy; nor do we despise any way of philosophizing throughout the universe. Indeed, we highly value anyone who, relying on their own intellect for the contemplation of things, has produced something in a skillful and methodical manner.

We do not abolish the mysteries of the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, known for their focus on numbers, harmony, and secret teachings.</note>; we do not belittle the beliefs of the **Platonists**; and, insofar as they have reached a real foundation, we do not despise the reasonings of the **Peripatetics** <note>The school of Aristotle, which emphasized logic and the observation of the physical world.</note>. We say this for this reason: to ease the concern of those who wish to measure other minds by their own talent. Such is that unfortunate class of people who, although they have labored long among the best philosophers, have not advanced their own spirits to the point where they stop lacking their own intellect; they always rely on someone else's. They are more to be pitied than those who, ignorant of their own poverty, dare what should not be dared; and in some part (unless it stems from negligence) they are to be pra[ised]... <note>The text ends mid-word with "lau-," likely intended to be "laudandi" (to be praised).</note>

...must be given. Those of such a kind, when they are filled with the <term>Aristotelian spirit</term> <note>original: "Aristotelico... spiritu." They will claim that "ideas" are merely dreams or monsters. Furthermore, when they encounter the section on the reasoning soul, they will say, "Jordan <note>original: "Iordane." To these people, we wish to make this clear: We too, when we were less wise, occupied our minds with those same things. For at that time (as was fitting), we used faith to grasp the sciences. But now, when by the grace of the higher powers we are able to use what we have acquired and discovered for our own further actions without the just mark of contradiction: if a <term>Platonic</term> <note>Refers to the followers of Plato, who emphasized the world of ideal forms.</note> term is suitable and the intention is useful, it is accepted. If, likewise, <term>Peripatetic</term> <note>original: "Peripateticæ." Another name for the followers of Aristotle, who famously walked while teaching.</note> intentions contribute to a better expression of a matter in this art, they are faithfully admitted.

Let others be judged in the same way. For we do not find a single craftsman who provides everything necessary for a single purpose. The same man, I say, will not forge and perfect the helmet, the shield, the sword, the spears, the banners, the drum, the trumpet, and all the other armaments of a soldier. In the same way, the workshop of Aristotle alone or of Plato alone will not suffice for those attempting greater works of other <term>inventions</term> <note>original: "inuentionum." In this context, it refers to the discovery of new knowledge or mnemonic systems.</note>. Sometimes (though it might seem that we are using familiar terms), that is not because we wish to explain the same intentions usually associated with them. Throughout the universe, however, by the various [writings] of different phi...

<summary>Bruno defends his use of both Platonic and Aristotelian terminology, arguing that no single philosopher possesses all the tools necessary for true discovery. We treat this **Art** under a twofold form and path: one of which is higher and more general, intended for organizing all the operations of the mind; it is also the source <note>original: "caput," literally "head" or "fountainhead."</note> of many methods by which an **artificial memory** can be tested and discovered, as if through various instruments. This first part consists of: 

1. Thirty **intentions of shadows** <note>original: "intentionibus umbrarum." In Bruno’s system, "shadows" are the intermediate stage between the physical world and the divine Ideas; "intentions" refer to the way the mind directs its focus toward these reflections.</note>. 
2. Thirty **concepts of ideas**. 
3. Several **combinations** <note>original: "complexionibus," referring to the systematic joining of different elements.</note> which can be formed from these intentions and concepts through the diligent adaptation of the elements of the **first wheel** to the elements of the **second** <note>Bruno utilizes concentric, rotating wheels—a technique borrowed and adapted from Ramon Llull—to mechanically generate logical and mnemonic associations.</note>. 

The other path which follows is more specific <note>original: "contractior," literally "more contracted" or "narrower."</note> to a certain kind of memory to be acquired through artificial means.

<meta>Continuing from the introduction where Bruno explained that his art of memory is divided into two parts—one theoretical (the Thirty Intentions and Thirty Concepts) and one practical (the mnemonic wheels)—he now begins the first of the Thirty Intentions. # THIRTY INTEN-
## TIONS OF SHADOWS

---

### THE FIRST INTENTION. A.

With the favor of the one God, therefore, and the assistance of the great gods under that same most high prince, we thus begin.

Suggesting the perfection of man and the attainment of the best thing that can be had in this world, the wisest of the Hebrews <note>A reference to King Solomon, traditionally regarded as the author of the Song of Solomon and the wisest of all men.</note> introduces his beloved speaking in this way:

> **I SAT BENEATH THE SHADOW OF THE ONE I HAD DESIRED**. <note>original: "SVB VMBRA ILLIVS QVEM DESIDERAVERAM SEDI." From the Song of Solomon 2:3. Bruno uses this biblical quote to justify his philosophy: since humans cannot look directly at the "sun" of divine Truth, we must sit in its "shadow" to understand it.</note>

For our nature is not so great that it can inhabit the very field of truth by its own capacity; for it is said: *A living man is vanity.* <note>original: "Vanitas homo viuens." From Psalm 39:5.</note> *All is vanity.* <note>original: "Vniuersa vanitas." From Ecclesiastes 1:2.</note> And that which is true and good is one and primary. How can it be that that which is not essentially true, and whose essence is not properly truth, could possess the power and action of truth? Therefore, it is sufficient and quite enough for it to sit beneath the shadow of the good and the true. I do not mean beneath the shadow of a natural or rational truth and goodness (for from that perspective it might be called false and evil), but beneath that which is <term>Metaphysical</term> <note>That which exists beyond the physical world; the ultimate reality.</note>, <term>Ideal</term> <note>Pertaining to the "Ideas," which in Platonic thought are the perfect, divine blueprints for everything in the universe.</note>, and <term>supersubstantial</term> <note>Being that exists above and beyond all material substance.</note>: from which the mind is made a participant in the good and the true according to its ability. Even if the mind does not possess enough to *be* the image of that truth, it is nevertheless made *according* to its image; while of his

<summary>Bruno introduces the first "intention," arguing that human beings cannot perceive absolute truth directly, but must instead perceive it through its "shadows" or reflections. He uses biblical wisdom to argue that inhabiting this shadow is the highest state a human mind can achieve.</summary>
<keywords>shadows of ideas, Solomon, Neoplatonism, metaphysics, divine truth, image of God, participation</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>Continuing from the First Intention, Bruno is explaining how the human mind interacts with "shadows" (mental images) rather than pure, blinding Truth. He is moving from the metaphysical justification for using shadows to a technical definition of what a "shadow" actually is in his system.</meta>

...the soul’s <term>translucency</term> <note>original: "diaphanum." In the philosophy of the time, this refers to a transparent medium (like air or glass) that allows light and images to pass through. Bruno uses it here to describe the soul's capacity to receive knowledge.</note>, bounded by the opacity of the body itself, experiences within the human mind something of an image insofar as it is directed toward it. However, in the internal senses and the reason—where we dwell while living as physical creatures—it experiences the **shadow itself**.

## THE SECOND INTENTION. B.

When you have considered this, I would also like this to occur to you: that you must distinguish the nature of the **shadow** from that of **darkness**. A shadow is not darkness; rather, it is either a trace of darkness within light, or a trace of light within darkness. Or it is a participant in both light and darkness. Or a composite of light and darkness. Or a mixture of light and darkness. Or it is neither light nor darkness, being separated from both. 

And this may be because it is not the full truth of light, or because it is a "false" light. Or perhaps because it is neither true nor false, but a "trace" <note>original: "vestigium." Often translated as "footprint" or "vestige," Bruno uses this to mean a sign or impression left by a higher reality upon a lower one.</note> of that which exists truly or falsely, and so on. For our current purpose, however, let the shadow be considered a **trace of light**, a **participant in light**, and a **light that is not full**. <note>This is a crucial definition: for Bruno, a "shadow" is not "evil" or "ignorance," but a necessary, diluted form of light that the human mind can handle without being overwhelmed.</note>

## THE THIRD INTENTION. C.

Furthermore, since light can be understood in two ways—both in the realm of <term>substance</term> <note>original: "substantia." In a philosophical context, this refers to the essential nature of a thing, its core reality.</note> and in the realm of those things that exist around or within substance (which is why shadows are understood through a twofold opposition)—you must remember this: the light that exists *around* a substance, acting as its final trace, proceeds from the light which is called the <term>first act</term> <note>original: "primus actus." A technical term in Scholastic philosophy referring to the primary reality or "being" of a thing. Bruno is tracing the lineage of light from the core of existence to its outer appearances.</note>. 

Likewise, the shadow which is around a substance [is derived] from the shadow which is said to come *from* the substance...

<summary>Bruno defines the "shadow" not as total darkness, but as a mixture or trace of light that allows the human mind to perceive truth indirectly. He begins to categorize different types of light and shadow based on their relationship to the "substance" or essential reality of things.</summary>
<keywords>shadow vs darkness, translucency of the soul, trace of light, substance, first act, Giordano Bruno, metaphysics of light</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>Continuing from the Third Intention, Bruno concludes his thought on the "primary subject" (matter) and begins a technical classification of different types of shadows based on their moral and intellectual orientation. He introduces the concept of the soul as a "horizon" between the spiritual and physical realms.</meta>

...to flow forth. It is the primary subject itself, which our <term>natural philosophers</term> <note>original: "phisici." In the 16th century, this term referred to scholars of "physics" or "natural philosophy," meaning the study of the physical world and its underlying principles.</note> call **matter**. Because all things that participate in it do not receive pure light, they are said to exist and operate under the **shadow of light**.

## THE FOURTH INTENTION. D.

Consequently, let it not escape your notice that since a shadow has something of light and something of darkness, there are two ways one may happen to be under a shadow. 

The first is the **shadow of darkness** and (as they say) the **shadow of death**. This occurs when the higher powers of the soul wither away and grow idle, or when they become servants to the lower powers—insofar as the mind is concerned only with physical life and the senses. 

The second is the **shadow of light**. This occurs when the lower powers are subjected to the higher powers, which aspire toward eternal and more eminent objects. This happens to one who dwells in the heavens, as it were, and who uses the spirit to trample down the provocations of the flesh. 

The former is the shadow leaning toward darkness; the latter is the shadow leaning toward light. Indeed, in the **Horizon** <note>original: "Orizonte." A central concept in Bruno’s thought. He views the human mind as a boundary or "horizon" between the material world and the divine intellect.</note> of light and darkness, we can understand nothing other than the shadow. This same shadow exists in the horizon of good and evil, and of truth and falsehood. It is the very thing that can be made good or made evil, be falsified, or be formed by truth. Whichever way it tends, it is said to be under the shadow of the one or the other.

## THE FIFTH INTENTION. E.

For our purpose, we specifically consider those shadows which are the objects of our desires and our <term>cognitive faculty</term> <note>The "cognitive faculty" (cognoscitiua facultas) refers to the mind’s ability to process and understand information.</note>. These are conceived under the appearance of the true and the good; they gradually descend from that <term>supersubstantial unity</term> <note>original: "vnitate illa supersubstantiali." A term from Neoplatonic philosophy referring to God or the ultimate source of reality, which exists "above" or "beyond" (super) all created substances.</note> through a growing...

<summary>Bruno distinguishes between two types of "shadows": one that leads the mind toward physical decay (shadow of darkness) and one that leads toward spiritual elevation (shadow of light). He describes the human experience as existing on a "horizon" where we must choose which direction to turn.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the Fourth and Fifth Intentions regarding the nature of shadows as they move away from the "One." It bridges the metaphysical descent of light into matter with the psychological experience of memory and contemplation.</meta>

...tude, they advance into an infinite multitude (to speak in the manner of the **Pythagoreans** <note>original: "Pythagoreorum." Followers of Pythagoras, who believed that the universe was ordered by numbers and that all things proceed from a single "Monad" into plurality.</note>): and the further these things recede from unity, the further they are distanced from truth itself. For there is an outflow from the <term>superessential</term> <note>original: "superessentiali." A term used to describe God or the Divine Source which exists "above" or "beyond" the category of being or essence.</note> toward the essences; from the essences to the things themselves; and from those to their **traces, images, likenesses, and shadows**. This movement flows both toward matter, so that they may be produced in its womb, and toward sense and reason, so that they may be discerned through their faculty.

### THE SIXTH INTENTION. F.

A **shadow** exists in matter or nature, in natural things themselves, and in both internal and external sense, in the form of **motion and change**. In the **intellect**, however, and in the **memory** that follows the intellect, it exists as a **state** <note>original: "statu." Here Bruno contrasts the physical world of "becoming" (motion) with the mental world of "being" (stability).</note>. 

Therefore, that wise man <note>Bruno likely refers to King Solomon, traditionally considered the author of the Song of Songs.</note> introduces an image of one who has attained a kind of supernatural and supersensual knowledge: sitting under the shadow of that first Truth and desirable Good. Because this "sitting" or state of rest does not last long for those living in a natural way (for soon and immediately those senses of ours leap upon us and disturb us, and our very leaders—the <term>phantasms</term> <note>original: "phantasmata." In Renaissance philosophy, these are the mental images or "pictures" the mind uses to think, bridging the gap between physical senses and abstract thought.</note>—surround and seduce us), that sitting is described by the past perfect or the imperfect tense rather than the present. For he says: *I sat*, or *I was sitting*, under his shadow. <note>original: "sub vmbra sedi, vel sedebam." A reference to Song of Solomon 2:3. Bruno suggests that human contemplation of divine truth is fleeting; we can only say we "have sat" there, as the distractions of the body quickly pull us away.</note>

### THE SEVENTH INTENTION. G.

Since there is an **order and connection** in all things, such that lower bodies follow the middle ones, and middle bodies follow the higher ones; so too do compound things follow simple ones, and simple things follow [even simpler ones...]

<summary>Bruno explains how the divine unity descends into a multitude of "shadows" and "images," and notes that while physical shadows are characterized by change, intellectual shadows (memories) provide a stable state for contemplation.</summary>
