--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument regarding the unity of the cosmos, transitioning from the descent of the soul into matter to the possibility of its ascent back to the divine "One." It builds on the distinction between the "state" of the intellect and the "motion" of the physical world.</meta>

...be united more closely. Material things adhere to spiritual ones, and spiritual things are rooted entirely within material ones. So that there might be one body of universal being, one order, one governance, one beginning, one end, one first, and one last. 

And since (as the leaders of the **Platonists** <note>original: "Platonicorum." Philosophers following the tradition of Plato, who taught that the physical world is a shadow of a higher, spiritual reality.</note> were well aware) there is a continuous migration from light to darkness—for certain minds, by turning toward matter and turning away from <term>actuality</term> <note>original: "actu." In philosophy, "act" refers to a thing being fully realized or "real," whereas "potency" refers to its potential to change or become something else.</note>, subject themselves to nature and fate—nothing prevents the lower realms from being gradually recalled to the heights by the sound of the universal lyre of **Apollo** <note>original: "Apollinis." The god of music and light; here, his lyre represents the mathematical and harmonic laws that hold the universe together.</note>. Thus, lower things may take on the nature of the higher through the middle realms. 

Just as it is evident to the senses that earth is thinned into water, water into air, and air into fire; likewise, fire is thickened into air, air into water, and water into earth. Thus, we generally see in those things that change, that motion is always bounded by a state of rest, and rest by motion. Certain of the **Peripatetics** <note>original: "Peripateticorum." Followers of Aristotle, who focused on the logic of the physical world and the movement of the heavens.</note> have best considered that this "being" and "becoming" exists even in the heavens themselves; for when they say that the heavens possess <term>actuality</term> mixed with <term>potency</term> <note>original: "potentia." The capacity for change or movement.</note> (though there are other reasons for this mixture), they understand that its motion is at its end regarding the past, and at its beginning regarding the future. 

Whatever the case may be regarding that other kind of descent upon which the wisdom of the **Theologians** <note>original: "Theologorum." Scholars of religious doctrine.</note> must decide, we must strive with all our might for the excellent operations of the mind. Keeping the **ladder of nature** <note>original: "schalam." Often called the *Scala Naturae*, this is the idea that all things in the universe are ranked in a hierarchy from "lowest" matter to "highest" spirit.</note> before our eyes, we should always strive to move from motion and multitude toward a state of rest and unity through internal operations. When we have achieved this to the best of our ability, we shall also, according to our capacity, be conformed to the divine works that are wonderful in their multitude. To this end, let the preordained connection of things and the sequence of these connected parts comfort and encourage us.

<summary>Bruno describes the universe as a single, interconnected body where the soul can ascend from the "multitude" of the material world back to "unity" with the Divine. He uses the metaphor of Apollo’s lyre to explain how cosmic harmony allows for the transformation of lower elements into higher spiritual states.</summary>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the unity of the cosmos, specifically focusing on how the human mind and higher intelligences ascend from the complexity of many individual things toward the simplicity of the "One."</meta>

- 25 -

Antiquity certainly knew and taught how human reasoning (<term>discourse</term> <note>original: "discursus." The step-by-step movement of the mind as it reasons from one idea to another.</note>) moves forward from many individuals to a species, and from many species to a single genus, ascending in thought. Furthermore, it taught how the lowest of the <term>intelligences</term> <note>original: "intelligentiarum." In this philosophy, these are spiritual beings or pure minds, higher than humans, who perceive truth directly rather than through step-by-step reasoning.</note> understands species distinctly through all forms. Lower intellects conceive all these species through many and various forms; higher ones through fewer; the supreme intellect through one; and that which is above everything else <note>Referring to the "One" or the Divine.</note> understands not through any form at all. Moreover, even if antiquity knew how memory improves itself—by moving from many memorable images to a single "species" or category of many memories—it certainly did not teach the method for doing so.

### EIGHTH INTENTION. H.

A lower thing is promoted toward the thing immediately above it by a likeness that is drawn closer through certain stages. When it has attained all these stages, it should no longer be called "similar" to the higher thing, but "the same" as it. We are clearly taught how this happens by fire itself: fire does not draw water toward its own nature unless the water has first been made like it in heat and <term>thinness</term> <note>original: "raritate." The state of being less dense; for example, when water turns into steam, it becomes "thinner" or "rarer" like the air or fire.</note>. Therefore, through a shared likeness, a path is opened from shadows to <term>vestiges</term> <note>original: "vestigia." Literally "footprints." In Platonic thought, these are the faint traces or "tracks" of the divine found in the physical world.</note>, from vestiges to mirror-images, and from these to higher things.

### NINTH INTENTION. I.

Since it is true that what is similar to one thing is also similar to the things that resemble it—whether by ascending, descending, or across the same level—it follows that (within its own limits) nature can make all things from all things, and

- 26 -

the <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "intellectus." The faculty of the mind that grasps universal truths and principles.</note>, or reason, knows all things from all things. Just as, I say, matter is shaped by all forms from all things, so too the **passive intellect** (as they call it) <note>original: "passiuus intellectus." In Aristotelian philosophy, this is the part of the mind that has the potential to become all things by receiving their "forms" or concepts.</note> can be shaped by all forms from all things; and memory by all memorable things from all things. This is because every like thing is made by its like, every like thing is known by its like, and every like thing is contained by its like. Furthermore, a distant "like" thing tends toward its remote target through a similar medium that is close to itself.

Hence, the <term>form</term> <note>original: "forma." In this context, the essential nature or "blueprint" of a thing, rather than its physical shape.</note> of a plant, once stripped of its matter, does not immediately take on the form of that animal [which ate it], but does so through the mediating forms of <term>chyle</term> <note>original: "Chili." A milky fluid produced in the intestines during digestion; an intermediate stage between food and blood.</note>, blood, and seed. Therefore, he who understands the appropriate "middle steps" between two extremes will be able to draw out all things from all things, both naturally and rationally.

### TENTH INTENTION. K.

Furthermore: Regarding that likeness which runs alongside equality and accords with uniformity (which they call <term>equivalence</term> <note>original: "æquiparantiam." A state where two things are exactly equal or identical in value or nature.</note>): consider it useless and poorly suited for the intended sense of operations, whether they refer to the internal or external senses. For it happens that a subject affected by a similar heat perceives neither that which is like it, nor anything standing within the degrees of that likeness; rather, it perceives that which excels what already exists in the <term>sensing subject</term> <note>original: "sensitiuo subiecto." The person or organ doing the perceiving.</note>. Therefore, foresee what kind of likeness you ought to put into practice, lest the things you seek be repelled by those already attained, preventing them from entering.

<summary>This page explains the "like to like" principle of cognition, suggesting that the mind and memory can encompass everything because they can become "like" everything. It also notes that transformation (like food becoming an animal) and perception require specific intermediate stages and a degree of difference rather than perfect equality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of "likeness" and "variety," moving from the mechanics of the mind to the aesthetic and metaphysical structure of the universe, arguing that beauty requires diversity rather than uniformity.</meta>

### ELEVENTH INTENTION. L.

Consider that this corporeal world could not have been beautiful if its parts were entirely identical to one another. It is, therefore, in the connection of diverse things that the beauty of the parts is revealed; and the beauty of the whole consists in that very variety. For this reason, the vision of a <term>shadowy</term> <note>original: "vmbratilis." In this philosophical context, it refers to things that are mere reflections or "shadows" of higher, divine realities.</note> thing is the most imperfect of all visions. This is because while an image displays itself through variety, a shadow—which exists within the boundaries of an external shape that is often deceptive—presents itself almost entirely without variety. I speak here of a shadow insofar as it is a shadow, and not as the specific type of shadow we are considering in our current project <note>The author likely refers to his broader work on the "Shadows of Ideas," where "shadows" represent the physical world as a bridge to the divine.</note>.

### TWELFTH INTENTION. M.

Truly, <term>Anaxagorean Chaos</term> <note>original: "Anaxagoricum Chaos." Referring to the pre-Socratic philosopher Anaxagoras, who taught that the universe began as a confused, undifferentiated mass until "Mind" (Nous) began to order it.</note> is variety without order. Just as we perceive a wonderful order within the variety of things themselves—an order that creates a connection between the highest things and the lowest, and the lowest with the highest—it causes all the parts to harmonize into the most beautiful face of one <term>great animal</term> <note>original: "vnius magni animalis." This refers to the *Anima Mundi* or World Soul, a concept where the entire universe is viewed as a single, living, breathing organism.</note> (which is what the world is). 

Since such great order requires such great diversity, so too does such great diversity require such great order. Indeed, no order can be found where no diversity exists. Because of this, one cannot understand the <term>First Principle</term> <note>original: "primum principium." God or the ultimate source of all existence.</note> as being "ordered" or existing "within an order," for it is beyond all division and variety.

### THIRTEENTH INTENTION. N.

Certainly, if an indissoluble harmony connects the ends of the first things with the beginnings of the second; and [the] hea... <note>Text breaks off mid-word "cal-" (likely "calor" or "heat").</note>

## INTENTION 14. O.

The ascent that occurs through things connected and chained together, in this proposal of <term>ideal shadows</term> <note>original: "vmbrarum idealium." ...his shadow. From which those things that produce understanding and memory in us are derived and emanate: and into which, finally, those climbing toward the light are terminated. Those called <term>Cabalists</term> <note>original: "Cabalistæ." Practitioners of Jewish mysticism who sought hidden meanings in scripture. This veil—which was present typically or figuratively on the face of Moses <note>A reference to Exodus 34:33, where Moses wears a veil because his face shines too brightly after speaking with God.</note>, and figuratively on the face of the Law—was not intended for deception; rather, it was for the orderly progression of human eyes, which suffer injury if they are suddenly moved from darkness into light.

For nature does not allow an immediate transition from one extreme to the other; instead, it happens through the mediation of shadows, and through shadowed light gradually. Some have lost their natural power of sight by coming out from darkness into sudden light, so far were they from attaining the object they sought. Therefore, the shadow prepares the vision for the light. The shadow tempers the light. Through the shadow, the divinity tempers and serves the announcing <term>species</term> <note>original: "species." In this context, it refers to the mental forms or images of things that enter our perception.</note> of things to the dimming eye of the hungry and thirsty soul. Therefore, recognize those shadows which do not extinguish, but rather preserve and guard the light within us, and through which we are advanced and led to understanding and memory.

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 30 regarding the "shadows" of ideas. Bruno is justifying his mnemonic system, which uses mental images (shadows) as a necessary step for the human mind to grasp higher truths that would otherwise be too bright or complex to perceive directly.</meta>

From those things which contain [forms] in potential, in the root, and in a certain enfolding, we must proceed to the unfolding of forms through a natural and rational course. Nature gives <term>species</term> <note>original: "species." In Bruno's philosophy, these are the formal appearances or "types" of things that the mind perceives.</note> enfolded before she delivers them unfolded. God does the same, and so do the arts which follow the divine and natural order according to their worth. If it seems arduous to some to practice among shadows, and they suspect it is a vain pursuit if access to the light does not lie open through them: let them know that such a defect does not come from the shadows themselves. Let them also know that it is quite useful to hold onto what is enfolded, if you cannot grasp it in its naked state.

### INTENTION XVII. R.

<note>Bruno uses "Intentions" as philosophical axioms or guidelines for his memory system. The letters "R" and "S" likely refer to his complex mnemonic wheels.</note>

Concerning physical shadows: there are those from trees and herbs that drive away serpents and nourish gentler creatures; there are also shadows contrary to these. Regarding "ideal shadows" <note>Shadows of the "Ideas" or divine archetypes. Since we cannot see the Ideas directly, we see their "shadows" in our minds.</note> (if they are truly ideal), since they all refer to the intellect and the purified inner sense, there are none that are not highly beneficial—provided that an ascent is made through them, and one does not fall asleep under them.

### INTENTION XVIII. S.

You will not sleep if you are moved from the inspection of physical shadows to the proportional consideration of ideal shadows. If a body far from our eyes approaches a distant light, its shadow appears smaller to our eyes. But if the body itself recedes further from the light, a smaller shadow is cast from it, and a greater obstacle is presented to the sight.

<summary>Bruno explains that the mind must progress from "enfolded" potential to "unfolded" understanding using shadows as intermediaries. He distinguishes between physical shadows and the "ideal shadows" of the intellect, which serve as tools for spiritual and mental ascent.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bruno's series of "Intentions"—philosophical axioms designed to help the reader understand the nature of "shadows" (mental images or reflections) as a bridge between the physical world and divine ideas. ### INTENTION XVIII. T.

With a greater intensity of light and density of the body, the shadow is made more distinct; more clearly defined, I say, and more shaped. This occurs because the shadow imitates the body in its <term>density and rarity</term> <note>original: "densitate & raritate." In Renaissance physics, density refers to how "packed" a material is, while rarity refers to its thinness or transparency.</note>, and in its continuity and discontinuity. Yet truly, such an imitation is only revealed through the body itself.

### INTENTION XX. V.

A shadow follows the movement of the body and the light simultaneously. Does the body move? The shadow moves. Does the light move? The shadow moves. Do both move? The shadow moves. Contrary to standard <term>physical observations</term> <note>original: "physicas obseruantias." Bruno is pointing out a paradox where a single thing (the shadow) appears to be doing two opposite things at once, which usually contradicts the laws of nature.</note>, the same subject—the subject of motion, I mean—is subjected to different and opposite movements at the same time. 

Why is this? Does not the shadow necessarily follow the body's movement in relation to the light? And does it not follow the light's movement in relation to the body? Is this necessity dissolved when both move together in opposite directions? Furthermore, notice how the shadow moves in response to the light's motion as if fleeing; yet in response to the body's motion, it is as if following. Thus, there is no apparent contradiction here, but rather a harmony between the flight of one and the pursuit of the other opposite and contrary thing. 

As for how it stands with these matters, and proportionally with others: investigate and consider it yourself; for we have revealed the matter more than enough for those who turn their minds to these and other things.

<note>Bruno often leaves the final "leap" of logic to the reader, encouraging them to apply these physical observations about shadows to the way the human mind (the shadow) follows both the material world (the body) and the divine intellect (the light).</note>

Intention, shadow, light, body, motion, density, rarity, imitation, physics, movement

## INTENTION XXI. X.

Finally, do not let the similarity between shadows and <term>ideas</term> <note>original: "ideis." In Bruno's philosophy, Ideas are the perfect, divine archetypes of all things in the mind of God.</note> escape your notice. For both shadows and ideas are not the opposites of opposite things. In this category, the beautiful and the ugly, the fitting and the unfitting, the perfect and the imperfect, and good and evil are all known through one single <term>form</term> <note>original: "speciem." In logic, this refers to the mental "kind" or "type" used to categorize something.</note>. For evil, the imperfect, and the ugly do not have their own "ideas" by which they may be known. However, because they are said to be known and not ignored—and since whatever is known intellectually is known through ideas—they are known through the form of another, not through their own form (for they have none). Indeed, that which is characteristic of them is "non-being" within a being, or (to speak more clearly) a defect within an effect.

## INTENTION XXII. Y.

If you call a shadow an <term>accident</term> <note>original: "accidens." In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a quality of a thing that is not essential to its nature, like its color or position.</note> of the body from which it is cast, you then have an accident of a single subject from which it may depart and to which it may return, either according to the same kind or the same individual number. However, if you wish for the shadow to be an accident of the *surface* onto which it is cast, you then make an accident so separable from one subject that the same individual thing wanders through different subjects. For example, when by the movement of the light or of a horse, the "horse-shadow" that was being projected onto a stone is now projected onto wood. This is against the physical logic of an accident <note>Traditional philosophy held that an "accident" (like a shadow's shape) cannot move from one object to another; if the shadow moves from a stone to a tree, it was thought to be a new accident, not the same one "traveling."</note>—unless you transport yourself into <note>Scylla: A reference to the mythical sea monster. Bruno suggests that denying a shadow is an "accident" leads to a different set of logical dangers or monsters.</note> by denying that a shadow is an accident at all. 

Furthermore, what do we say regarding "ideal shadows" <note>These are the mental images or reflections of divine truths in the human mind.</note>? You should understand that they are neither substances nor accidents, but rather certain [reflections] of substances and acciden—

...notions of things. If anyone is pleased to call these <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "accidentia." In traditional logic, an accident is a quality that can change without changing the essence of the subject (like the color of a shirt). Bruno argues mental images are more than just "accidental" leftovers.</note> of the mind and of reason, he speaks unskillfully: for they are not <term>habits</term> <note>original: "habitus." For to those who contemplate correctly, <term>substance</term> and accident do not divide everything that is said to exist throughout the entire universe, as we are now assuming. This consideration is of no small value for understanding the nature of shadows.

## INTENTION XXIII. Z.

A shadow is not subject to time, but to the time of that [the body or light]; not to place, but to the place of that; not to motion, but to the motion of that. The same should be understood regarding opposites. It is, therefore, abstracted from all truth, yet it is not without it. And it does not make those who conceive of it inept (if it is an <term>ideal shadow</term> <note>An "ideal" shadow is a mental image that perfectly reflects the higher Divine Idea.</note>), for it allows one to conceive of contraries and diverse things, even though it is one. For nothing is contrary to a shadow—neither darkness nor light, precisely speaking. Therefore, man fled to the shadow of the **tree of knowledge** for the cognition of darkness and light, of truth and falsehood, of good and evil, when God asked him: **ADAM, WHERE ARE YOU?** <note>A reference to Genesis 3:9. Bruno interprets Adam's "hiding" among the trees as humanity's transition from direct divine light to the "shadows" of intellectual knowledge.</note>

## INTENTION XXIV. ψ.

One must also not neglect to consider that a single opaque body, when placed opposite two or more sources of light, casts two or more shadows. ### INTENTION XXV. A.

Do not let it escape your notice that just as a shadow flees from light, it mimics the <term>quantity</term> <note>original: "quantitatem." In this context, Bruno refers to the physical dimensions—the size and shape—of an object.</note> of the body. Only at a certain and specific distance, position, and arrangement—when its length and width are equal to the body—is the shadow produced by the opposing light. Indeed, it seems to flee the light itself no more than it suggests the dimensions of the body through that very shadow. In certain regions, the sun never casts a shadow equal to the body; in others, it does so only rarely and for a very short time.

### INTENTION XXVI. E.

If the size of the <term>opaque body</term> <note>An object that does not allow light to pass through it.</note> exceeds the size of the light source, it produces the vertex of the shadow’s cone on the body itself, while projecting the base of the shadow into an infinite or unbounded distance. <note>In geometry, Bruno is describing how a small candle casting a shadow of a large wall creates a shadow that grows larger and larger the further away it gets.</note>

If, however, the size of the light exceeds the size of the opaque body, it produces the base of the shadow on the body itself, but it will fix the cone’s point at a specific distance in its projection beyond the body. This distance is determined by the proportional ratio of the light body’s size relative to the opaque body’s size. <note>This describes a larger light source (like the sun) shining on a smaller object (like the Earth); the shadow tapers down to a point (a cone) rather than expanding forever.</note> From this, the shadow which the luminous body of the moon would produce from the earth on the opposite side (assuming the sun were absent from the lower <term>hemisphere</term>) would have as its cone a certain portion of the earth's...

...edge. Its base, however, extending beyond the earth and growing as if into infinity, would not be determinable. But the shadow which the body of the sun produces from the earth has the certain boundaries of the earth as its base; however, the point of its cone does not reach the <term>sphere of Mercury</term>. <note>In the geocentric model Bruno used, Mercury’s orbital sphere was above the Earth; he is noting that the Earth's shadow is not long enough to reach that far into space.</note> Now, make a similar judgment regarding ideas and their shadows.

### INTENTION XXVII.

From this, note how shadow is born from light and darkness (for I call the density of a body "darkness"). Light is its father, and darkness its mother. It is not present unless both the mother and the father are there. Thus, the shadow follows the light in such a way that it simultaneously flees from it—as if it were ashamed to present the mother’s appearance to the father, so that through this very shame, at least, it may testify to its royal lineage. Just like those of noble birth who, being unable to show their nobility through their outward clothing, demonstrate it well enough through the very shame they feel for their own attire. Hence, as the light increases, the shadow is thinned; when the light contracts, the shadow expands; and when the light completely surrounds the body, the shadow flees.

### INTENTION XXVIII.

Just as we derive the <term>meridian line</term> <note>A north-south line used to determine noon and astronomical positions.</note> from the imaginable shadow of a <term>gnomon</term> <note>original: "gnomone." The part of a sundial that casts a shadow.</note> placed perpendicularly upon a plane between the <term>Bear</term> <note>original: "Arcton." Referring to the constellations Ursa Major or Minor, signifying the North.</note> and the eye; and just as we infallibly derive many other differences of time which are revealed in the nightly circuit of the polar stars—revealed by the differences in the parts of the small circle which the line, stretched across its circumference, makes manifest through numbers—so it is no less true that the shadows of ideas, through phy... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "physical" or "physiognomy," to be continued on the next page.</note>

## INTENTION XXIX.

And just as the sun produces six cardinal differences of <term>shadows</term>. One from the rising sun, when it projects the <term>shadow</term> of a body to the west; another from the setting sun, when it extends it to the east. Another from the meridian and in the Southern latitude toward the North. Another in the Northern latitude toward the South. Another if it admits no latitude <note>Here, "latitude" refers to the angular distance from the celestial equator; "no latitude" implies being directly under the sun's path.</note>. Directing perpendicular rays from the belt of heaven (as they say), it produces the <term>shadow</term> of the earth toward its <term>nadir</term> <note>The point directly opposite the zenith, or the point directly below the observer.</note>. From the opposite side of the other hemisphere, however, it will pour out a shadow toward the <term>apogee</term> <note>original: "augem" - the point in an orbit farthest from the center; here used to describe the peak or highest extension of the shadow's path</note> that is to be thinned out by its very progression. So for us, situated on the horizon of nature and within the balanced and upright sphere of the same, under the <term>equinoctial</term> <note>Equinoctial: the celestial equator; a state of balance where light and dark are equal.</note> of the senses, or the <term>intellect</term> which is equal to the day: six differences of <term>shadows</term> are formed under eternal ideas, from which we may receive a complete conversion toward the light.

## INTENTION XXX. A.

Just as you understand that all differences of <term>shadows</term> are ultimately referred to the six cardinal ones, you should no less know that all must eventually be reduced to one most fertile shadow, the most general source of the others. In our proposition, I say, there can be one <term>shadow</term> of all ideas, which—through addition, subtraction, and alteration broadly speaking—fuses, judges, and presents all the others. Just as in art, materially, through substan-

...substantive subject, but formally through an adjective, which receive within themselves things that are altering, transposing, and universally diversifying. For metaphysics, logic, and physics admit a certain analogy: or rather, that which is <term>pre-natural</term><note>Metaphysics, dealing with things beyond the physical world.</note>, the <term>rational</term><note>Logic, dealing with the patterns of thought.</note>, and the <term>natural</term><note>Physics, dealing with the material world.</note>. These correspond respectively to the True, the Image, and the <term>Shadow</term><note>In this philosophical system, a "shadow" is not just an absence of light, but the way a high, divine truth is reflected or "dimmed" so it can be understood by the human mind.</note>.

Furthermore, the <term>Idea</term> in the divine mind exists as a single, simultaneous whole in a single act. In the <term>Intelligences</term><note>In Renaissance philosophy, "Intelligences" refers to celestial or angelic minds that exist between God and humanity.</note>, ideas exist through distinct and separate acts. In the heavens, they exist in a manifold and successive active potential. In nature, they exist by way of a <term>trace</term><note>original: "vestigii" - often used to describe the "footprints" or hints of God found in the natural world.</note>, as if by an impression. In intention and reason, they exist by way of a shadow.

Here is a <term>paradigm</term><note>A pattern or model.</note> of the One: of an idea having infinitely many differences of things in actuality, and of a single shadow having the capacity for infinite differences. Line AB, lying flat, receives line CD falling perpendicularly and forming two right angles. Now, if the falling line is inclined toward B, it will yield an <term>acute angle</term> on one side and an <term>obtuse angle</term> on the other. Inclined more and more toward F, G, H, I, K, and so on, it will produce more obtuse and acute angles on either side.

Thus it is clear how, within the capacity of those two straight lines, there are infinite varieties of acute and obtuse—

<meta>This page continues the geometric analogy from Intention XXX. ...lar and the differences of the angles. In the <term>First Cause</term><note>In this philosophical context, the First Cause refers to God or the ultimate source of all existence.</note>, this <term>capacity</term><note>original: "facultas" - the power or potential to do or be something.</note> does not differ from the **act**; in this Cause, whatever is able to be, already is. This is because "being" and "possibility" are made identical within it. Therefore, in the point D itself, the infinite differences of the angles are simultaneously one and the same.

In the <term>celestial mover</term><note>The spiritual intelligence or force responsible for the motion of the stars and planets.</note>, this exists as an **active potential**: much like a hand that has the power to move toward points E, F, G, and countless others, even if it has not yet moved.

As is evident, it participates in both act and potential.

What we have said regarding the differences between angles should also be applied to the **differences between species**, which are said to be like **numbers**<note>A common Neoplatonic idea: just as adding "1" to a number creates a completely new number, changing an essential trait of a species creates a completely new species.</note>. From this, it is manifest that anything has the potential to be shaped within all things and through all things.

<meta>This page transitions from the geometric and physical metaphors of the previous section into the core philosophical arguments of the "Thirty Concepts of Ideas." # ON THE CONCEPTS OF THE THIRTY
## IDEAS.

---

Let us now proceed to the concepts of the thirty Ideas—first, considering them simply; and second, considering them in a complex manner along with the "intentions of the shadows" <note>Bruno uses "shadows" to describe how divine ideas are reflected or obscured in the physical world and the human mind.</note>.

### FIRST CONCEPT. A

God (says Plotinus) <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was a major Greek-speaking philosopher of the ancient world and the founder of Neoplatonism. His work deeply influenced Renaissance thinkers like Bruno.</note> fashioned **light-bearing** <note>original: "Luciferos" — here used in its literal Latin sense of "bringing light," referring to the eyes as windows or lamps for the soul.</note> eyes in the face and provided instruments for the other senses, so that through them they might be naturally preserved and might also draw something in from a **kindred light**. By these words, he clearly shows that there is something exceptional belonging to the senses which originates from the <term>intelligible world</term> <note>The realm of pure ideas, forms, and truths that can only be grasped by the intellect, as opposed to the "sensible world" perceived by the body.</note>.

### CONCEPT II. B

It is not right to think that this world has multiple principles, and consequently, it is not right to think it has multiple orders beyond the one. Accordingly, if the order is one, then its members are connected and subordinate to one another. Thus, the higher things exist according to a truer state of **being**, while they stretch out toward <term>matter</term> <note>In this philosophical tradition, matter is seen as the furthest point from the divine source, where the unity of an idea is broken into many physical parts.</note> into extended mass and multiple numbers. Hence, it descends from that which is, in itself, the greatest <term>being</term>; down to that which possesses the least amount of <term>entity</term> <note>original: "entitas" — the quality of existing or having a distinct identity.</note> and is not rashly called "almost nothing."

<summary>This page introduces the "Thirty Concepts of Ideas," using Plotinus's theories to explain the link between human perception and the divine world, while establishing a hierarchy of existence from the supreme "Being" down to nearly non-existent "Matter."</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, light-bearing eyes, intelligible world, hierarchy of being, matter, unity, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition into the "Thirty Concepts of Ideas." The previous page ended with a discussion of the unified order of the universe and how higher things possess a truer state of being. This page concludes that thought by explaining how the human mind can "contract" or internalize the order of the macrocosm to act with divine ease, before moving into Concepts III and IV regarding the nature of spontaneous action and the soul's relationship to the body.</meta>

...let access be made. Whoever conceives this order with its steps in the mind will acquire a likeness of the <term>macrocosm</term> <note>The "great world" or the universe as a whole, which Renaissance Neoplatonists believed was mirrored in the "microcosm" of the human being.</note> different from the one he possesses within himself by nature. From this, acting as if by nature itself, he will perform all things without difficulty.

### CONCEPT III. <sig>C</sig>

Because in those things which always occur, there is no deliberation or reasoning. If something is demonstrated to always do the same thing, the act of reasoning is removed from it, and so is deliberation <note>Bruno is arguing that perfect, divine action is so certain and natural that it doesn't require "thinking it over" or "weighing options" like humans do.</note>. Instead, it completes its works as if by a certain form expressing itself outwardly in a natural way, or unfolding and pouring out something from its own nature. That which operates in the same way most often and most frequently comes closest to the likeness of this "worker" <note>Referring to the divine or natural creative force.</note>. For it will happen that by thinking and deciding less—or even not at all—it proceeds into a perfect and exquisite act. Therefore, he who, while existing in place and time, shall release the <term>principles of the ideas</term> <note>original: "rationes idearum" — the underlying logic or essential patterns of the divine Ideas.</note> from the constraints of place and time, will be conformed to divine beings in his works, whether they pertain to the intellect or to the will. This was perhaps the aim of the one who said:

-> **THOUGH WE EXIST IN THE FLESH, WE DO NOT LIVE ACCORDING TO THE FLESH.** <-
<note>original: "IN CARNE CONSISTENTES NON SECVNDVM CARNEM VIVIMVS." A variation of 2 Corinthians 10:3. Bruno uses this to illustrate the soul's ability to operate on a higher plane than the physical body.</note>

### CONCEPT IV. <sig>D</sig>

For if it is possible and true, one may understand the intellectual soul not as truly implanted, fixed, and existing within the body, but truly as assisting and governing it; so that it can display a perfect form separate from the body. To which opinion (if...

<summary>This page argues that the human mind can achieve divine-like efficiency by internalizing the macrocosmic order and moving beyond labored reasoning toward spontaneous, "natural" action. It also introduces the Neoplatonic view of the soul as a governor presiding over the body rather than a prisoner trapped within it.</summary>
<keywords>macrocosm, divine action, spontaneity, deliberation, intellectual soul, body-soul dualism, Plotinus, reasoning</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Thirty Concepts of Ideas." The previous page (Concept IV) discussed how a person can act with divine ease by internalizing the cosmic order. This page concludes Concept IV by identifying this elevated state with the "interior man" and then explores the soul’s substance (Concept V) and the various levels of existence for "Forms" (Concept VI).</meta>

(without controversy) That Theologian <note>Likely a reference to St. Paul, who uses the term "inner man" in the New Testament (e.g., Romans 7:22), or potentially a Neoplatonic figure like Origen.</note> agrees most strongly: who, entitling it with the name of perfection, called it the **interior man**. If you seek, for the sake of confirming this, the operations possible for the soul without the body: behold, it is joined to Ideas not bound by a certain place or time, whenever a human being—freed in mind or spirit—leaves behind matter and time.

### CONCEPT V. E

The soul has a substance that relates to the higher intelligences just as a diaphanous <note>original: "diaphani" — transparent or translucent, like glass or clear air.</note> body relates to lights (as even the leading <term>Platonists</term> <note>The followers of Plato, particularly the Neoplatonists, who viewed the soul as a bridge between the material and the divine.</note> understood). Because of its diaphaneity and transparency, it admits something like an innate luminosity, which is always in action when the soul is stripped from the body, as if inhabiting a region of light. But while dwelling in the body, it is like a crystal whose transparency is limited by opacity: it possesses wandering sensible appearances <note>original: "species sensibiles" — the mental images or forms of things perceived through the five senses.</note> which, through turning toward or away from things according to differences of time and place, approach and recede.

### CONCEPT VI. F

The forms of things are in the ideas; they are, in a certain way, in themselves; they are in the heavens; they are in the <term>cycle of the heavens</term> <note>original: "periodo caeli" — the orbital movements and seasonal cycles of the stars and planets.</note>; they are in the proximate <term>seminal causes</term> <note>original: "causis seminalibus" — the hidden "seeds" or formative principles in nature that allow things to grow and develop according to their kind.</note>; they are in the proximate <term>efficient causes</term> <note>original: "causis efficientibus" — the immediate agents or forces that produce an effect.</note>; they are individually in the effect; they are in light; they are in the external sense; they are in the internal sense: each in its own way.

<summary>This page defines the "inner man" as the soul acting independently of time and matter, compares the soul's substance to a crystal that receives divine light, and lists the various levels where the "Forms" of things exist in the universe.</summary>
<keywords>interior man, soul, Platonists, diaphaneity, sensible species, celestial cycles, seminal causes, efficient causes, internal sense</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Concepts of Ideas." The previous page concluded with Concept VI, which discussed how forms exist within the divine Ideas. This page moves into Concept VII, addressing why material reality is illusory compared to the permanence of Ideas, and Concept VIII, which uses the hierarchy of Plotinus to explain how a human can ascend from physical existence back to the original "Idea" of Man.</meta>

### CONCEPT VII. G

Matter is not filled by the reception of forms (as it protests through its eternal longing for new ones) because it neither accepts true forms, nor does it truly receive what it seems to receive. For those things which truly *exist* are not the **sensible** <note>Things perceived by the five senses rather than the intellect.</note> and individual objects themselves—as is claimed by the one who calls these things first, principal, and most great <term>substances</term> <note>original: "substantias" — here referring to Aristotle’s view that individual material objects are the most "real" things, a view the author is challenging from a Platonist perspective.</note>. For those things that truly exist remain forever; however, those things subject to **generation and corruption** <note>original: "generationi... atque corruptioni" — the natural cycle of being born, changing, and eventually decaying.</note> are not truly said to "be." 

This view is not only pleasing to those who philosophize more correctly, but we even hear some of the **Theologians** calling the "outer man" a **vanity** <note>original: "vanitatem" — meaning something empty, fleeting, or without substance. This likely refers to St. Paul’s descriptions of the physical self.</note> under this natural condition. Others indeed wish for all things that happen "under the sun"—that is, things that inhabit the universal region of matter—to fall under the mark of vanity <note>A reference to the Biblical Book of Ecclesiastes, which describes worldly life as "vanity of vanities."</note>. Therefore, let the soul seek the "fixing" of its concepts from the **Ideas**, from the **Ideas**—if you understand.

### CONCEPT VIII. H

**Plotinus** <note>A 3rd-century philosopher and the father of Neoplatonism, who taught that all existence flows from a single source.</note> described the first man as the **Idea**, the second as the **Soul**, and the third as almost "no longer man," in the passage where he discourses on the reason for the multitude of ideas. The second depends on the first, and the third on the second, while it is ordered toward a physical existence through arrangement, contraction, and composition. For a **metaphysical** concept, therefore, let the third ascend into the second, and the second into the first. <note>This describes a process of mental "ascent," moving from the physical body (third) to the individual soul (second) and finally back to the divine archetype or "Idea" of humanity (first).</note>

<summary>This page argues that material objects are not truly "real" because they are subject to decay, whereas divine Ideas are eternal. It presents a Neoplatonic hierarchy from Plotinus, suggesting that a person must mentally ascend from their physical state back to the original "Idea" of Man to reach true understanding.</summary>
<keywords>Platonism, Plotinus, substance, vanity, material world, divine ideas, metaphysics, generation and corruption, the soul, archetypes</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of "Concepts" regarding the nature of Ideas and the Intellect. The previous page (Concepts VII and VIII) established that material things are fleeting "vanities," whereas the true "Forms" or "Ideas" are permanent and real. Here, the author explores the characteristics of these eternal Ideas—specifically their identity and their source in a "First Intellect" that acts like a vast ocean of light.</meta>

### CONCEPT IX. I

The **Same**, the **Enduring**, and the **Eternal** coincide as one. For that which is the **Same** <note>original: "Idem" — in Platonic philosophy, this refers to the unchanging, self-identical nature of divine reality, contrasted with the "Other" or the changing world of matter.</note>, because it is the same, endures and is eternal. The **Eternal**, because it is eternal, endures and is the same. The **Enduring**, because it endures, is both the same and eternal. 

Therefore, you must strive toward the "Same" itself, or toward that which possesses the quality of identity, so that you may hold onto things permanently and with perseverance. If you grasp this, you will possess the "head" or the principle by which you can fix the **species** <term>species</term> <note>original: "specierum" — here referring to the mental images or essential forms of things that the soul perceives.</note> firmly within the soul.

### CONCEPT X. K

This thought is worthy enough for the sharp edge of the mind to be fixed upon it: The **First Intellect** <note>The highest level of divine mind, often associated with God or the first emanation in Neoplatonism.</note> is an **Amphitrite of light** <note>original: "lucis amphitrites" — Amphitrite was a Greek sea goddess. This is a poetic way of describing the Intellect as a "vast ocean" or "reservoir" of divine light.</note>. It pours out its light from its innermost depths to the external world and draws it back from the furthest edges. 

It does this so that anyone, according to their own capacity, can "contract" or draw all things from it, and so that anything, according to its ability, can strive toward it through the path of that very light. This is perhaps what a certain author understood when saying: **"IT REACHES FROM ONE END TO THE OTHER."** <note>A quote from the biblical Book of Wisdom 8:1, describing Divine Wisdom reaching across the universe.</note> And another who said: **"THERE IS NO ONE WHO HIDES THEMSELVES FROM ITS HEAT."** <note>A quote from Psalm 19:6, referring to the sun/divine presence from which nothing is hidden.</note> 

By "light" here, I mean the **intelligibility** <term>intelligibility</term> <note>The quality of being understandable or "knowable" by the mind; the author suggests that things only make sense because they participate in this divine light.</note> of the things that come from it and strive toward it, as well as everything that accompanies that understanding. As these things flow forth from one another—diverse things from diverse sources—they are multiplied into such an innumerable count that no one can determine their number, except for Him who **numbers the multitude of the stars**. But when they flow back <note>Referring to the "return" of all creation back to its divine source.</note>, they are united into that very **Unity** which is the fountain of all unities.

### CONCEPT XI. L

The First Intellect, by its own **fecundity** <note>Fruitfulness or creative power.</note> and in its own specific way, propagates **Ideas**—which are not "new," nor are they produced "newly" <note>This suggests that while we might discover an Idea for the first time, the Idea itself has existed eternally in the mind of the First Intellect.</note>. Nature, however, propagates new things...

<summary>This page defines the "First Intellect" as an eternal, oceanic source of light (intelligibility) that connects all things. It explains that true reality is found in the "Same"—the unchanging, eternal Ideas—rather than the fleeting, "new" things of the material world.</summary>

<keywords>First Intellect, Ideas, Intelligibility, Eternity, Sameness, Divine Light, Neoplatonism, Unity, Fecundity</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the Intellect interacts with universal "species" or Forms. While the previous page described the First Intellect as an ocean of light, this page focuses on how the human Mind (Ratio) processes these forms and how they exist in a unified state in the celestial realm compared to their divided state on Earth.</meta>

...produces them in number, yet not in a newly fashioned way (after its own manner) if it always operates in the same way. **Reason** <term>Reason</term> <note>original: "Ratio" — here referring to the human faculty of logical or discursive thought.</note> forms new **species** <term>species</term> <note>original: "species" — the mental images, categories, or essential forms of things.</note> and forms them in new ways unto infinity: composing, dividing, abstracting, contracting, adding, subtracting, ordering, and disordering.

### CONCEPT XII. M

The forms of deformed animals are beautiful in heaven. The forms of metals, which do not shine in themselves, shine in their own planets. For neither man, nor animals, nor metals exist there as they exist here. For that which moves discursively <note>original: "discurrit" — referring to the way things in the material world are subject to change, motion, and the passage of time.</note> here, flourishes there in actuality, by a superior discourse. For the **virtues** <term>virtues</term> <note>original: "virtutes" — the inherent powers or essential qualities of a thing.</note> which are unfolded toward matter are united and **enfolded** <note>original: "complicantur" — a philosophical term suggesting that many diverse things are contained as a single unity within their source.</note> toward the first act.

From this it is clear what the **Platonists** <note>original: "Platonici" — the followers of Plato's philosophy who believed that physical objects are mere shadows of perfect, eternal "Ideas."</note> say: that any idea of things, even of non-living things, is a certain life and intelligence. Likewise, that in the **first mind** <note>The supreme divine intellect or the mind of God.</note> there is a single idea of all things. Therefore, by illuminating, quickening <note>original: "viuificando" — giving life to or making alive.</note>, and uniting, you—by conforming yourself to these superior agents—may be carried away into the conception and retention of species.

### CONCEPT XIII. N

The first light, life, intelligence, and unity contain all species, perfections, truths, numbers, and the degrees of things. While those things which in nature are different, contrary, and diverse: in that unity they are the same, harmonious, and one. 

Try, therefore, if you can by your own powers identify, harmonize, and unite the species you have received; then you will not weary your **genius** <term>genius</term> <note>original: "ingenium" — one's natural mental ability or innate talent.</note>, you will not disturb your mind, and you will not confuse your memory.

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic idea that all earthly things exist in a state of perfect, unified intelligence within the "First Mind." It encourages the student to harmonize their mental images (species) to achieve better memory and intellectual clarity.</summary>
<keywords>Reason, Species, Platonists, Ideas, Universal Unity, First Mind, Memory, Metals, Celestial Forms</keywords>

### CONCEPT XIV. O

When you have reached the **reason** <term>reason</term> <note>original: "rationem" — the method or logical path of thinking.</note> by which you will be conformed to the **physical heaven** <note>original: "cœlo corpori" — the visible, material cosmos or the starry sky.</note>, which contains the forms of lower animals—even the lowly ones—in a manner that is not lowly, do not plant your foot there. Instead, you must strive for conformity with the **intellectual heaven** <note>original: "intellectualis cœli" — a higher realm of pure thought or divine ideas that exists beyond the physical stars.</note>, which possesses the forms of the entire world in a more excellent way than the physical heaven does.

### CONCEPT XV. P

You will find and experience that you are truly making such progress when you achieve an approach, through your own efforts, from **confused plurality** toward a **distinct unity**. For this is not merely the "cobbling together" of **logical universals** <term>logical universals</term> <note>original: "vniuersalia logica" — general categories used in traditional logic (like "animal" or "substance") which the author suggests can be vague or "confused" compared to real, organic understanding.</note>, which seize upon confused middle-categories from distinct lowest species, and even more confused highest-categories from those. Rather, it is like fitting a formed "whole" and "one" to oneself out of many unformed parts. Just as a hand joined to an arm, a foot to a leg, and an eye to a forehead possess a greater **knowability** when they are composed together than when they are placed apart, so it is with the parts and species of the universe. Nothing is placed apart or exempt from the **order** (which is most simple, most perfect, and beyond number in the **first mind** <term>first mind</term> <note>original: "prima mente" — the divine or universal intellect that precedes all created things.</note>). If we conceive of things by connecting them to one another and uniting them according to reason, what is there that we cannot understand, remember, and do?

### CONCEPT XVI. Q

It is **One** that defines all things. There is **one** splendor of beauty in all things. **One** flash of light shines out from the multitude of species. If you should cast this together: such among the eyes...

### CONCEPT XVII. R

Error and forgetfulness happen to us because, in our realm, the composition of things thrives upon form and the **weak** <note>original: "infirmi" — likely referring to unstable, decaying matter which, in Platonic thought, is the "weak" element that limits the perfection of a form.</note>. Indeed, the formation of the corporeal world is an inferior form, for it is composed from the mere trace and the **deformity** <note>original: "deformitate" — here meaning the lack of perfect form or the distortion that occurs when a perfect idea is manifested in physical matter.</note> of the higher form. Therefore, ascend to that place where the **forms** <term>forms</term> <note>original: "species" — the eternal, perfect patterns or "ideas" of things that exist in the divine mind, rather than physical appearances.</note> are pure, where nothing is unformed, and every formed thing is the form itself.

### CONCEPT XVIII. S

Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the founding philosopher of Neoplatonism. His works, collected in the *Enneads*, focus on the soul's journey back to the "One."</note>, the prince of the Platonists, noted: As long as someone is occupied with looking at a figure that is only manifest to the eyes, they are not yet seized by love. But as soon as the mind, calling itself back from that figure, conceives a figure within itself that is **undivided** <note>original: "non diuiduam" — an internal mental image that is unified and whole, unlike physical objects which are composed of separate parts.</note> and beyond the visible, love immediately arises. There is a similar judgment regarding **intelligible objects** <term>intelligible objects</term> <note>original: "obiectis intelligibilibus" — concepts or truths that can only be grasped by the intellect, not perceived by the physical senses.</note> as there is regarding desirable things. From here, therefore, investigate and contemplate how these forms may be conceived more quickly, more vividly, and more tenaciously.

### CONCEPT XIX. T

Plotinus understood that the **Ladder** <term>Ladder</term> <note>original: "Schalam" — a common mystical metaphor (the *Scala Naturae*) for the specific stages of discipline the soul must undergo to return to the Divine Source.</note> by which one ascends to the **Principle** <note>original: "principium" — the First Cause or the Divine.</note> consists of seven steps (to which we add two). Of these:
The **first** is the purification of the soul.
The **second** is attention.
The **third** is intention.
The **fourth** is the contemplation of order.
The **fifth** is proportional comparison derived from that order.
The **sixth** is **negation, or separation** <note>original: "negatio, seu separatio" — the process of "unsaying" or stripping away physical characteristics to understand a spiritual truth in its pure state.</note>.
The **seventh** is the **vow** <note>original: "votum" — can also mean a sacred desire, prayer, or focused will.</note>.
The **eighth** is trans- <note>The text breaks off here mid-word; likely "transitus" (passing over) or "transformatio" (transformation).</note>

...the formation of oneself into the thing. Ninth, the transformation of the thing into oneself. ### CONCEPT XX. V

Everything that exists, after the **One** <term>the One</term> <note>original: "vnum" — the supreme, simple principle of reality in Neoplatonism, from which all things emanate.</note>, is necessarily manifold and numerous. Therefore, apart from the One and the First, all things are number. Hence, below the lowest step of the **ladder of nature** <term>ladder of nature</term> <note>original: "schalæ naturæ" — the hierarchical order of reality ranging from base matter up to the divine spirit.</note> is infinite number, or matter; at the highest point, however, is infinite unity and **pure act** <term>pure act</term> <note>original: "actus purus" — a state of complete reality or perfection without any potential for change, usually identifying the divine.</note>. Thus, the descent, dispersal, and wandering happens toward matter. The ascent, gathering together, and determination happens toward act.

### CONCEPT XXI. X

Through numbers (as some say), beings relate to that which truly is—or the **true being** <note>original: "verum ens"</note>—just as matter, through the **commencement of forms** <note>original: "inchoationem formarum" — the initial stage or potential for a form to emerge in physical matter</note>, relates to the forms themselves.

### CONCEPT XXII. Y

Consider a triple form <note>While the text says "triple," it goes on to list four distinct types; this may reflect a traditional philosophical categorization where the fourth type—the "Idea"—is treated as the primary category.</note>. The first is that from which it happens that the thing itself is formed, such as that which produces the act; and this we do not properly call an **Idea** or the form of things to be produced. The second is that by which the thing itself is formed as a part; and it is not appropriate for this to be called a likeness of that of which it is a part. The third is that which defines and shapes something as an **inherent quality** <note>original: "inhærens qualitas" — a property that resides within the physical object rather than existing as an external archetype.</note>; and this kind cannot receive the definition of an Idea, since it is not separated from that of which it is the form. The fourth is that toward which something is formed, and which something imitates; and this, in the usage of speakers, is accustomed to retain the name of **Idea**. And this...

<meta>This page continues the classification of "Forms" and "Ideas" from the previous page. While the previous page began discussing a "triple form," this page opens by clarifying that the subject is spoken of in four distinct ways across different fields of study (technical, logical, physical, and metaphysical). It then moves to Concept XXIII, which argues against the materialist views of ancient philosophers like Democritus.</meta>

...is spoken of in four ways. In **artificial things** <note>original: "artificialibus" — things created by human craft or art.</note> themselves, existing before the crafted objects. In **first intentions** <term>first intentions</term> <note>original: "intentionibus primis" — in Scholastic logic, these are primary concepts that refer directly to things in the world, whereas "second intentions" are concepts about those concepts.</note>, existing before the second. In the principles of nature, existing before natural things. Finally, in the **divine mind** <note>original: "diuina mente"</note>, existing before nature and the entire universe. In the first instance, this is called **technical** <note>original: "technica" — relating to art or craft.</note>; in the second, **logical**; in the third, **physical**; and in the fourth, **metaphysical**.

### CONCEPT XXII. 2

Certain forms imitate as if by nature: just as an image in a mirror imitates the form of the object placed before it. Certain forms [imitate] by convention: like a figure impressed by a seal. Again, some imitate **by their own nature** <term>per se</term> <note>original: "per se" — essentially or through their own inherent logic.</note>: just as a painting, through the intention of the painter, represents a specific person. Some imitate in a middle way between **by accident** <term>per accidens</term> <note>original: "per accidens" — incidentally or by chance, rather than by essential design.</note> and "by their own nature": as if a painting were produced to represent whomever it is *capable* of representing. Others, however, [imitate] as it happens by chance: just as when a painted likeness happens to resemble someone else, quite apart from the artist's intention. Finally, some forms imitate neither by their own nature nor by accident; these refer to nothing at all, nor can they be referred to anything capable of being imitated—if it is even possible for such forms to exist. In the first group, the **ideal reason** <note>original: "ratio idealis" — the degree to which a thing conforms to its perfect, conceptual pattern.</note> is greatest. In the second, it is lesser. In the third, it is minimal; and in the fourth, there is none.

### CONCEPT XXIII. 1

An agent that acts by nature or by chance, rather than by the command of the will, does not require **ideas** <term>ideas</term>. If the **first efficient cause** <term>primus efficiens</term> <note>original: "primus efficiens" — the primary source of change or creation, often identified as God in this philosophical tradition.</note> were such an agent, there would be no "ideas," and the agent would perform no work by its own choice. But enough of **Democritus, Empedocles, and Epicurus** <note>These ancient Greek philosophers were associated with atomism and materialism; the author rejects their view that the universe was formed by random chance or mechanical necessity without divine "Ideas."</note>. If you consider it impossible for the "reason of the agent" to be separated from anything—and even more persistently that this very thing...

### CONCEPT XXV. E

One of our own <note>original: "nostratibus" — likely referring to a fellow member of the author's religious order or philosophical school, such as the Dominicans or Jesuits.</note> said: The **exemplary form** <note>original: "Exemplaris forma" — the perfect blueprint or pattern of a thing existing in the mind of a creator.</note> has the nature of an **end** <note>original: "finis" — the goal or purpose for which something is made.</note>, and from it the agent receives the form by which he acts upon that which is outside himself. It is not proper, however, to think that God acts for the sake of an end other than Himself, or that He receives from elsewhere that which makes Him sufficient for acting; therefore, He does not have Ideas outside of Himself. We, however, must seek them outside and above ourselves, since we possess only their **shadows** within us.

### CONCEPT XXVI. I

Through the **species** <term>species</term> <note>original: "speciem" — in Scholastic philosophy, this refers to the mental representation or "image" that allows the intellect to understand an object, rather than a biological category.</note> that is in the intellect, something is better apprehended than through the species that is in a physical subject, because it is more immaterial. Similarly, something is better known through the species of the thing that is in the **divine mind**, than it could be known through its own essence. Two things are required for a species which is a medium of knowing: the representation of the thing known (which occurs according to its proximity to the **knowable object** <note>original: "cognoscibile"</note>) and a spiritual and immaterial existence according to its existence in the one who knows.

### CONCEPT XXVII. O

Just as Ideas are the primary forms of things, according to which everything that arises and perishes is formed: and they have regard not only to that which is generated and corrupted; but also to that which [can] be generated and perish...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the nature of Ideas, specifically how human mental "shadows" relate to divine archetypes. It addresses the philosophical debate over whether "accidents" (non-essential qualities) and "individuals" (singular things) possess their own perfect Ideas in the mind of God or nature.</meta>

...can be. Thus it is then true that we have formed **shadows** <note>original: "vmbras" — here meaning mental images or reflections of the higher, divine Ideas.</note> of the ideas within ourselves, when they allow for such capacity and tangibility: so that they are adaptable to all possible formations. We have formed them by a certain likeness, which consists in the **revolution of wheels** <note>This likely refers to a mnemonic system or a logical device, such as the rotating paper wheels (volvelles) used in the "Art" of Ramon Llull or Giordano Bruno to combine concepts.</note>. If you are able to attempt another way, then attempt it.

### CONCEPT XXVIII. v

Plato did not posit Ideas of **accidents** <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "Accidentium" — in philosophy, an accident is a property that can change without the thing itself ceasing to exist, such as color, size, or location, as opposed to its "essence."</note>. This was because he understood Ideas to be the proximate causes of things; hence, if anything other than the Idea were the proximate cause of a thing, he did not want that thing to have an Idea. Therefore, in those things which are spoken of by **priority and posteriority** <note>original: "per prius & posterius" — a logical distinction where one thing naturally precedes another in a sequence or hierarchy.</note>, he did not want there to be a common Idea, but rather that the first thing be the Idea of the second. For this reason, **Clement the philosopher** <note>Likely referring to Clement of Alexandria (c. 150–215 AD), who attempted to reconcile Greek philosophy with Christian theology.</note> held that, among beings, the higher ones were the Ideas of the lower ones.

**Theologians** <note>Referring to Scholastic thinkers who integrated Aristotelian and Platonic thought into Christian doctrine.</note> wish for there to be Ideas of accidents, as they understand God to be the immediate cause of each and every thing, even if they do not exclude **secondary gods** <note>original: "secundos deos" — likely referring to celestial intelligences, angels, or planetary powers acting as intermediate causes.</note> and causes. And for our purpose, we also wish for there to be Ideas of all things: because from every conceivable thing, we ascend to these same [Ideas]. For we form **ideal shadows** <note>original: "vmbras ideales"</note> concerning all things. Nor do we thereby destroy Platonic doctrine, as is evident to the intelligent reader.

### CONCEPT XXIX. A

Plato did not posit Ideas of **singular things** <note>original: "Singularium" — individual, specific objects (like "this particular dog") as opposed to the general category.</note>, but only of **species**. This is partly because Ideas pertain only to the production of forms, not of matter. It is also because forms are principally intended by nature, whereas **genera** <term>genera</term> <note>The plural of "genus"; a broad category or class of things.</note> and **individuals** <term>individuals</term> <note>original: "indiuidua" — the smallest, indivisible units of a species.</note> are not.

<summary>This page discusses whether non-essential qualities (accidents) and individual things have divine "Ideas," contrasting Plato's view that only essential species have Ideas with the theological view that God is the cause of all things. It also references a mnemonic system involving the "revolution of wheels" to represent these concepts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion from page 52 regarding the nature of Ideas. While the previous page touched upon Plato's rejection of Ideas for "accidents," this page explores why theologians and the author accept Ideas for "singular things" (individuals) and how these are organized hierarchically within the mind and the divine.</meta>

Theologians posit Ideas of **singular things** <note>original: "Singularium" — individual, specific things as opposed to general categories or species.</note> because they assert that God is the total cause of everything, as much in regard to its matter as to its form. In our current project, we also desire Ideas of singular things. This is because we understand the nature of the **ideated** <term>ideated</term> <note>original: "ideati" — the object or entity that is produced or understood in accordance with a corresponding Idea.</note> through the universal likeness of that which is shaped and perceived. This likeness may exist **before the thing** <note>as a divine plan or archetype</note>, **in the thing** <note>as its internal structure</note>, as the **thing itself**, or **after the thing** <note>as a concept or memory formed after observing the object</note>. Furthermore, this likeness can exist either within the senses or within the intellect, whether that intellect is practical or speculative.

### CONCEPT XXX.

Some thinkers situate less common Ideas as being generated within more common ones; ultimately, they unite all classes of Ideas within the **first being** <note>original: "ente primo" — a philosophical term for the primary source of existence, or God.</note> itself, which they call the **highest intelligible** <note>the ultimate reality that can be grasped by the mind.</note>. You, however, must remember to situate the less common **shadows of ideas** <note>original: "vmbras idearum" — the mental reflections or images humans use to understand the higher, divine Ideas.</note> within the more common ones. Likewise, you should place their less common extrinsic subjects within those that are more common.

<summary>This page argues that Ideas exist for individual things because God is the cause of both matter and form. It also describes a hierarchical system where specific mental "shadows" are nested within more general concepts, all leading back to a primary source of being.</summary>

# ON THE COMBINATION WHICH
## RESULTS FROM THE CONCOURSE OF THE FIRST
### wheel with the second.

---

It will therefore be necessary for someone wishing to grasp for themselves the **general art** <term>general art</term> <note>original: "artem generalem" — a universal system of knowledge, often associated with the combinatoric methods of Ramon Llull, designed to organize all information through logical structures.</note> as a habit of the intellect, the will, and the memory (although for the present we shall restrict it to the perceptions of memory): First, to master the first **primer of elements** <note>original: "elementarium" — a foundational table or list of symbols, letters, or principles used in the system.</note> along with its meanings. Second, the second set. Third, to derive the second through the first. 

We have provided the first two, which are most easily accessible to those well-versed in **Peripatetic** <note>original: "peripateticis" — followers of Aristotle, whose philosophy focused on logic and the physical world.</note> and **Platonic** doctrines. The third we entrust to the student’s own diligence.

Now, let us begin the application and the **focusing** <note>original: "contractionem" — literally "contraction," the process of narrowing down universal concepts into specific, practical applications.</note> of universal intention toward the **art of memory** <note>original: "artem memoriæ" — the Renaissance discipline of using mental architecture and imagery to store and retrieve knowledge.</note>.

<note>The vocabulary below highlights the core components of the mind and the philosophical traditions being synthesized here: combination (complexio), intellect (intellectus), will (voluntas), memory (memoria), general art (artem generalem), Peripatetics (peripateticis), Platonists (Platonicis), and the art of memory (artem memoriæ).</note>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the formal treatise on the "Art of Memory." It follows the preceding discussion on the "concourse of wheels" by providing the philosophical justification for why such an art is necessary and where it resides within the human psyche. Bruno moves from the "shadows" of the divine to the architectural structure of human thought.</meta>

# THE ART OF MEMORY
## BY GIORDANO BRUNO

---

### I.

We consider that Art exists under the shadow of **Ideas** <term>Ideas</term> <note>original: "Idearum" — in Bruno’s Neoplatonic philosophy, "Ideas" are the divine archetypal patterns. Humans cannot see the light of these Ideas directly, only their "shadows" as reflected in the physical world and the mind.</note> when it either rouses a slumbering nature by leading the way; or directs and guides nature when it wanders off course; or strengthens and supports it when it is weak and exhausted; or corrects it when it errs; or follows it when it is perfect and seeks to emulate its diligence.

### II.

Indeed, an art of this kind for pursuing things is, in a general sense, a **discursive architecture** <term>discursive architecture</term> <note>original: "discursiua architectura" — a structural framework for the mind that allows it to move logically and "discursively" from one concept to another.</note>; it is a certain habit of the reasoning soul, extending itself from the principle of the world’s life toward the principle of life in each and every individual thing. 

It does not rest upon any single one of the soul's powers like a mere branch, nor does it emerge from any one specific faculty: rather, it occupies the very trunk of the whole, being the very essence of the entire soul. I believe this is not said without reason: for if this art consisted only in the power of memory, how could it flow forth from the intellect? If it resided only in the intellective faculty, how could it pass across from memory, sense, and appetite—

### III.

But indeed, it is not sufficiently clear what kind of thing this is, or in what manner it is directed generally toward each and every function of the soul; this is what is being investigated. For it is asked: what is the means by which the soul clothes itself in art? By what art does the soul put on art? <note>Bruno is asking a meta-philosophical question: if "Art" is a skill we learn, what is the underlying "Art" that allows the human soul to learn any skill in the first place?</note> Is it not fitting to call "Art" that by which technical Mother Nature <term>technical Mother Nature</term> <note>original: "technica mater natura" — Bruno personifies Nature as a master craftsman or "technician" who instills innate abilities through repetition.</note> strives to render herself expert through repeated actions?

### IIII.

May we not say that before the many specific arts, there exists an art which I would call **organic** <term>organic art</term> <note>original: "ars organica" — from the Greek *organon*, meaning "tool" or "instrument." Bruno is referring to a "tool-making" art of the mind, similar to how Logic was often called the *Organon* of philosophy.</note>? For most craftsmen use an instrument; yet their art is not the instrument itself, but rather something pursued *through* the instrument. Is it not permitted to speak of an art that fabricates the very instruments of the arts? For what else would it be, if not an art?

Furthermore, if no instrument preceded the thing that needed to be crafted, explain to me the logic of the art which must have preceded the [secondary] art? In what subject of the agent did this **organic art** first stand forth? Without any doubt, it existed in that physical reality which was pre-eminent. That reality was formed by a certain positioning into a receiver of the innermost designation <note>The Latin *deminatiōis* is likely a shorthand for *denominationis* (designation/naming) or *deminutionis* (diminution/limitation).</note> of the first instrument. 

If it pleases those who philosophize in a common way to first name the essence of a thing based on its outward form, we let it pass. For it is the custom of artificial things—
