### V.

If this is so, as it appears to the best philosophers: that which is "art" in the primary sense must be called nothing other than a faculty of nature born within reason, along with the seeds of first principles <note>Bruno is referencing the "logoi spermatikoi" or "seed-reasons"—the idea that the blueprints for all knowledge are already latent in the human mind.</note>. In these seeds there exists a potentiality by which they are enticed by external objects, as if by various charms; they are illuminated by the active intellect <term>active intellect</term> <note>original: "agente intellectu" — in medieval and Renaissance philosophy, this is the "light" of the mind that turns potential thoughts into actual ones.</note> as if by a radiating sun; and they receive an influx from the eternal Ideas as if through the mediation of the stars’ alignment—all while being ordered by the Best and Greatest <note>original: "optimo maximo" — a traditional title for God.</note>, who brings all things to fruition toward their actualization and their own proper end, to be pursued according to their capacity. From these things it is clear that it is not without reason that we wish **daedal Nature** <note>original: "dedalam naturam" — referring to Daedalus, the mythological master craftsman; here it means Nature as the supreme artist or architect.</note> to be called the source and substance of all arts.

### VI.

Consider, then, the sense in which we can say that art excels nature in some respects, while being surpassed by her in others. This can only be the case when we observe nature showing certain greater principles in her more distant actions than in those more immediate. Nature is said to perpetuate the **substantial form** <term>substantial form</term> <note>original: "formam substantialem" — the internal essence that makes a thing what it is (e.g., the "human-ness" of a human).</note> within the same species, though she is not able to perpetuate the exact same individual; in these matters, the faculty of art does not extend. ## VII.

But from where, I ask, does this power come to Art? It comes, without doubt, from wherever **innate genius** <term>innate genius</term> <note>original: "ingenium" — a person's natural capacity for invention, wit, and creative thought.</note> flourishes. To whom does this genius most closely belong? To man. And from where did man first emerge, along with all his faculties? Truly, from **Nature** herself as she gives birth. Therefore, if you look at the matter from its origin, and wish to uproot this tree from its very base to transplant it, you will find it leans toward the cultivation and recognition of Nature.

You will surely achieve this when you direct your mind toward that principle which cries out and shouts, illuminating us from within. It is **Nature** who fashions bodies for souls; it is Nature who supplies the souls with their proper instruments. (This is why the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of the Greek philosopher Pythagoras, known for their focus on numbers and the soul’s journey.</note> and the brilliant **Magi** <note>original: "Magorum" — here referring to ancient sages or practitioners of natural magic who understood the hidden links between the physical and spiritual worlds.</note> are said to have discerned the quality of life and the very nature of the soul from the form of the body).

**Nature** herself will be present with you in all things—unless you turn away. For universal Nature does not shrink away or cease to be helpful to us; indeed, **Jupiter** <note>Used here as a poetic or philosophical name for the supreme governing principle of the heavens.</note> rains down seeds upon all things, and the kind **Apollo** <note>The sun god, representing the light of intelligence and the vital heat that sustains life.</note> rises over every plant. Yet not all things drink in an equal measure of life from the powers above, since not all things are turned toward them in the same way. This is clearly evident in ourselves, who, by our own choices, are torn away from communion with those higher powers.

### VIII.

Since, therefore, **Nature** provides everything possible—whether prior to natural things, within natural things, or through natural things—you should understand that while all action proceeds from natural things, you must not ignore that Nature herself is acting through them. Let **common philosophy** <note>This refers to the standard academic teachings of the time, predominantly the Aristotelian tradition taught in universities.</note> distinguish the **positive agent** <term>positive agent</term> <note>original: "agens positiuum" — an entity that acts with intention or a specific, directed force, as often contrasted with passive natural processes.</note> from the natural one as much as it likes; I will not deny it. However, I want this point to be rightly admitted: they are distinguished only in the way an **instrument** is distinguished from the one using it, or as a **middle ground** is distinguished from the one who organizes it, or a **limb** from the one who moves it.

### IX.

For this reason, understand that we are in no way bound to common philosophy when it restricts the name of "Nature" only to **matter** and **form** <note>This refers to "hylomorphism," the traditional view that all things are composed of passive matter and an organizing form.</note>. We recognize it also as an **intrinsic efficient principle** <note>original: "efficiens intrinsecum principium" — a power that resides *inside* a thing and causes it to act or grow, rather than a force applied from the outside.</note>, whether it is common to all things or narrowed down and adapted to one specific individual or another. This is why we listen more gladly to the speech of **ordinary people** <note>original: "idiotas" — in the Renaissance, this term referred to laypeople or those without a formal university education, often seen by some philosophers as having a more direct, "natural" understanding of reality.</note> when they compare the nature of one man with the nature of another. For we should not perceive Nature as a **logical universal** <term>logical universal</term> <note>original: "vniuersale logicum" — an abstract category or mental concept (like the idea of "Humanity") that does not have a physical existence of its own.</note>, but as a **physical reality** that is present both in the whole of existence and within each individual.

### X.

This is the power which makes things that were once perceived by the senses—but are now past or absent—**present** and **visible** once more. From here, indeed...

# XI.

That which people have been accustomed to freely call either **Fate**, or **Necessity**, or **the Good**, or the <term>Demiurge</term> <note>original: "demourgon" — in Platonic philosophy, an artisan-like figure who fashions the physical universe.</note>, or the **World Soul**, or **Nature**: it proceeds from the imperfect to the perfect, communicating to these lower things through motion and time, because the same principle exists in all and in each. Hence, **Art**—which the principle leads by the hand—is said to progress in this same sequence.

Therefore (as it pertains to the purpose of our intention), antiquity is said to have first written with knives upon the **bark of trees**. After this followed an age inscribing upon stones hollowed out with a **chisel**. Then followed **papyrus**, written upon with the juices of cuttlefish. Next came **parchment** membranes, dipped in more skillfully crafted ink. Then, **paper** and ink, and finally letters to be pressed by the **printing press**—by far the most suitable use of all. From knives, I say, to styluses; from styluses to sponges; from sponges to reeds; from reeds to quills; and from quills we have finally arrived at **cast metal elements** <note>This refers to the invention of movable type for the printing press.</note>.

We believe it happened no differently regarding those things which seem to pertain to **internal writing**: while human study from ancient times—whether it took its start from <term>Simonides of Ceos</term> <note>original: "Melico Simonide" — a Greek lyric poet traditionally credited with inventing the "Method of Loci," a mnemonic technique for memorizing information by placing images in mental locations.</note> or from another—in which the use of **places** and **proportional images** takes the place of paper and writing, and the acts of the **imagination** and the **cogitative faculty** take the place of the writer and the pen: species...

## XII.

In the book of the <term>Great Key</term> <note>original: "clauis magnæ" — This likely refers to Bruno's own lost or intended work, *The Great Key*, which detailed his system of mnemonics and philosophy.</note>, you have twelve subjects of **induments** <note>original: "indumentorum" — literally "garments" or "coverings"; here it refers to the different ways a concept can be "clothed" or represented.</note>: **Species**, **Forms**, **Likenesses**, **Images**, **Specters**, **Examples**, **Vestiges**, **Indications**, **Signs**, **Notes**, **Characters**, and **Seals**.

Some of these refer to things sensible to the eye (whether shaped by nature or by art), such as extrinsic form or an **Image**. An **Example** describes—and is described by—painting and other figurative arts that emulate the **Great Mother** <note>original: "matris magnæ" — a common Renaissance reference to Nature as the source of all forms.</note>.

Some refer to the **internal sense** <note>The "internal sense" includes the imagination and memory, located within the mind rather than the physical eyes.</note>, in which they are magnified, extended, and multiplied in measure, duration, and number: such are those things which offer themselves to be handled by the <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "phantasticæ facultati" — the "phantastic faculty," the part of the mind responsible for creating and manipulating mental images.</note>.

Some are those that turn upon the same point of likeness: namely, those which draw their pattern from a form of the same genus and a substance of the same species.

Certain things fall short of the actual substance of the subject, as is evident in all cases where the <term>sophist</term> <note>Here used to mean a philosopher or artist who deals in superficial appearances rather than truth.</note> begs from the real, and where, universally, the **emulative art** begs from nature.

But some things seem so appropriated to art that they appear to support natural things entirely: these are **Signs**, **Notes**, **Characters**, and **Seals**. In these, art has such power that it seems to act **beyond nature**, **above nature**, and, if the matter requires, **against nature**.

<summary>The author introduces twelve categories of mental and physical representation from his "Great Key," distinguishing between images perceived by the eyes and those manipulated by the imagination. He suggests that certain symbolic tools like "Seals" allow art to transcend or even command natural processes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the categorization of the twelve "induments" or ways of representing concepts, specifically focusing on those that cannot be easily visualized, and how "indications" serve as a bridge between physical images and abstract signs.</meta>

## XIII.

These types provide a remedy where one cannot render figures and images, specifically when dealing with things that do not belong to the category of the "imaginable" or "figurable" <note>original: "imaginabilium, vel figurabilium" — things that can be pictured in the mind or drawn as shapes.</note>. For such things lack those <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "accidentibus" — In Renaissance philosophy, "accidents" are the external qualities of a thing (like color, shape, or smell) that allow our senses to perceive it, as opposed to its internal essence.</note> by which the doors of the senses are usually struck; they lack any distinction or arrangement of parts, and without these preceding elements, the act of portraying a likeness cannot succeed. 

Into this class, from one side, enter certain things that hold a middle way: namely, those which in some way refer to something and are themselves referred to; such things are **Indications**. For we use **Indications** to point out not only things that can be made, imagined, or exemplified (as well as the examples, images, and likenesses themselves), but also those things which express—and are expressed by—**seals**, **notes**, and **characters**. Thus, it was not by chance that in our previous list, **Indications** were allotted the middle seat <note>This refers to the list of twelve categories on the previous page, where "Indications" sits between the visual "Vestiges" and the abstract "Signs."</note>.

## XIV.

Therefore, a **Species** <note>original: "species" — here meaning a mental "sight" or appearance.</note> represents the god Mercury; so too do a **Form**, a **Simulacrum**, an **Exemplar**, and a **Specter** <note>These terms represent different levels of "pictures" of Mercury, from a physical statue to a mental memory-image.</note>. But the substance, essence, goodness, justice, and wisdom of Mercury are represented by **Notes**, **Characters**, and **Seals**.

However, those things which indiscriminately make present both the figure of Mercury and all the abstract qualities that are said about Mercury are more properly called **Indications**. Using these as a common trunk for both the "image" and the "note," we indicate and represent both. 

This is clearly seen in the use of demonstrative pronouns <note>Words like "this" or "that."</note>: such as when we speak of Mercury or of a virtue and say "**this** simulacrum," "**this** sign," "**this** note," or "**this** likeness."

<summary>Bruno explains that abstract concepts lacking physical form require "indications" to be remembered. He distinguishes between visual representations (like a picture of Mercury) and symbolic representations (like signs for his wisdom), noting that "indications" bridge both categories.</summary>
<keywords>mnemonics, indications, Mercury, accidents, representation, signs, mental images, abstraction</keywords>

## XV.

Having considered these matters: remember that for this art, no other means can be used to achieve its end than those that are **sensory**, **formed**, and **figured**, and narrowed down to a specific time and place. As was explained in the first volume of the <term>Great Key</term> <note>original: "Clauis magnæ" — likely a reference to Giordano Bruno's own work on the art of memory or a similar hermetic manual.</note>, this is how it happens in all other technical works of the soul. 

Nevertheless, it should be understood that the art does not use everything as if they were **images**. Indeed, many things that must be remembered are not "imaginable" <note>original: "imaginabilia" — things that can be pictured in the mind as a physical shape.</note>, nor can they be shaped into a likeness, nor can they be introduced through some similar visual representation. Such things are technical terms like **essence** <term>vsia</term> <note>From the Greek "ousia," referring to the fundamental nature of a thing.</note>, **substance** <term>ypostasis</term> <note>From the Greek "hypostasis," often used in theology and philosophy to describe the underlying reality or personhood of a thing.</note>, **mind** <term>mens</term>, and others of that kind. Instead, the art uses **signs** for things that can be signified, and **images** for things that can be imagined. Along with this, one must not overlook the fact that images are no less tied to signs than signs are attached to images.

## XVI.

It is from a failure in this connection that a certain weakness arises, whereby the **mental form** <note>original: "species" — in this context, the specific image or "look" of the thing stored in the memory.</note> often fails to appear to those using the art. However, our predecessors do not seem to have considered the matter from this perspective. 

This failure acts like a denser darkness that refracts the sense of sight more than an excellent light might reveal it; it is like a great **crowdedness** <note>original: "celebritas" — here referring to a mental clutter or a frequency of images that causes confusion.</note>, a dispersing distance, and other such things that usually occur in the "places" <note>The "places" (loci) refer to the mental architecture—like rooms in a house—where a practitioner of the Art of Memory "locates" their images.</note> they are accustomed to using. Because of this, they are like dogs that have been struck and bite the stone or the stick, failing to perceive the true author of the blow, and so they blame something else. But since it has been granted to us to find that cause, and

From this, we presume to have succeeded so well that whatever was considered, taught, and organized by the ancients regarding this matter (at least what is explained in their writings that have come into our hands) does not constitute a fitting part of our invention. Our invention is exceptionally potent, and it is to this that the book <term>The Great Key</term> <note>original: "Clauis magnæ" — likely a reference to Giordano Bruno's own work on the art of memory, *Clavis Magna* (1583).</note> is dedicated. ### XVII.

It is said that the study of nature is comparable to the study of the **snub-nosed** <note>original: "simitas" — a classic example from Aristotle's *Metaphysics*. You cannot define "snubness" without the "nose" (matter); it is a "material form" that cannot exist or be understood in abstraction from the physical object.</note>, which looks neither at form alone nor at matter alone (both of which are distinguished by the name of **nature**), but at **formed matter** and the **material form** applied to that matter. It is through these together that what is properly called "natural" is produced. This is the bond which, if removed, leaves no work at all that nature is able to fashion; much less is **art**—which is nature’s handmaiden—able to do so, unless one chooses to dream that there is something even less than nothing. 

For **art** not only assumes nature itself as its primary subject, but also the **natural object** itself as its immediate subject. Therefore, just as every art requires for its fundamental elements a principle of **matter** suitable for its productions, as well as a more harmonious **form** (since the general goal of all things is to exist in some subject... <note>The text cuts off here due to the page break; the catchword "ali-" suggests the sentence continues "in some other subject" or "in some subject or another."</note>

He then begins a philosophical argument that Art, like Nature, must combine both matter and form to create anything substantial.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical comparison between the "art of memory" and the physical arts of painting and writing. The previous page ended mid-word with "natur...", referring to "nature," and discussed the concept of the "snub-nosed" (material form). ...[nature prefers to preserve] rather than to innovate form. In the same way, this art—which shares the same principle as the graphic faculty in general and follows the method of excellent proportion—is structured according to two manifest types of that same faculty. For there is an **internal painting**, which occurs when the mind produces images of things and works that are to be remembered. There is also an **internal writing**, which occurs when the mind organizes and assigns signs, marks, and characters to represent reasons and words. Because these are also given a subject within the realm of things that can be imagined, I do not deny that in common speech, people call those forms "images," whether they are organized for the memory of things or for the retention of words.

### XVIII.

Painting (to use terms appropriate to this art) has a **primary subject** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "subject" or "matter" is the underlying substance that receives a "form."</note> in which it exists, such as a wall, a stone, or similar surfaces. It has a **proximate subject** from which it is made, which is the color itself; and it has for its **form** the very strokes and lines of the colors. Writing also has as its primary subject the paper, acting as a place; it has as its proximate subject the cinnabar <note>original: "minium" — a bright red pigment used for ink; here used as a general term for the medium of writing.</note>, and it has for its form the strokes of the characters themselves. 

In this same way, this art [of memory] admits a twofold subject objectively: first, that which is the **place** <term>locus</term> <note>A "locus" is a mental "room" or "slot" in a memory palace.</note>; and second, the **proximate subject**, which is the thing placed upon or added to it. Potentially, it also admits a twofold subject: namely, **memory** and **imagination** in general (occupying the position of the first subject), and the **imaginable or thinkable form** <note>original: "speciem" — the mental representation or "species" of an object.</note> in general (occupying the position of the other). Furthermore, it admits as its **form** the **intention** and the **comparison of the forms** existing in one subject to the forms existing in another subject. Just as painting and writing adapt physical tools <note>original: "organa" — literally "organs" or "instruments," referring to the hands, brushes, or pens.</note> with which they may shape their matter, so...

<summary>The author defines memory as a form of "internal painting" and "internal writing," breaking it down into its philosophical components: the "place" (matter) and the "image" (form).</summary>

### SECOND PART.

Therefore, a triple consideration must precede the practice of this art. The first examines what, and of what kind, the **subjects** <note>In the context of the art of memory, "subjects" refers to the memory places or "loci"—the mental rooms or settings where information is stored.</note> ought to be. The second teaches what, and of what sort, the **forms** <note>The "forms" are the mnemonic images or "imagines" that the practitioner places within the "subjects" to represent specific ideas or words.</note> are to be prepared. The third teaches how to adapt the **organ** and that medium through which the soul <note>original: "anima"</note> operates more skillfully. All of these matters have been most perfectly dealt with in the first part of the *Great Key* <note>original: "Clavis magna"—likely referring to a larger, foundational work by the author on the art of memory.</note>. However, for the sake of this book—so that it is not truncated and imperfect in itself (for it is not always convenient to send a person seeking the principles of an art back to a secondary discipline; for insofar as principles are narrowed down to a specific type by certain differences, they migrate into the first part of that specific science)—we will provide three orders of rules for these three points. 

The first concerns the **matter** or the **subject**. The second concerns the **form** or the **addition**. The third concerns the **instrument**, which carries the power of the efficient cause <note>The "efficient cause" is the primary source of change; here, the mental process that actually creates the memory.</note>; thus, the causes, the efficient type, and the instrument converge into the same thing.

#### ON SUBJECTS.

Therefore, the primary subject is a **technical extension**, or an ordered fold within the **fantastic faculty** <note>original: "phantastica facultas"—the mental power of imagination that allows a person to visualize things not currently present to the senses.</note>. It is sown with types of receptacles that have flowed in through the windows of the soul <note>The "windows of the soul" are the five senses through which we perceive the world.</note>. It is distinguished by different parts, receiving all things seen and heard in their own order, and retaining them at the soul’s pleasure...

...ing. This definition regards the common **subject** of common **forms**, derived from the common art which has been handed down to us from antiquity. The first subject, according to the principles of the <term>Great Key</term> <note>original: "Clavis magna"; likely the author's primary treatise on mnemonics.</note>, is the <term>imaginative chaos</term> <note>original: "phantasticum chaos"; refers to the raw, unorganized state of the mind's creative potential before it is shaped into specific memory images.</note>. This "chaos" is so manageable that the <term>cogitative power</term> <note>original: "cogitatiua potentia"; the active faculty of the mind that processes and organizes information.</note>—by weighing and balancing things seen and heard—can bring them into such an order and likeness that the mind is able to consistently present those things perceived through the ears or eyes, from their first to their final parts. It is as if one were suddenly encountering the sight of a new tree, an animal, or an entire world. 

Indeed, such a chaos seems to behave no differently than a cloud driven by external winds; depending on the different forces and patterns of those winds, the cloud is able to take on infinite shapes and every kind of appearance. How fruitful and noble this subject truly is can be judged better by personal experience than by any description. However, let him who can draw this knowledge out from the *Great Key* do so; for **not everyone is granted the chance to visit Corinth** <note>original: "non enim omnibus dabitur hanc adire Corynthum"; a classical proverb meaning that not everyone has the means or talent to achieve a difficult or expensive goal.</note>. Now, therefore, let us return to the subject as it was first defined.

## II.

The first subject consists of material parts—and indeed, parts so material that they do not escape the visual faculty. This allows the <term>imaginative faculty</term> <note>original: "phantastica facultas"; the mind's ability to form and manipulate mental images.</note> to contemplate them in its own order. It can use these parts and principles to arrange them into "monsters" and countless new <term>transformations</term> <note>original: "metamorphoses"</note>, looking upon them once they are arranged as if they were fixed to a globe. Therefore, subjects that are more immaterial are not admitted into this fellowship; those are discussed in the "true art of arts" and the "faculty of faculties" <note>This likely refers to pure Logic or Metaphysics, which deal with abstract concepts rather than the visual "material" images required for this specific mnemonic system.</note>.

### III.

In the arrangement of these subjects, a **proportion** <note>original: "ratio"; here meaning a harmonious relationship or rule of measurement.</note> must be maintained between greatness and smallness in relation to the scale and perspective of a human being. A balance is required between intensity and relaxation, relative to the limits of the senses; between the past and the future, relative to the present act; between an excess of parts and a deficiency of them, relative to the integrity of the whole thing being presented; between distance and closeness, relative to the fitness of motion; and between the starting point and the destination, relative to the natural arrival of that which is moved.

### IIII.

Of these subjects, one type is **most common**, because it can be extended as far as the lap of the <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "phantasia"; the faculty of the mind that forms and stores mental images.</note> can encompass; the imagination can add as much as it likes to the quantity of the established world, though it cannot subtract as much as it likes. Another type is **common**, which consists of a collection of <term>cosmic perspectives</term> <note>original: "Cosmicarum perspectarum partium"; likely referring to large-scale mental maps of the universe or heavens.</note>. Another is **less common**, such as the <term>political</term> <note>original: "politicum"; in this context, memory places modeled after the layout of a city or public state.</note> scale. Another is **proper**, namely the <term>economic</term> <note>original: "oeconomicum"; memory places modeled after a private household or domestic building.</note> scale. Another is **more proper**, which is the <term>four-part</term> <note>original: "tetrathomum"; a memory room divided into four specific areas or "atoms."</note> or <term>five-part</term> <note>original: "pentethomum"; a memory room divided into five areas.</note> scale. Another is **most proper**, which is the <term>atom</term> <note>original: "athomum"; in mnemonics, this is the smallest, indivisible unit of space where a single image is placed.</note>—I say "atom" not in a general sense, but within this specific practice. 

Of all these modes, the first (the infinite) is excluded by its very nature from the use of this present art. For we already know how infinite things may be reduced into a single hall <note>original: "atrium"; a central room or court, used here as a metaphor for a primary memory location.</note> and multiplied within the same.

## V.

These things also happen to occur in two ways: as **animate** and **inanimate**. Animate things occur when the <term>substantive</term> subjects <note>original: "subiecta substantiua"; in this mnemonic context, the "substantive" refers to the fixed location or place itself.</note> appear bright and distinguished by <term>adjectives</term> <note>original: "adiectiuis"; refers to the images or qualities attributed to a location to make it memorable.</note>, which are indeed moved by the flow of arriving forms. Inanimate things, however, are brought forth empty and void. Therefore, beware lest you experience that common saying: "Empty things for the empty." For in the <term>practice</term> <note>original: "praxi"; the practical application of the art of memory.</note> of this art, you will hope in vain for what they say: *The walls will cry out; the stones will give forth their voice.* <note>A reference to Luke 19:40 and Habakkuk 2:11, suggesting that unless the memory places are properly prepared, they will remain "silent" and fail to trigger recollection.</note>

## VI.

Entrust common things to common places; less common things to less common places; proper things to proper places; and more proper and most proper things to those that are more and most proper. <note>The author is advising a hierarchical matching system where general information goes in general mental maps, and specific information goes in specific, private "rooms."</note> Here you have a point of consideration by which you may not only be freed from all fear of <term>forgetting</term> <note>original: "obliuionis".</note>; but you may also be made more ready and secure in the more perfect uses of **shaping** and **inscribing**, as well as in ordering and finding the **method of methods**. And you have this, in its own way, in the roots of the first **Great Key**. <note>original: "Clauis magnæ"; likely a reference to one of the author's primary mnemonic treatises or a specific conceptual tool within his system.</note>

By **subtraction**, a statue of **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius"; a classic example from Aristotle and Cicero where a sculptor "finds" the statue by removing excess stone.</note> is made from stone. By **alteration**, wine becomes vinegar. Likewise, other things are attempted through mixing, others through separation. Some by binding, others by loosening, and still others by sequence and continuity, in order to change the form and the universally changeable nature of the thing.

### IX.

That also must not be overlooked: subjects must be viewed in the gaze of internal thought in proportion to how the eyes actually see. For just as a physical object pressed directly against the sense of sight is not actually "seen," and a physical object placed too far away from the sense lacks the power of **sensibility** <note>original: "sensibilitatis"; the quality of being perceivable by the senses.</note> (since a book can be read neither when it is too close to the eyes nor when it is set too far away), so it is in our task. The gaze of internal thought must be regulated so that it fashions and shapes the object for itself at a moderate distance from the focusing power of the mind, according to the capacity of that faculty. Everyone must be equally careful not to bring a subject to the **specular vision** <note>original: "speculari visui"; from the Latin *speculum* (mirror), referring to the mind’s ability to "see" a mental image as if it were reflected in a mirror.</note> as if we were merely believing or remembering with our **natural memory**. For it often happens, from a lack of this careful consideration, that someone imagines they are shaping a subject or looking at it as a finished form, [when they are actually just reciting words from memory without a clear mental picture].

### X.

To remove those things that disrupt the continuity of the subjects or hinder their distribution and distinction, it is advisable that everything placed *between* one subject and the next be considered as if abolished or deleted. <note>The author suggests mentally "deleting" the empty space or irrelevant details between two memory loci to keep the sequence of information clear.</note> On the other hand, where a continuous and uniform space happens to be stretched too far—so that its very nature separates the subjects more than is necessary—you will be able to subordinate one after another by your own placement, and even conceive of subjects marked with **adjectives** <note>original: "adiectivis"; here meaning descriptive qualities or distinct mental markers added to a location.</note>. Indeed, one must dwell on them in thought until they become so familiar to you that they seem to differ in no way from the truest things. You will achieve this with only a small amount of care, if you wish.

### XI.

Truly, the repeated traversal of the subjects brings as much benefit as the present **artifice** <note>original: "artificium"; the specialized skill or method of the memory art.</note> itself can provide. A cithara-player <note>original: "cytharedus"; a performer on the cithara, an ancient Greek stringed instrument similar to a lyre or lute.</note> skilled by habit plays the cithara most perfectly by the power of habit alone, without having to think about it. They can see these expressions no less clearly than if they were reading them from a polished and exquisite manuscript.

<meta>This page shifts the focus from the psychological power of habit to the philosophical necessity of "variety" (varietas) in the Art of Memory. The author argues that because nature itself never repeats exactly, the human mind requires distinct, individual images to function effectively without becoming weary or confused.</meta>

# XII.

You see, therefore, how much variety is favored by nature’s high standing. The limbs <note>original: "membra"; refers to the constituent parts or elements of the universe.</note> of the world are various. Within those limbs of the world, the species are various. Within those species, the shapes of individuals are various: for one olive tree is not shaped exactly like another olive tree, nor is one man exactly like another. 

And so, all things are distinguished by their differences according to their capacity; individuals are set apart from other individuals, and all things are separated from all other things by their own unique characteristics, as if by boundary lines. Therefore, when you are about to conform to nature <note>The author suggests that the student of the Art of Memory should mimic the "creativity" of nature when designing mental images.</note>, strive for diversity in all things: in the mode of being, in size, in form, in shape, in outward appearance, in disposition, in its limits, and in its position. Clothe your subjects in as many distinctions as you can—in acting, in enduring, in giving, in taking, in subtracting, in adding, and by other ways of altering them, as we have mentioned. 

Indeed, "being" and "one" are used interchangeably <note>This refers to a principle in Scholastic philosophy: "Ens et unum convertuntur." For something to exist as a "being," it must be a single, unified thing.</note>: whatever is not "one" is not a "being." We perceive each thing to be a single "one" for this very reason: because it is bounded in its own way by its own proper difference. 

To all our senses, uniformity produces a sense of weariness. For the senses are not only denied pleasure by a single, frequently repeated, and continuous type of quality; they cannot even endure one and the same thing for any length of time if it remains affected in exactly the same way. This fact was by no means missed by those who, considering the extremely swift flux <note>original: "fluxum"; the idea that the physical world is in a constant state of change.</note> in all natural things, thought it impossible for the same river to be touched by the feet twice—or indeed, even once <note>A reference to the famous doctrine of Heraclitus: "No man ever steps in the same river twice." The "even once" addition is often attributed to his follower Cratylus, who argued that since the water is moving while you step, the river is changing even as you touch it.</note>.

<summary>This chapter argues that variety is a fundamental law of nature and a necessity for the human mind, instructing the memory practitioner to make every mental image distinct and unique to avoid mental fatigue.</summary>
<keywords>variety, diversity, nature, Heraclitus, being and unity, mental images, distinction, flux, philosophy of memory</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page follows a discussion on the necessity of variety in nature and how the mind requires distinct, individual differences to perceive and remember objects. The author now moves from the philosophical principle of variety to its practical application in "affectivity"—the power of an image to strike the mind and lodge itself in the memory.</meta>

## XIII.

From this depends the **affectivity** of subjects. I use the term "affectivity" <note>original: "affectiuitas"; in the context of the Art of Memory, this refers to the "strikingness" or the capacity of a mental image to move the soul and stay in the mind.</note> to mean the active faculty of producing an impression, which occurs when subjects are either endowed by their intrinsic nature or marked by their very position with some alluring or pressing variety. For this reason, it is advisable for some to add "attached subjects" to the principal subjects; this is so that the affectivity which they do not possess by themselves, they may be able to receive through the addition of other things grafted onto them, as it were. 

Why is this so? Because the more they are intensified or relaxed in their affectivity, the more powerfully or slowly they can move the "affectible" **imagination** <note>original: "phantasia"; the faculty of the soul that receives, stores, and combines images from the senses.</note> itself, and enter the hall of memory and return to it. 

From this come the spurs, the salts, the stings, and the seasonings of thought. Hence, when those who have forgotten something attempt to remember, they repeat, recapitulate, and resume their search, as if through diversity itself—through these very shifts, or to express it better, through the *diversity within those shifts*—they hope to summon the **spirit of memory** <note>original: "spiritum memoriae"; in Renaissance psychology, "spirits" were subtle, vaporous substances that acted as the medium between the physical body and the soul, carrying information to the memory.</note>. 

This process succeeds most easily for those who do so with a mind that is least disturbed; otherwise, they are drawn into even greater confusion as that mental disturbance grows hotter and hotter. 

However, how great the power of the "affections" <note>original: "affectuum"; the emotions or psychological states, such as joy, fear, or anger, used to make memory images more vivid.</note> is in general, and how they are to be provoked, preserved, and varied, is suggested quite clearly in the book **The Great Key** <note>original: "Clauis magnae"; a reference to "Clavis Magna," a lost or projected work by Giordano Bruno intended to explain the deeper secrets of his mnemonic system.</note>.

<summary>The author explains "affectivity," the power of mental images to move the mind, suggesting that bland images be "seasoned" with added details to make them more memorable. He references the use of emotions and "spirits" to retrieve forgotten information, citing his work "The Great Key" for further study.</summary>

<keywords>affectivity, imagination, phantasia, spirit of memory, Clavis Magna, Giordano Bruno, mnemotechnics, emotions, variety</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on "affectivity" from the previous page, transitioning from the concept of making mental images striking to the formal definition of "The Attached" (Adiectum). This section introduces the specific methods of creating these mental forms within the imagination, referencing the author's broader system of mnemonics and the "Great Key."</meta>

# ON THE ATTACHED.

## I.

"The Attached" <note>original: "Adiectum"; refers to the additional details or attributes "grafted" onto a memory image to make it more memorable.</note>, or form, in this category is defined as something placed beside a subject—whether physical, technical, or imaginary—in order to express or signify something through the skillful machinery of thought, by presenting, portraying, noting, or indicating it in a manner similar to painting or writing. This principle regards those common forms that have flowed down to us from antiquity to our own times. 

However, "Form," as it is drawn from the roots of the **Great Key** <note>original: "Clauis magnæ"; likely a reference to a specific work by the author, such as Giordano Bruno's *Clavis Magna* (The Great Key), which details his hermetic and mnemonic systems.</note>, is a brought-forth and unfolded order of thinkable species <note>original: "specierum"; in this context, mental images or the "forms" of things as they exist in the mind.</note>, arranged into statues, or into the <term>microcosm: a "little world," usually referring to the human being as a reflection of the entire universe</term>, or generally into some other architecture. This is done for the purpose of inwardly noting or depicting anything that can be spoken, led out from the imaginative <term>chaos: the primordial, unformed state of the mind's potential before a specific image is created</term>, which allows for all <term>metamorphoses: transformations or changes from one form into another</term>. We provide its Image (or Type) here, not so much to be explained at this moment, but to be inspected.

## FIGURE ONE.

## II.

There is chaos there, in your primordial nature, which does not exclude the order and series of elements and numbers, since it is necessary to understand it not only as the <note>original: "informe formabile"</note> formless capable of being formed, but also to conceive of it as that which can be formed in an orderly way. It is, as you see, distinguished by various intervals within itself and its parts, formal in every respect...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mnemonic "forms" and the "Great Key," specifically focusing on how a visual or conceptual diagram (the "figure") operates to organize information. It transitions from the general definition of "The Attached" to the technical application of a memory wheel system involving letters, numbers, and celestial symbols.</meta>

**Figure:** while the designated **shaper** <note>original: "formator"; the active principle or agent that gives shape to mental images.</note> through element **A** runs through the unformed circuits and semi-diameters <note>The "circuits" (concentric rings) and "semi-diameters" (spokes) refer to the components of a memory wheel or volvelle, a tool used for combinatoric thought.</note> of numbers and elements, he impresses one figure through **Aries**; another through **Taurus**; and others through all the rest. He impresses one through **Aries** returning again with **Saturn**, another migrating back with **Mars**, another with both of these, and another without either. From this, things can be formed and reformed unto infinity—whether they be the numbers themselves and the elements, or the "motors" <note>original: "motores"; the moving parts of the mnemonic system that drive the creation of new combinations.</note> and efficient causes led in various ways.

This is what it means to give form to the unformed <term>chaos: the primordial, disorganized state of potential information before it is structured by the mind</term>. Whether you refer these same shapers to the things formed, or refer various and different shapers to the same formed things, it makes no difference to our purpose. 

Nevertheless, that which remains immobile and underlies—by the very fact that it underlies and is formed—should be regarded as the **female** to the **male** <note>A traditional philosophical metaphor where the "male" (Form) provides the shape and the "female" (Matter/Chaos) provides the receptive substance.</note>, and as something utterly unformed so that it can be formed from every direction. By common judgment, it would be a "more perfect" chaos if it consisted of disordered, <term>heterogeneous: consisting of diverse and unrelated parts</term> elements, but such a state would be of no use to anyone. 

Indeed, for the service of memory, it is necessary that numbers and elements be arranged in a certain order; so that when the motors and shapers arrive, they are able to receive other memorable forms as well. Moreover, they are (as you see) so orderly arranged that the same thing can never occur in the same semi-diameter or in the same circuit, whether it be an element or a number. 

Many other most excellent things can be treated through this figure, but this is by no means the place for it. Whether it was placed here by deliberate consultation, I do not decide, but I affirm it nonetheless. I would say this one thing: if you contemplate it more attentively along with the reasons explained here, you will be able to acquire such a figurative art as is useful not only for memory, but...

...and it will contribute in a wonderful way by coming to the aid of all the other **powers of the soul** <note>original: "animæ potentiis"; in Renaissance psychology, these include the faculties of reason, imagination, and will.</note>.

### III.

Therefore, first, it must be noted regarding these "attached things" <note>original: "adiectis"; items added to or associated with the memory images to enhance recall.</note> that their nature exists between **excess** and **deficiency**, **intensity** and **slackness**, the **past** and the **future**, **distance** and **proximity**—all relative to the scale of a human (or half a human), to our perception, and to the present moment in which memory must reside.

### IIII.

Some of these things are **animate** (living), and these can function as instruments, efficient causes, adjuncts, and works; others are **inanimate**, and these function only as instruments, adjuncts, and works. Among the animate things, some **possess reason** <note>original: "rationis participia"; literally those "participating in reason," referring to human beings as opposed to animals.</note>, and these are suited for every form of action, passion, and the neutral state of being. Others lack reason, and to these (as is quite clear), what has been said generally does not fully apply.

### V.

Of these things, some are **natural**, some are **artificial**, and some have glided from the external senses into the **internal senses** <note>The "internal senses" were thought to be faculties of the brain—like imagination and estimation—that processed data from the eyes and ears.</note>. Some are formed within the internal senses themselves; the various types of these are <term>Form: the essential shape or nature of a thing</term>, <term>Likeness: a resemblance or similarity</term>, <term>Image: a mental representation</term>, <term>Figure: a geometric or symbolic shape</term>, <term>Pattern: a model or archetype to be followed</term>, <term>Character: a distinctive mark or letter</term>, and <term>Sign: a symbol that stands for something else</term>, distinguished according to their formal meanings as set forth in the considerations of the **Great Key** <note>original: "Clavis Magna"; the author's primary system for organizing knowledge through mnemonic wheels.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "attached things" (the mnemonic images or attributes added to a memory system). While the previous page categorized these images into living and non-living types, this page moves into the practical rules for their appearance, specifically focusing on their size and their emotional impact on the mind.</meta>

### VI.

As it pertains to their **size**: it is appropriate that these "attached things" <note>original: "adiecta"; these are the symbols, tools, or attributes added to a memory image to help a person remember a specific concept.</note> be proportionate to the subjects they are placed upon, just as they are in the actual nature of things. Otherwise, they would easily be lost, and they would either dull or scatter the **vision of the imagination** <note>original: "visum phantasiæ"; in this period, "phantasia" was the faculty that created and stored mental images.</note>. Certainly, a tiny script <note>original: "microgramma"; literally "small writing."</note> on a large page requires a much longer search, and once sought, it is found only with difficulty. Likewise, a tree that fills or exceeds its space with its sheer bulk does not present a clear shape. 

Instead of these extremes, we highly approve of the diligence of certain practitioners who, when small things that escape the vision of the imagination must be added as subjects, add that form to another "adjunct" <note>original: "adiectiuo"; a secondary object or person associated with the primary memory image.</note> which it is accustomed to cling to or accompany. Thus, the **Archer** will bring the arrow, the **scribe** the pen, and the **Shoemaker** the needle. So great is the power in connection, attachment, precedence, accompaniment, and consequence, that they make invisible things visible, make **intellectual concepts** <note>original: "intelligibilia"; things understood by the mind rather than the physical senses.</note> universally perceptible to the senses, and make even difficult perceptions easily felt.

### VII.

As it pertains to their **quality**: let them be illustrious; let them be things that have the power to strike the imagination and the thought. For instance, they should bring with them something marvelous, fearful, pleasant, sad, friendly, hostile, abominable, plausible, prodigious—something that inspires hope or suspicion, and altogether, whatever might powerfully burst into the **innermost emotions** <note>original: "intimos affectus"; the goal of the "Art of Memory" was to use "striking images" (imagines agentes) that provoked strong feelings to ensure they were not forgotten.</note>. From this point on, take care that you do not wander.

It emphasizes using logical associations, like a scribe's pen, to make abstract or small concepts easier to visualize.</summary>

...understanding our instruction less soberly when we enumerated signs, badges, marks, and seals among the types of **adjuncts** <note>original: "species adiectorum"; these are the secondary symbols—like a crown or a tool—added to a main memory image to give it a specific meaning.</note>. For you must modify all these things by what was just said regarding quantity [size], and the eye of consideration must be fixed on what is held in the considerations of the **Great Key** <note>original: "Clauis magnæ"; likely a reference to a specific mnemonic manual or a foundational concept within this memory system.</note>: namely, that the entrance from the senses and the imagination to the memory faculty can open through nothing except the **thinking faculty** <term>cogitatiuam facultatem</term> <note>in Renaissance psychology, this was the "bridge" faculty that processed sensory images into something the intellect could store.</note>.

### VIII.

As it pertains to **relation** <term>relatio</term>. It is necessary that adjuncts are not applied to subjects as if they were thrown there by chance or accident; rather, they must be related as things contained to their containers; as proper garments to the wearer; as things protected to their protectors. They should be so connected to one another that they cannot be shaken apart by any whirlwind. 

Let them be related in all their parts to all the parts of the subject: whether as relevant or irrelevant, as ordered or disordered, resisting or agreeing. This must be done universally so that the concept of one is connected to the concept of the other. For who could conceive of this adjunct, **royal dignity** <term>Dignitas regia</term> <note>a quality or status used as a mnemonic marker.</note>, if it were cut off from any **subject** <term>subiectum</term> <note>the primary figure or object in a memory "place."</note>? 

Therefore, let the adjuncts be understood simultaneously with their subjects: and they will come forth like letters engraved in stone; they will not fly about as if driven by the wind, nor will they be confused like shapes traced in stretches of sand.

<summary>This page explains that for memory images to be effective, their symbolic attributes must be logically and physically "anchored" to the main subject rather than floating freely. It emphasizes that the thinking faculty is the bridge that moves these images into long-term memory.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for creating memory images. Following the discussion on "relation" (how symbols connect to their subjects), the text now moves to the importance of motion, action, and variety to keep these images vivid in the mind.</meta>

### IX.

Let the **adjuncts** <note>original: "adiecta"; the symbols or secondary images added to a main memory subject.</note> also be understood to act upon the **subjects** <note>original: "subiecta"; the primary images or locations in the memory palace.</note>, and within the subjects: or to be acted upon by the subjects, or within the subjects. I say, let them be considered enlivened by some action or passion; so that by some motion, they may awaken the **internal sight** <term>internum visum</term> <note>The "mind's eye" used in visualization.</note>, which is as if drowsy, through a certain agitation. This is achieved by: Wandering, Passing through, Going under, Approaching, Departing, Coming together, Ascending, Descending, Confronting, Deviating, Avoiding, and Abandoning. 

By these means, they may Move something near, Push, Thrust, Exclude, Separate, Turn around, Turn away, Restrain, Agitate, Cast away, Twist back, Cast down, Demolish, Tear down, Erect, Elevate, Uproot, Stretch out, Scrape away, Wipe off, Remove, Empty, or Drain. 

By all these actions, it is not brought about that the adjuncts are less attached and fixed to the subjects; rather, it causes them to be fixed even more. For the power of **fixing** <term>fixionis</term> and perseverance is situated within motion itself. Do not, therefore, despair of the stability of these things for that reason; for continuous motion does not lack its own stability by which it remains continuous: just as the poet calls Fortune "constant in her own inconstancy" <note>This is a common Renaissance reference to Ovid or other classical poets describing the predictable unpredictability of fate.</note>. However, let a limit be observed in variety, multitude, speed, and slowness; and let them not lack the conditions pertaining to the quality of the adjuncts.

### X.

Just as in the subjects, so also in the adjuncts, **uniformity** <term>vniformitas</term> must be avoided. For indeed, how much nature— <note>original: "natu-"; the word is cut off and likely continues as "natura" (nature) on the following page.</note>

It also cautions against uniformity, advocating for variety in how these images are constructed.</summary>

...and how harmonious that variety should be can be gathered from the things said above. Hence also the saying:

->*By such variety nature is beautiful.*<- <note>original: "Per tanto variar natura è bella." This is a famous Italian proverb often cited in Renaissance philosophy to justify complexity and diversity in art and thought.</note>

It is indeed permissible to apply the same **adjuncts** <note>The secondary symbols or attributes added to a memory location.</note> to different **subjects** <note>The primary locations or central images in a memory system.</note>: but this should only be done in cases where they are well-separated, and placed at a distance after many other things have been interposed, or when they are involved in different actions and exist according to different types of **dispositions** <term>habitudinum</term> <note>The specific state, posture, or "habit" of the image.</note>.

### XI.

The adjuncts also share this principle in common with the subjects: it is fitting and necessary that they follow the same distinction as the subjects themselves. Thus, those things which pertain to one subject should not embrace or overlap with those belonging to another. Instead, they should avoid all continuity, connection, **crowding** <note>original: "constipationem"; refers to the dense packing or thickening of images that leads to mental confusion.</note>, and mingling. 

For indeed, if the adjuncts of different subjects were to admit actions and motions **back and forth** <note>original: "vltro citroque"</note> between one another—as if they were joining hands in conflict and were occupied with other business—you would call them to your service in vain.

<summary>This page explains that while variety is essential to nature and memory, symbols (adjuncts) must be kept strictly distinct to avoid mental confusion. It warns that if symbols from different memory locations interact or "mingle," the memory system will fail to function.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the physical arrangement of memory symbols to the internal psychological "instrument" (the organ) and the cognitive process of recollection. It moves from external mnemonics to internal Aristotelian-style psychology.</meta>

## ON THE INSTRUMENT <note>original: "DE ORGANO"; here referring to the mental or physical tool used by the soul to process thoughts.</note>

### I.

It remains for us to determine something concerning the **instrument** which the soul uses for its purpose. For it is not enough for the agent <note>The "agent" refers to the active part of the mind or soul that performs the work of thinking.</note> to understand the form to be introduced and the subject to be shaped; rather, when the necessity of the task demands it, it is worthwhile not to omit what the **vehicle** <note>The medium that carries information from the mind into the memory.</note> of the form (which moves from the agent into the subject) is in its essence. Likewise, we must consider what it ought to be like and how it is to be handled.

### II.

Nine things concur to produce recollection <term>rememorationem</term> <note>The active process of searching for a lost memory, as opposed to simple memory.</note> and memory:

1.  **Antecedent intention**, by which some sense—whether external or internal—is first put into action because it is moved by an object.
2.  **Provocation of the imagination**, where the moved sense now awakens the imagination, either directly or indirectly.
3.  **The passive motion of the imagination**, by which it is driven to investigate.
4.  **The active motion of the imagination**, by which it now performs its investigation.
5.  **Scrutiny**, by which the focusing imagination searches.
6.  **The image**, namely the **memorable representation** <term>species</term> <note>In Renaissance psychology, a "species" is the mental likeness or "stamp" of an object that exists in the mind.</note>.
7.  **The intention of the image**, namely the reason by which it is made memorable in the present moment while other images are excluded.
8.  **The presentation of that intention**, that is, the act of making that intention present to the mind.
9.  **The judgment**, by which it is grasped that this is indeed the specific intention of that image.

<summary>This page defines the "instrument" (organum) of the soul as the vehicle for thought and outlines a sophisticated nine-step psychological process for how a person moves from an initial sensory stimulus to the successful recollection and judgment of a memory image.</summary>
<keywords>instrument of the soul, cognitive process, recollection, imagination, mental species, intention, Aristotelian psychology, memory mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nine factors required for memory. Having listed the factors on the previous page, the author now focuses on "scrutiny" (the fifth factor), describing it as a functional tool or "instrument" of the mind that sorts through thoughts like a physical staff sorting through a pile of objects.</meta>

### III.

Among all these things, it is easily established that what we call **scrutiny** <note>original: "scrutinium"; the active search or investigation within the mind to find a specific memory.</note> or the **separator** <note>original: "discerniculum"; a tool used to distinguish or divide one thing from another.</note> (namely, that by which thought inquires and distinguishes) takes on the nature of an **instrument**. We designate it by this common name because, since no consideration has been given to it until our own times, it lacks a specific and well-known name of its own. The lack of such a name, definition, and consideration has closed off the path to this discovery; for the root of how recollection <term>reminiscentia</term> and memory are formed has lain hidden in a blind depth of thick darkness. 

This instrument exists, therefore, within the faculty of thought. It is proportional to a **staff** in our hand (from which you may understand the reason for the name I have given it, or rather, the name that ought to be given), by which, while standing, we move, break down, and scatter a pile lying on the ground. We do this so that a chestnut might emerge for us from the midst of acorns, or so that a specific, chosen chestnut might be pulled from a heap of other chestnuts.

### IV.

And this instrument is known by its action. For since there is a twofold power—namely, **conservation** <note>The ability of the mind to store or "keep" information over time.</note> and **recollection**—which, although they are one in reality (as the authorities say), they are distinguished by their logic; just as both are distinguished from the **imagination**, though even more so in my judgment. 

For the power of conservation lies on the border of the memorative and imaginative powers, and serves as the boundary between them. Therefore, the power of recollection is distinguished from the imaginative power; since sometimes, even without an "imaginable" form <note>A mental image or picture.</note>, the **intention** <term>intentio</term> <note>In this context, "intention" refers to the mental "pointer" or the conceptual meaning of an object, rather than its visual appearance.</note> of an imaginable form accompanies...

<summary>The author defines "scrutiny" as a mental instrument, metaphorically like a staff used to sort through a pile of objects to find a specific item. He also begins to distinguish between the powers of conservation (storage), recollection (retrieval), and imagination.</summary>

<keywords>scrutiny, discerniculum, recollection, memory, imagination, mental instruments, conservation, intention, cognitive psychology</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the "instrument" of scrutiny, the mental tool used to distinguish and organize memories. It bridges the gap between the internal faculties of the mind—thought, imagination, and memory—and the practical "Art of Memory" used by scholars.</meta>

...be grasped; at other times, however, the form is not stripped from its intention. From this it happens that although we might retain many things at once, we cannot imagine many things at once. In these matters, then, this is what the **instrument** <note>This refers to "scrutiny," the mental process defined on the previous page as a tool for sorting thoughts.</note> does: it **Discerns**, **Distinguishes**, and **Orders**—or, if one prefers to speak more precisely, it is that by which **Discretion**, **Determination**, and **Ordering** occur.

Thus, from the **thinking faculty** <term>cogitativa</term> <note>In medieval and Renaissance psychology, the *cogitativa* was the power that compared and analyzed mental images.</note>, one thing is set apart for itself into a present view out of many; I say that one thing imagined, or the image of one thing, is drawn out for the purpose from the many things retained. And just as the **imaginative faculty** <term>imaginativa</term> is said to grasp what is depicted on a wall by a painter, and the **memorative faculty** <term>rememorativa</term> retains the "intention" <note>The "intention" (intentio) refers to the internal meaning or mental concept of the object, rather than its physical appearance.</note> of that picture, so this organ takes the role of conveying and applying—or rather, it is the means by which the delivery and application of one thing to another occurs, as if they were bound together, just as happens in the interlocking of links in a chain and similar things. 

Therefore, with nature itself as a guide, **Art** <note>The "Art of Memory" (Ars Memoriae).</note> until our own times has emulated this connection through the **order of places** <note>The "Method of Loci," where memories are "placed" in a mental building or street to be retrieved in order.</note>. Where Art was not able to connect thing to thing, it arranged that which belonged to one after that which belonged to another—not, I say, what was proper to the one or the other by nature or reason, but what was "appropriated" to it by its current position. In this way, as if from external things, it sought to capture the application of the image to the memorative faculty.

Behold, therefore, the duty of **scrutiny** <note>original: "scrutinij"</note> is that the **units** <note>original: "vnitates"; the author clarifies he means "many individual things."</note> (for so I might call the "many ones," if the critics of words will allow me something) that are to be taken one by one are arranged into order through it. This happens just as if we had branded a hundred sheep individually with different numerical marks, such as 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, and so on; soon, as they run together in a crowd and in confusion, so that one hinders another, the shepherd turns these sheep aside with his **staff**...

<summary>This page describes how the mind uses "scrutiny" to isolate a single thought or image from a mass of stored memories. It explains that the Art of Memory imitates this natural process by using spatial "places" to link unrelated ideas together in a sequence.</summary>
<keywords>Scrutiny, Thinking faculty, Imaginative faculty, Memorative faculty, Art of Memory, Method of Loci, Mental Organization, Analogy of the Shepherd</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of "scrutiny" as the bridge between raw thought and the formal Art of Memory. It moves from describing the mechanics of mental focus to the use of numbers as a foundational system for organizing data.</meta>

...turns them; it handles these, entices those, so that it might lead each one into its own order. Thus, once other thoughts are driven away most promptly, the **thinking faculty** <term>cogitatio</term> selects a specific thing after another by the very power of **scrutiny** <term>scrutinium</term> <note>The mental tool for investigating and sorting ideas.</note>. 

Here is the place to consider an analogy: just as a shepherd <note>original: "gregarius"</note> receives the order of his sheep from his memory of the order of numbers—an order he could not have obtained from the sheep themselves—so we easily find a kind of **Art** <note>The Art of Memory.</note> by which we shape things heard or seen into numbers that fit their specific type. Then, by the sequence of the numbers themselves, we can grasp the order of the thing we have perceived. This is so effective that, if people know how to count, we can very easily teach them how to remember. 

Concerning the formation of numbers throughout all things, some of our work exists in the hands of a few. The theory behind it is found in the books of the **Great Key** <note>The "Clavis Magna," likely a reference to one of the author's own specialized treatises on mnemonic systems.</note>, in the section on **semi-mathematical numbers** <term>numeris semimathematicis</term> <note>Numbers used not just for calculation, but for symbolic, philosophical, or mnemonic purposes.</note>. I believe it is enough to have shown the "place" in this section for those who are ingenious. If we have not introduced enough here, perhaps given the nature of the topic, we have expressed more than enough. For we are advised to look toward the few—who may be grateful—rather than toward everyone.

### V.

Therefore, **scrutiny** is a certain kind of **number** <note>Here, "number" implies a structured, discrete sequence or a mathematical principle of organization rather than a simple quantity.</note> by which the thinking faculty touches preserved mental images <term>species</term> in its own way. It does this according to its capacity by **Distinguishing**, **Scattering**, **Gathering**, **Applying**, **Changing**, **Forming**, **Ordering**, and **Referring** them into a **unity** <term>vnitatem</term> to be selected. 

It is indeed called a "number" because it cannot be placed more fittingly into any other category. Furthermore, such a number is this: there is nothing that can take on or possess memorability through anything other than this very principle—despite the fact that it is not known to others...

<summary>This page argues that "scrutiny" operates like a mathematical system, allowing the mind to organize memories by assigning them to a numerical order. The author references his work "The Great Key" and explains that memory is essentially the art of applying numerical structure to perceptions.</summary>

<keywords>scrutiny, thinking faculty, Art of Memory, Great Key, semi-mathematical numbers, mental images, numerical order, unity</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of how numbers provide the structural "order" for memory. It transitions from the general concept of counting to the specific philosophical distinction between "how many" (quantity) and "which one in line" (ordinality), which is the true basis of the Art of Memory.</meta>

...I might have seen; nor is it in any way defined by them. Instead, it is a principle necessarily concurring in the act of remembering. (For **places** <note>original: "loca" — the "seats" or background images used in a mnemonic system to anchor information.</note> have their power not because they are locations, nor because they are imagined, but because they possess a specific number hidden within their order.) 

We explain the logic of this through two "differences"—one which narrows down the closest **category** <term>genus</term>, and another which narrows the next, as best we can in this manner. Specifically, we speak of a number defined by **ordinality** <term>quotuitas</term> <note>A technical term for the "which-ness" or the specific position of a thing in a sequence (e.g., being the 3rd or 10th).</note>, as opposed to a number defined by **quantity** <term>quotitas</term> <note>The total sum or "how-many-ness" of a group.</note>. We would use quantity to answer someone asking: *How many sheep are there?* But we use the number defined by ordinality to answer one asking: *How many times did the sheep come to meet us?* For by this number we satisfy the person asking: *Which number in order is this sheep? Which number is that sheep?* And so, it is spoken of differently through ordinality.

Since this ordinality is understood in two ways—namely, either as it answers via "first," "second," and "third," or as it occurs (or causes things to occur) in its own order of first, second, or third—it is specified in our current proposal according to the second "closest difference," and not the first. For **ordinality** <term>quotuitas</term> is practical rather than theoretical; that is, it consists not in abstract reason but more properly in use.

This use occurs in two ways. First, it occurs with a certain focus and a specific, determined habit, as happens with those who remember through ingenuity, reason, and intelligence. These people know that *this* must be spoken of, *this* must be brought forward, and that *that* follows after this, and another follows logically after that. In these instances, **recollection** <term>reminiscentia</term> is properly said to take place, as is clear from the famous distinction between it and **memory** <term>memoria</term> <note>Following Aristotle, the author distinguishes between "memory" (the passive storage of images) and "recollection" (the active, logical effort to retrieve a specific thought).</note>. 

Second, it occurs without any apparent focus, but rather with a certain more absolute (though not truly ab- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "absoluta," meaning "absolute" or "independent."</note>...

Nor certainly does it refer to the **cogitative faculty** <term>cogitativa</term>, since that faculty deals with things that can be apprehended and known, whereas things of the kind used in our example are not in the category of the "apprehensible" or "knowable." 

What, then, is that interior power which—after those words were perceived by the ear and brought to the **common sense** as mere naked sounds—was able to thrust them into the memory? Certainly, if it is the cogitative faculty (since we do not wish to invent another internal power equal to it and located next to memory for the purpose of thrusting these things in), it is not the "naked" cogitative faculty. Rather, it is a faculty armed with **scrutiny** <note>original: "scrutinio" — a specialized, searching attention.</note>, by which it sends into the **storehouse of memory** <term>memoriae promptuarium</term> not only those things it can almost touch with its hand, but also those to which it cannot extend its hand. <meta>This page transitions from the specific problem of remembering nonsense words (discussed on page 89) to the broader mechanisms of the mind. It introduces five specific operations of the "rational soul" that allow humans to process and organize information, moving from the theory of how we think to the practice of how we investigate those thoughts.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>

From these things, it is clear that this instrument must necessarily be established; its hidden nature has blocked the path to discovery for many.

## VI.

The genus of acts pursued by **investigation** <term>scrutinium</term> <note>original: "scrutinio" — a systematic inquiry or mental examination.</note> is divided into five species: **Application**, **Formation**, **Transformation**, **Unification**, and **Ordering**. These are truly known to very few. For just as not everyone who sees and hears knows well *how* they see and hear, or what the means are by which they see and hear; so too, not everyone who applies, forms, transforms, unifies, and orders knows how they perform these things, or what the faculty is by which they perform them. 

It is well enough known in a general and somewhat vague way that these acts are produced by the **rational soul** <term>anima ratiocinante</term> <note>In the psychology of the era, the "rational soul" was the highest part of the human psyche, responsible for logic and deliberation, distinguishing humans from animals.</note>. But it is not quite clear by which specific powers, faculties, or organs they are produced. Nor, as we might wish, has anyone appeared before us who has examined these matters more deeply than certain of the **Arabs** who were well-versed in the **Peripatetic discipline** <term>Peripateticam disciplinam</term> <note>The "Peripatetic discipline" refers to the philosophy of Aristotle. Medieval Arabic scholars like Avicenna and Averroes were famous for their advanced commentaries on Aristotle's works regarding the mind and soul.</note>, by whom some aspects have been touched upon. 

But if we wished to bring all these things to light one by one, we would be undertaking a massive task and one difficult to communicate—especially since I see that in these times there are very few true philosophers (as was indicated in the introductory remarks). I pass over how much the novelty of certain names—which new discoveries and considerations require—might offend many. For this reason, **I leave the thickets to the good** <note>original: "SILVI A BONIS" — likely "silvam bonis," a proverbial expression meaning to leave the dense, "wooded" complexities of theory to others. The author is choosing to skip the dense philosophical jargon to get to the point.</note>, and also because these things are not strictly necessary for this business, which is primarily directed toward practice. Therefore, we must most especially observe what we will now systematically present regarding the **investigation** <term>scrutinium</term> and the five operations previously listed.

<summary>The author identifies five mental operations—Application, Formation, Transformation, Unification, and Ordering—as the core functions of the rational soul. He argues that while most people use these faculties, few understand the mechanics behind them, and he prioritizes practical application over dense philosophical theory.</summary>
<keywords>rational soul, five species of acts, Peripatetic discipline, investigation, mental faculties, Arabic philosophy</keywords>
