### VII.

Regarding **Application**, it must be noted that these faculties are said to maintain an analogy and order among themselves: the **external sense** <term>sensus exterior</term> <note>The five physical senses that interact with the material world.</note> is concerned with physical bodies; **Phantasia** <term>Phantasia</term> <note>In the psychology of this period, Phantasia was the faculty that stored and organized the sensory images (simulacra) received from the eyes, ears, etc.</note> is concerned with the likenesses of those bodies. **Imagination** <term>Imaginatio</term> <note>Distinguished here from Phantasia as a higher power that focuses on specific qualities or "intentions" within an image.</note> is concerned with the individual **intentions** <term>intentiones</term> <note>In medieval philosophy, an "intention" is the mental representation of a thing's specific essence or purpose.</note> of those likenesses; the **Intellect** <term>Intellectus</term>, however, is concerned with the common natures and entirely incorporeal reasons of those individual intentions.

From this analogy it follows (as we have shown elsewhere) that just as there is an art which charms, draws, and binds the external sense, so too is there an art which attracts and most firmly conquers the internal sense. Why did the birds fly toward the grapes painted by **Zeuxis**? <note>original: "Zeusim depictas" — Zeuxis was a legendary Greek painter whose work was so realistic that birds allegedly tried to eat his painted grapes.</note> Why was the **Venus** sculpted by **Praxiteles** <note>A famous ancient Greek sculptor; his Aphrodite of Knidos was so lifelike it became the subject of many legends regarding its beauty.</note> barely kept safe from the touch of its lovers? Because a certain form created by these artists applied its subject to things in such a way that it discerned them more intensely and exquisitely; moreover, it found the seats from which **species** <term>species</term> <note>In this context, "species" refers to the mental "images" or "appearances" that radiate from an object into the mind.</note>—so to speak—primarily and essentially creep into the senses. Although [as said above] this principle is common to all, it is plain enough that it is not uniform in everyone; for those whose instrument (namely the body) is more fit and better balanced, their souls are clearer.

### VIII.

A **clearer soul** <term>Anima clarior</term>, being more exposed to **Divine Ideas**, receives the forms of objects more intensely; just as one who has sharper vision discerns things more easily and aptly. For the forms in physical bodies are considered to be nothing other than images of the **Divine Ideas**; and these same forms in the senses of men...

within these internal faculties, by what better name can they be titled than **shadows of divine ideas** <term>divinarum idearum umbrae</term>; since they are as distant from the reality of natural things as natural things are distant from the truth of metaphysical things? Regarding the entrance of these species into the intellect, I would believe it happens immediately through a turning toward that **light** <note>original: "lumen" — likely referring to the "Agent Intellect," a concept in medieval philosophy describing a light that allows the human mind to process abstract thoughts.</note> which produces understanding within us, rather than through the mediation of the forms of physical things brought inside through the external senses. On the one hand, we experience it in one way, and on the other, in another way. Therefore, it is useful to embrace both ways of thinking without contradiction; how this is done we have demonstrated elsewhere; and you can determine this for yourself if you are versed in common philosophy. Truly, unless the outward projection of this "eye" through **application** <note>The act of focusing the mind's attention on an object or idea.</note> flourishes in you, how can you hope to achieve the inward projection of knowable things through it into the soul's other internal powers? For what else is it to fail to approach <note>original: "non appellere" — here meaning to fail to apply one's attention.</note> than to have closed eyes? What else is it to close one's eyes than to dwell (as they say) in the shadow of death? Has it not been passed down from the truth of things even to the common people's speech that for someone to have closed their eyes is equivalent to being dead?

## IX.

Regarding the **formation** which follows application: it must first be noted that its entire power resides in the manner and species of that application. For the **apprehensive power** <term>potentia apprehensiva</term> <note>The mental faculty responsible for taking in and perceiving information before it is fully processed by the intellect.</note> in general has this in common with **matter** <term>materia</term>: that in and of itself, it is nothing but a womb and a receptacle. Just as none of the elements <note>The four classical elements: earth, water, air, fire.</note> possess in themselves any specific odor, or taste, or heat; con-

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 92 regarding the necessity of "application" (focused attention) for the intellect. The previous page ended with a reference to common wisdom, which this page completes by using the analogy of fire and its interaction with different bodies.</meta>

...while those things are in motion, the story goes that every color, flavor, and scent emerges in various orders and degrees. **Fire**, when applied to another body, shines; moreover, depending on the variety of the bodies, it gleams in different ways, for better or for worse. Certainly, fire does not possess this quality in itself, nor does the other body possess it in itself, but both have it by virtue of their **application** <note>The act of bringing two things into contact or focusing the mind's attention.</note> to one another. Now you understand, through this analogy, that the formation of both the **intellect** <term>intellectus</term> and the **memory** <term>memoria</term> follows upon application. This formation is all the better as the preceding application was more excellent; and the greatest part of its efficacy consists in the guidance of **scrutiny** <note>The careful, methodical examination of an idea.</note>.

## X.

There are those who maintain that forms of "easy recall" <note>original: "facilis reductionis" — literally "easy reduction," referring to the ease with which an image is brought back from the memory to the conscious mind.</note> are those found within the **imaginative power** <term>virtus imaginativa</term> and the **common sense** <term>sensus communis</term> <note>In medieval psychology, the internal faculty that collects and integrates data from the five external senses.</note>. These forms, they say, possess much **physicality** <term>corporalitas</term> and little **spirituality** <term>spiritualitas</term> <note>In this context, "spirituality" refers to the degree of abstraction or refinement of a mental image, whereas "physicality" refers to how closely it mimics a heavy, material object.</note>. Conversely, they claim that forms of "difficult recall" possess much spirituality and little physicality. 

They convince themselves of this because forms of great physicality linger while the common sense distinguishes their spiritual essence from their physical weight; thus it happens that the form becomes fixed within that faculty, especially when the faculty receives it with a thin **husk** <note>original: "corticis" — meaning a thin outer layer or covering, suggesting the mind captures the surface detail easily.</note>. From this, they conversely infer that a person of **slow movement**, in whose soul the sensible things that have passed by become fixed, possesses a better memory. 

All of these ideas have a certain superficial persuasiveness, but when one uses words to explain them, they are similar to the opinions and speeches of dreamers. For they categorize the species of memory as some being fast, some slow, some timely, and some mature; others they claim are shaped like a horse...

<meta>This page continues the critique of previous theories regarding memory and physicality. While page 93 introduced the idea that mental forms might be better retained if they are "physical," this page refutes that claim, arguing that true mental action comes from spiritual qualities, not bodily bulk.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

...of Martinus, otherwise known throughout his age as Georgius, which is neither fitting for men of their gravity to say nor to believe. For whatever they may think, **physicality** <term>corporalitas</term> as physicality—or to speak more precisely, the **body** insofar as it is a body—should never be understood to perform any action. On the contrary, it must be universally asserted that action does not proceed from physicality. It is less so from a greater body, and least of all from the greatest; for a body, as a body, does not act. Every action, indeed, comes from **quality** <note>In this philosophical framework, a "quality" is a non-essential property (like heat or redness) that has the power to affect other things, unlike "matter" which is passive.</note>, and more so from that which is more spiritual than quality itself, and most of all from the **incorporeal** <term>incorporeus</term>.

Therefore, however the words of those famous men may be understood, they cannot escape a constant inconsistency. And if you wish to excuse them by saying that physical things do not act more because they are physical, but because they endure for a long time and are the **vehicles of accidents** <term>vehicula accidentium</term> <note>original: "vehicula accidentium" — In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are qualities like color or size that exist within a "substance" (the body) but are not its essential nature. The body "carries" these qualities.</note>, from which impressions are formed—which endure as long as the bodies endure—and things that endure are more firmly fixed: this excuse cannot be accepted nor upheld; it is merely vomited out in passing from their words—the opinion of a most shallow mind. 

I pass over the fact that those who are more unrefined are more sluggish, and those who are more sluggish are more unrefined. Nor does it stand in the way that we experience ourselves becoming more mindful by lingering over the consideration of one thing, rather than by a light consideration of things passing by. For we also experience, no less frequently, that we remember forever certain things heard and seen—or even considered—without any delay, while other things, seen and considered more longly and attentively, we do not retain at all. 

Therefore, the power <term>virtus</term> resides not in delay or physicality; on the contrary, it resides in the very opposite as far as the body is concerned. As for the other matter, it is not delay that creates the "fixing" <note>The process by which a memory becomes permanent in the mind.</note>, but the **activity of the form** <term>activitas formae</term>. It seems, however, that delay sometimes contributes because a certain form is not suited or inclined to act quickly, or the subject <note>The mind or the organ receiving the information.</note> is not ready to receive it quickly; therefore—

<summary>The author refutes the idea that physical mass or long duration are the causes of memory, arguing instead that mental action and retention come from spiritual "qualities" and the active power of the form itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the refutation of the idea that physical bulk aids memory. On page 94, the author argued that action comes from "quality" rather than "physicality." Here, he uses fire as an example of how spiritual intensity, rather than size, dictates power.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>

...out, by which action is completed through enduring. But where a **form** <term>forma</term> <note>In this context, a "form" is the essential nature or "blueprint" of a thing that gives it its properties.</note> is more **spiritual** <term>spiritualior</term>, it is also more active. Hence **fire** is the most active of all elements, because it is the most spiritual among them, and the most powerful at converting things into itself; and given enough matter, it alone grows infinitely by its own power. Even if it acts greatly, it does not act because it is "much" or "large"—that is, on account of a large **physicality**—but because of a more intense **quality**, which is usually preserved within that magnitude. Indeed, as some of the **Platonists** <note>Followers of Plato who believed that the physical world is a shadow of a higher, more "real" world of ideas.</note> note, if this quality could be compressed into half of that size, it would be intensified so much more that it would act twice as powerfully; if compressed into the smallest **quantity**, it would be most powerful of all; and if reduced to an indivisible point, it would be infinitely more powerful. From these things, therefore, one can see how thoughtlessly the aforementioned philosophers have spoken.

## XI.

From this it is also clear how ignorantly certain **horse doctors** <note>original: "medici equini" — Likely a derogatory reference to unrefined or "veterinary" thinkers who focus only on the physical body rather than the soul.</note> put forward that common saying: *Not quality, but quantity* <note>original: "Non qualitas, sed quantitas"</note>—even though it is useful to note this for our present purpose. For the exact opposite of their claim ought to be put forward. Be that as it may: even if a quality of such virtue were not found within a small quantity, and even if a large quality is preserved within a large quantity, nonetheless, **activity** <term>actio</term> should never be attributed to bulk, nor to anything pertaining to the nature of matter. For extension itself is ordered—even in their own judgment—toward the containment of quality and form. This can be tolerated, however, if it were said by their **Apothecaries** <note>Pharmacists or merchants of medicines.</note>, because they receive their price according to the quanti... <note>The text cuts off here as the word "quantitatem" continues on the next page.</note>

### XII.

**Unification** <term>Adunatio</term> and **transformation** <term>immutatio</term>, though they are two distinct acts, nevertheless occur simultaneously. For by transforming, a unification is made; by unifying, a transformation is made. We reveal the reasoning behind these things as follows. **Heraclitus** <note>A Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher famous for the idea that "everything flows" and for his cryptic style.</note> said that if all existing things were to become smoke, the nostrils would discern them all. Let us speak more crudely: if all things except the Fox were turned into chickens, the fox would not go hungry in vain; all things would be devourable by the fox. Among those powers capable of converting all things in their own way, one is the human **imagination** <term>phantasia</term>; among those powers capable of devouring and tasting all things in their own way, one is the human **cogitative faculty** <term>cogitatiua</term> <note>In the psychology of this period, the "cogitative" faculty was the "internal sense" that organized and judged the images gathered by the imagination.</note>. The imagination will be able to achieve this kind of transformation (not without an act of thought), so that it may powerfully render all things memorable, not without the act of the cogitative faculty. But you will say that it is necessary for the imagination to convert all things, or...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion from page 96 regarding the "Unification" and "Transformation" of mental images. The author is exploring how the mind (symbolized by a "wolf") can process diverse information into a single, memorable system without losing the essential details of the original subjects.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>

into one **form** <term>speciem</term> <note>In this context, a "species" refers to a mental image or a specific category of thing.</note>, or into many: if it converts and reforms them into one, there will no longer be a **memory** <term>memoria</term> of many things, but of one. Indeed, if everything were converted into a sheep, then the **wolf** <note>The author uses the "wolf" as a metaphor for the intellect or a mnemonic system that "devours" and transforms information.</note> would not increase the variety of edible things; it could then be said that nothing but that one thing is edible to it. 

If it converts and reforms them into many forms: these are either finite or infinite. If they are finite, one must either know and determine them, or they are indeterminate, and thus we would remain exactly where we are. To attempt infinite things, however, is foolish. 

One must therefore understand that this conversion should not be done in such a way that **substantial diversity** <term>substantialis diuersitas</term> <note>The essential, core differences that make one object distinct from another.</note> is destroyed; nor should the **accidents** <term>accidentia</term> <note>Non-essential qualities, such as color, size, or location, that belong to an individual thing.</note> proper to each thing be removed. Instead, it should be done so that all these diverse things, by having a single **form** <term>forma</term> <note>The organizing principle or "mental container" used to structure the memory.</note> applied to them as it arrives, are affected in their own way. In this way, they take on a single character for the memory, derived from one and through one. 

Thus, that wolf will swallow all things as if they were one, provided that all **substances** <term>substantiæ</term> and the qualities that immediately follow them take on the "garments" of a single class of accidents.

<keywords>mnemonics, mental forms, substance and accidents, wolf metaphor, cryptography, cipher disk, alphabet, logic</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument for a unified mnemonic system. Having established that the intellect must organize diversity without destroying it, the author now uses the metaphor of music and a center point to explain how a single "spirit" or "vowel" can harmonize many different elements into a memorable whole.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>

Just as countless elements can be related to the same vowel at the center of a circle; (without taking away the fact that each of them retains its own diversity) they all take on the character of a single sound. Indeed, it is so far from the truth that by the application of a vowel they shut out their own essential diversity from others, that they are better able to communicate the same things through it. So too, when several organs are applied to one **spirit** <note>original: "vni spiritui"; in this context, it refers to the breath or the animating force of the mind.</note>, diverse voices are produced according to their own nature. Why then, losing hope, do you not prepare yourself, and as it were gird yourself to acquire one of the countless possible **forms** <term>speciem</term> <note>original: "speciem". A mental image or category used as a "container" for memory.</note> for yourself; to be situated at the boundary of the **imagination** <term>phantasiæ</term> and the **thought-faculty** <term>cogitatiuæ</term> <note>The "cogitative faculty" was believed by medieval and Renaissance thinkers to be the part of the brain that connects physical sensations to higher intellectual thought.</note>? Through this, might not all things take on a "memorability" in the soul, just as they take on a "visibility" in a book? This we leave to your diligence, O ingenious one, whoever you may be. Consider what the method of invention of **Pan, the God of Arcadia** <note>In Greek mythology, Pan created the panpipes (syrinx) by joining reeds of different lengths together. The author uses this as a metaphor for a memory system that joins different ideas into one "instrument."</note>, achieved when he first joined together the rustic reeds: these things, which were previously produced by different talents and were not once easily able to harmonize; now, by a single breath of the same spirit sent into those reeds joined together as if into one, the dispersion is removed and a union is achieved, which one person performs so conveniently.

### XIII.

Regarding that which pertains to **organization** <term>ordinationem</term>, there are common propositions depending on this (though they are less relevant and less related to the matter), which can be better clarified through use by considering those things that exist through our work. Primarily, therefore, **recollection** <term>reminiscentia</term> <note>original: "reminiscentia". This refers to the active, logical process of hunting for a lost memory, distinguishing it from spontaneous remembering.</note> is said to occur when this motion follows that motion by necessity,

<summary>The author uses the metaphors of a central vowel and Pan’s pipes to explain how the mind can unify diverse information into a single harmonious memory system. He introduces the concept of "reminiscence" as a chain of necessary mental movements.</summary>

<keywords>Pan's pipes, harmony, mnemonic organization, recollection, imagination, cogitative faculty, mental unity</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general metaphor of Pan’s pipes and unified diversity to the specific mechanics of mental association. The author is moving from the "why" of mnemonic systems to the "how," specifically focusing on the chain of associations that allows the mind to navigate from one idea to the next.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>

...or this motion accompanies that one: whether it be in terms of place, time, reason, nature, art, or any other positive accompaniment and succession of one thing after another. In this very manner, we proceed from the memory of snow to the memory of winter; from there to the memory of cold; from there to that of **antiperistasis** <term>antiperistasis</term> <note>original: "antiperistasis"; a philosophical concept where a quality is intensified by being surrounded by its opposite, such as internal body heat increasing when the outside air is cold.</note>; from there to the intensification of heat in the stomach; then to that of powerful digestion; from this to appetite and more lively nourishment, strength, and exercise; and so on in this fashion. 

It is similar in all other things. If anything has a nature that lacks order, let it be referred to and lean upon something else that *is* ordered. This ordered thing must always be something **sensible** <term>sensibile</term> <note>original: "sensibile"; in this context, something that can be perceived by the physical senses (like an image or a sound) rather than a purely abstract thought.</note>. For this reason, it is not said rashly by the reasoning philosopher that **order** <term>ordo</term> is the inherent nature of sensible things, and he does not recognize it outside the boundaries of **nature** <term>natura</term>. 

Hence, if you ask him, "What is order?" he says, "It is the progress of a thing according to the way of nature." "What is the lack of order?" He says, "It is a departure from the way of nature." 

These are the things we wished to be said concerning the **instrument** <term>organum</term> <note>original: "organum"; the author uses this to mean a "logical tool" or "method," referring to the mnemonic system itself.</note> and the investigation pertaining to that instrument. If you contemplate these things quite attentively, there is nothing that can prevent you from progressing further; for by these means, you are removed from that which made the path toward subsequent actions closed to you. And certainly, we could not fail to provide, under the heading of **theory** <term>theorice</term>, those things which are able by themselves to complete the **practice** <term>praxis</term>.

<summary>This page explains how the mind uses chains of natural associations—such as snow leading to the thought of winter—to organize memory. The author argues that an effective mnemonic system must be based on "sensible" (perceivable) things, as order itself is a fundamental property of nature.</summary>
<keywords>association, order, nature, antiperistasis, theory and practice, sensible things, mnemonic method</keywords>

# THIRD PART

Come now, let us proceed to introduce the **form of operation** <term>operandi formam</term> <note>The practical method or "how-to" of the memory system.</note>. Acting as if by way of preludes, let us descend from the knowledge of simple **elements** <term>elementorum</term> to the consideration of the whole and perfect composite.

First, therefore—following the likeness of those who teach the hand to move across the page before the eyes are even cast upon the inscribed characters—we propose that the **subjects** <term>subiecta</term> <note>original: "subiecta"; in mnemonic systems, these are the mental locations or "rooms" (loci) where information is stored.</note> whose **memory** <term>memoria</term> has been previously established must be prepared beforehand, so that their power and efficacy may be revealed in the following manner. Let a "common subject" be taken, and let it first be distributed into its major parts, which according to the practitioner's design ought to follow one another in sequence. These major parts were referred to above as "more proper subjects." From here, let us proceed to **subdivision** <term>subdiuisionem</term>; so that these parts—perceived as being bounded by one another and succeeding one another in order—may define for themselves other parts in a state of true or positive contiguity. These are the "most proper and individual subjects." These must be multiplied according to the scale of the breadth of the "more proper subjects" and the convenience of the available seats to be added.

## II.

Once these things have been arranged and held readily in the memory, let there be a **catalogue of diverse sensible things** <term>cathalogus rerum sensibilium</term> <note>original: "cathalogus rerum sensibilium"; a list of physical, perceivable objects used as memory images to be placed within the mental locations.</note>, which in the first group are twenty-five in number, then fi— <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the practical method of mnemonics, explaining how to divide a general mental space into specific, sequential "subjects" or locations. It begins to describe a catalogue of twenty-five physical objects used as memory markers.</summary>
<keywords>form of operation, mnemonic subjects, mental loci, subdivision, sensible things, memory technique, sequence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for building a "memory palace." The author is transitioning from the architecture of memory to the data stored within it.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>

... fifty, then soon a hundred: so that step by step, through the benefit of **practice** <term>exercitatio</term>, the desired discipline may be acquired. Together with this, it will be clearly seen how those things whose order is suggested by **natural memory** <term>memoria naturalis</term>—by virtue of the **subjects** <term>subiecta</term> <note>original: "subiectorum"; these are the mental "loci" or fixed locations used to anchor memories.</note> among which they are distributed and applied—will occur to you in an orderly fashion, no less than if they were written on a page. Thus, you may proceed from the last to the first with the same ease as you approached from the first to the last. Likewise, one may invert the order of things to be recalled however one wishes, with the **perceptibility of the places** <term>locorum sensibilitate</term> <note>This refers to how vivid and distinct the mental locations appear to the practitioner's "inner eye."</note> contributing to this.

### III.

Therefore, having seen the efficacy of the subjects, let us pass to the reasoning of the **added things** <term>adiectorum</term> <note>original: "adiectorum"; in the Art of Memory, these are the "images" or "markers" placed within the mental locations to represent specific information.</note> upon which natural memory relies, as if upon a foundation that is neither light nor fragile. There exists, therefore, memory of two kinds: namely, of **words** <term>terminorum</term> <note>original: "terminorum"; often referred to in classical rhetoric as *memoria verborum*, or word-for-word memorization.</note> and of **things** <term>rerum</term> <note>original: "rerum"; known as *memoria rerum*, or memorizing the general ideas and sequence of topics.</note>. 

Of these, the latter (the memory of things) simply admits of necessity <note>Meaning it is obviously essential for any speaker or thinker.</note>. The former (the memory of words), however, is necessary in a certain way: either because it is believed that the **memorative faculty** <term>memoratiua facultas</term> is sharpened by it for the execution of its own function—for having endured heavier burdens, we are accustomed not to feel lesser difficulty and hardship—or because the habit of some faculty requires the expression of initial words when the occurrence of a purpose or a sentence arises; or because it happens that things obtain a single and proper name that is not sufficiently at hand (such as the names of herbs, trees, minerals, seeds, and others of that kind, for which it is not enough to merely have the concept); or finally because the occasion often presents itself for uttering words for which we have very little understanding.

<summary>The text concludes the instructions on organizing mental "subjects" (locations) and introduces the two types of memory: "memory of words" and "memory of things." The author argues that memorizing specific words, though difficult, sharpens the mind and is necessary for technical subjects like botany or mineralogy.</summary>

### IV.

Concerning the kinds of categories by which the objects themselves are added, explained, and introduce themselves to one another: we shall declare this elsewhere. However, regarding the nature of this faculty <note>The "faculty" refers to the art of memory itself.</note>, the path is open enough for you from what has been said before; moreover, you have the best teacher and guide in both internal and external nature, which always enlightens you through innate reason and the objects present before you.

### V.

Also, many and countless modes <note>original: "modi"; the specific techniques used to attach information to a mental location.</note> by which terms are added will be able to occur to you, provided you are skilled in the operation of the **Great Key** <term>Clavis magna</term> <note>The "Great Key" likely refers to the author's specific mnemonic system or a combinatorial device, similar to the logic wheels of Ramon Llull.</note> (for that indeed is the source of all inventions <term>inventionum</term> <note>In Renaissance rhetoric, "invention" is the process of finding or "discovering" ideas and arguments.</note>). Those modes which seemed more convenient to us, we explain in various places throughout this work. The single method which the ancients used up until our own times we utterly disregard, because it is laborious and requires much practice, and it is not a certain achievement for everyone.

### VI.

We have therefore touched upon that breadth of internal reading <note>original: "intrinsecae lectionis"; this refers to the act of "reading" or visualizing information stored within the mind's memory places.</note> and have compressed its length; for since those things scattered and distracted the eye of the imagination <term>phantasiae</term>, a greater difficulty was caused, which—by the difficulty of the art and the long duration of practice—used to repel talented minds intent on more worthy business. For what? Through the individual subject <note>The sentence ends mid-thought and continues on the next page.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of ancient mnemonic systems. <page-num>104</page-num>

To a single added thing <note>original: "adiectum"; in this context, it refers to the mnemonic image or symbol attached to a mental location.</note> it was permitted to commit only a single element (as is the case in most instances), especially since there are countless variations of combinations and compositions. For this reason, the one who first appears to have transferred this art from the Greeks to the Latins <note>This likely refers to the anonymous author of the *Rhetorica ad Herennium* or perhaps Cicero, both of whom were instrumental in adapting Greek "art of memory" techniques for Roman orators.</note> ridicules the zeal of certain Greeks who wanted to compose **images for words** <term>imagines verborum</term> <note>A technique where a unique mental image is created for every single word in a text, rather than just for the main ideas or "things."</note> and prepare them for themselves in such a way that, when they were needed during an inquiry, no time would be wasted. 

For he saw that the multitude of words was infinite, and therefore it was ridiculous to want to pursue them all. Yet, to us, it is found to be not only possible but even easy to have prepared images by which I may apply whole terms of any kind to each individual location <note>original: "locis"; the mental "seats" or backgrounds where mnemonic images are placed.</note>. This can be done in several ways, as we demonstrate in various treatises organized for different purposes; for the present moment, however, let it be proposed through the method which follows:

## The First Practice, which is of Words.
### On the Fixing of the Wheels.

->I.<-

To prepare an orderly exercise: first, let there be a ready grasp of the elements—I mean the elements that can be explained by those additions <note>original: "adiectibilibus"; likely referring to the mnemonic attributes or symbols attached to letters in this system.</note> which are most suited for producing all actions and for receiving all passions <note>In the philosophical language of the time, "actions" and "passions" represent the two primary ways things exist or interact: either doing something or having something done to them.</note>.

->II.<-

Therefore, from these, let thirty [images] that stick most firmly in your knowledge be chosen to represent the thirty elements. These make up the full number of those elements which serve the different pronunciations in three languages <term>idiomatibus</term> <note>Specifically Latin, Greek, and Hebrew, the three primary languages of scholarship in the Renaissance.</note>. For it is not necessary to set up a triple alphabet <term>elementarium</term> <note>A foundational set of symbols.</note>, since the letter **A** itself is equivalent to **alpha** <note>original: "α"</note> and **aleph** <note>original: "א"</note>; **B** is equivalent to **beta** <note>original: "β"</note> and **bet** <note>original: "ב"</note>; and many others are similarly equivalent to one another. However, where there are Greek elements beyond those of our own kind <note>That is, sounds or letters not found in the standard Latin alphabet.</note>, such as **psi**, **omega**, and **theta** <note>original: "ψ ω θ"</note>, and further still the Hebrew ones, they are marked by their own specific characters. In this way, one simple alphabet serves three languages and those subordinate to them <note>The author suggests that his 30-element system is universal enough to encode any language derived from these three sources.</note>.

<summary>The author introduces a universal mnemonic alphabet of thirty elements designed to represent sounds across Latin, Greek, and Hebrew. This system uses "wheels" to organize these elements into a functional practice for memory and language.</summary>
<keywords>alphabet, mnemonic wheels, thirty elements, Latin, Greek, Hebrew, linguistics, combinatorial art</keywords>

Therefore, let thirty [images] present themselves to your imagination <term>phantasiæ</term> <note>In Renaissance psychology, the "phantasia" was the faculty of the mind that processed and stored sensory images.</note> with as much readiness and as little delay as possible. <page-num>110</page-num>

## V.

Once these steps have been successfully completed, one may proceed to the further stage of the operation. It is helpful, I suggest, to assign to each person <note>The "Agents" or characters like Jason or Apollo used in the memory system.</note> and to that person’s action a specific instrument or attribute <note>original: "insigne"; a distinguishing mark or tool that helps identify the character.</note>. This should not be something that refers only to that specific action, but rather something that is as adaptable as possible to all others—or at least compatible with all the tasks to be performed. For these wheels are not illustrated as fixed so that they remain static forever; rather, they are meant to be fixed in our memory through the addition of a single attribute. In this way, when they move back and forth <note>The author refers to the physical rotation of the paper wheels (volvelles) and the mental shifting of concepts.</note>, they can always and immediately be linked to the specific person (as will be shown later) for whom they are currently designated.

Thus, <term>Lycas</term> <note>The servant of Hercules.</note> has a <term>chain</term> <note>original: "cathenam".</note>, <term>Deucalion</term> <note>The survivor of the Great Flood in Greek myth.</note> a <term>headband</term> <note>original: "vittam"; a ritual ribbon or fillet.</note>, <term>Apollo</term> a <term>belt</term>, <term>Argus</term> <note>The hundred-eyed giant.</note> a <term>hood</term>, <term>Arcas</term> <note>The son of Callisto, associated with the constellation Ursa Minor.</note> a <term>pouch</term>, <term>Cadmus</term> <note>The founder of Thebes who sowed the dragon's teeth.</note> an <term>apron</term> <note>original: "antesinale"; a protective garment worn in front.</note>, <term>Semele</term> <note>The mother of Dionysus.</note> a <term>seat placed beneath her</term>, and so on for the others. Although these items are specific to each individual, they are nevertheless adaptable to all roles as needed—a point that requires careful attention. For the logic and advancement of the final <term>practice</term> <note>original: "praxis"; the actual application of the memory system.</note> should take precedence over the logic of the initial practice.

These "insignia" allow the user to quickly link different actions and people within the mechanical memory system.</summary>

Let the instruction, arrangement, and judgment regarding other figures be handled in a similar manner. However, when it happens that a tool or instrument must be added to signify a third element (even if this presents itself as less convenient, for it often happens that a specific or accidental action does not allow for such an object to be held in the hand), let that object be imagined as added, tied, or placed in some way for the sake of convenience. It might **Disturb** the action, **Assist** it, or be **Thrown away**, **Overturned**, **Removed**, **Extricated**, **Emptied**, **Collapse**, **Intervene**, or be positioned in whatever other way fits the requirements of the work. Nevertheless, it is incomparably more suitable to use <term>insignia</term> <note>Distinctive symbolic attributes or "badges" that identify a character, like a saint’s tool or a god’s weapon.</note>, since such attributes can be applied to anyone without any difficulty.

### VI.

I have decided to leave it entirely to your own diligence to find appropriate actions, tools, or symbolic attributes. Just as the images of certain famous men are better known and more celebrated by individuals, so too (since "each man is led by his own pleasure" <note>A paraphrase of Virgil’s Eclogues: "trahit sua quemque voluptas."</note>) each person has certain works, instruments, and symbols by which they are more easily moved...

### *On the movement of the wheels.*

->I.<-

Therefore, once the previously designated wheels are firmly fixed in your mind, and you hold ready—without change—whatever belongs to each part, it is time for you to be guided toward a greater practical application in the following way. First, you will come to the primary composition which consists of any two elements. You see the first figure <note>A "figure" here refers to a mnemonic diagram or a physical device called a "volvelle," consisting of moving paper circles.</note> consisting of two fixed wheels. Now, while the outer wheel remains fixed in its position, let the inner wheel be released. Previously, its fixed state was related to the disposition <note>Original: *habitum*.</note> of that which ought to stay stationary; now, however, for the sake of an action which—due to the many workable items and the countless things to be memorized—must be infinitely varied, it presents itself as something to be revolved in every direction.

->*On the movement of the inner wheel of the first figure for*<-
->*presenting any composition made of two elements.*<-

->II.<-

Let the operation, I say, seek the unique properties of one thing, and then let it be made communicable to all others within the circle. Consequently, for whatever composition <note>The text ends mid-word with "compo-", likely continuing as "compositione" on the next page.</note> must be established according to the needs of...

<meta>This page continues the explanation of how to rotate the concentric mnemonic wheels to create new combinations of ideas. It transitions from simple two-letter combinations (Person + Action) to more complex three-letter combinations (Person + Action + Attribute).</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>

...adapted for its position. Lycaon at the banquet <note>Lycaon is the mythological figure assigned to the letter A.</note> used to return to you the doubled element **AA**, while the **A** of the inner wheel was positioned under the **A** of the outer wheel; likewise Deucalion [turning] into stones was **BB**. 

Now, as the wheel revolves, you will have not a twin element, but a double one: namely, when **B** of the lower wheel is moved under **A** of the upper wheel. Now you no longer have **AA** (Lycaon at the banquet), but rather Lycaon [performing the action of] turning into stones <note>By rotating the inner wheel, the Person (Lycaon) is now paired with the Action belonging to the next letter, which is Deucalion's action of turning to stone.</note>. Deucalion [performs the action of] killing Python, Apollo [performs the action of] guarding the ox, and so on; each person takes on the action of their neighbor as new elements follow one another. 

Do you wish, then, to represent the combination **PA**? Place the **A** of the inner wheel under the **P** of the outer wheel; they will present to you Plato at the banquet <note>In Bruno’s mnemonic system, P corresponds to Plato and A corresponds to the banquet scene.</note>. Do you wish for the combination **RE**? Place the **E** of the inner wheel under the **R** of the outer wheel; it will be presented to you as Neptune <note>R = Neptune</note> stabbing Callisto <note>E = the action/scene of Callisto being stabbed.</note>. Similarly, the combination **SI** is shown through Pallas <note>S = Pallas (Athena)</note> with the boy Bacchus <note>I = the scene involving Bacchus as a child.</note>. **VO** is shown through Medea <note>V = Medea</note> kidnapping Proserpina <note>O = the scene of the Rape of Proserpina.</note>. You have, therefore, the method for proceeding to represent any composition of two elements to the senses.

Likewise, in the second figure—once the two inner wheels are released and ready for rotation <note>Original: *ad vertiginem*. Thus, where in three fixed wheels "Lycas at the banquet in chains" <note>This is a three-part mnemonic image: Lycas (the Person), at the Banquet (the Action/Scene), in Chains (the Attribute or Object). Each part corresponds to a letter on one of the three wheels.</note> represented **AAA** to you, now Lycas doing what Medusa [does]...

### *On how to represent any composition of four elements through a single method.*

#### IIII.

If, however, it happens that a compound of four elements must be added, it is not necessary to supply a fourth wheel. For it does not happen to all elements, but only to a few, that they occupy a fourth place in a combination: such as **S**, because the combination <term>MENS</term> <note>Latin: "Mind." Here used as the letter sequence M-E-N-S.</note> has a fourth [letter], and **T**, because the combination <term>DANT</term> <note>Latin: "They give." Here used as the letter sequence D-A-N-T.</note> has a fourth. Therefore, to designate the presence of this fourth element, what need is there for another physical track? It is sufficient to imagine some specific <term>accident</term> <note>In this context, a "secondary characteristic" or detail added to the mental image.</note> or attendant standing by with a certain relationship to the subject or the attribute; one of these would indicate the fourth element, while the other indicates something else.

### *On another way of presenting a composition of three elements, likewise in another ordinal manner.*

## **V.**

There is also another method by which a fourth element <note>In this system, an "element" usually refers to a single letter of a word being memorized.</note> is added to the third; namely, using the letters **L, R, and N** as intermediaries between the "nearby" element and the "underlying" element. You can see this in the first combination of the compound word **TRVNCVS** <note>Latin: "Trunk" or "Torso."</note>, in the second combination of the compound **INCRASSATVS** <note>Latin: "Thickened" or "Fattened."</note>, and in the third of the compound **PERMAGNVS** <note>Latin: "Very large."</note>. To denote the presence and position of these letters, you can assign certain "ways of being"—sensible physical characteristics—to the subject, or with the subject, or toward the subject. For myself, I found it useful to add a "logical attribute" where the person in the mental image would signify the third position by **sitting**, the second by **leaning**, or the first by **standing**. If other letters besides these should present themselves—which can hardly happen in the languages of the <term>Latins</term>, <term>Greeks</term>, <term>Hebrews</term>, <term>Chaldeans</term>, <term>Persians</term>, <term>Italians</term>, <term>Arabs</term>, and <term>Spaniards</term>—you may provide for those others using this same light by which you see we have assisted the three letters designated above.

As for the <term>French</term> (as for the <term>Germans</term>, <term>Goths</term>, <term>Scythians</term> <note>A nomadic people of Central Asia, often used here as a catch-all for "barbaric" northern or eastern peoples.</note>, and others of that sort, let them look to it themselves), who—not out of a lack of refinement in their language, but by some habit or custom—allow certain "silent" letters to be included to show some distinction: this is not something that should trouble you. The spelling remains complete enough for the necessity of expressing the thing even without those extra additions. Hence, there are those among them of no small talent—

<summary>The author explains how to encode "mediating" letters (L, R, N) by changing the posture of the person in the memory image (standing, leaning, or sitting). He also dismisses the need to encode silent letters found in languages like French.</summary>

->*On presenting any composition made of five elements.*<-

## VI.

For a compound of five elements <note>In this system, an "element" refers to an individual letter within a syllable or word being memorized.</note>, there is no need for us to introduce anything new. Instead, we should use or—to speak more plainly—"co-use" the methods already proposed. Indeed, it cannot happen otherwise than by the two modes just mentioned occurring together. For example, by adding **s** and **t** as the fourth element, and the mediating **L, R,** and **N** as the fifth: as in the combination **PLEBS** <note>Latin: "The common people."</note>, in the first combination of **TRANSACTVM** <note>Latin: "Transacted" or "Finished."</note>, and in the last combination of **STVPRANS** <note>Latin: "Defiling" or "Ravishing."</note>.

## VII.

---

->*On any composition made of many elements.*<-

As for one or two particular cases, and those hardly common: why should we trouble ourselves if they are compacted of even more elements? These very cases, with a minimal condition added, can be conveniently introduced to one already presenting a composition of four or five. Truly, I do not know if there are many more such examples besides this single one: **SCROBS** <note>Latin: "Ditch" or "Grave." This is a rare example of a single-syllable word with six letters.</note>; however, the memory of it can be made as a compound of five, **SCROPS**, because of the similarity of the sound.

<meta>This page transitions from the specific rules of spelling and letter-counts in mnemonic notation (the "elements") to the broader management of memory "subjects" (loci) and "additions" (images). <page-num>118</page-num>

## VIII.

### *About the letter U after Q.*

I do not wish to pass over this point in silence, as it should be noted regarding internal writing <note>original: "scriptura"; the author refers to the way words are mentally represented or encoded in a memory system</note> (though it is not strictly necessary for our purpose, but should be placed among the useful things). The **u** following a **q** should not be counted in the total number [of letters]. For **q** by itself has as much value as it does when paired with the **u**. Indeed, it is never accustomed to occur separately from it. It is so much the case that **qu** clearly possesses the power of only a single element <term>element <gloss>the basic unit of a word, such as a letter or a single sound</gloss></term>. 

Therefore, in a situation where it would be profitable to "sell" <note>The author uses a commercial metaphor: "selling" elements implies a situation where one is rewarded for complexity or length</note> the elements as they occur, it would be beneficial to write: **QUINTUS, QUINTUS, WHY, SQUARE, FIFTH, HOW MANY?** <note>original: "QVINTE QVINTE QVARE QVADRVM QVINTVM, QVATIS?" — examples of Latin words beginning with the 'qu' sound.</note> However, where it is necessary to "buy" <note>Following the metaphor: "buying" implies a need for economy, brevity, or saving space in one's mental "ledger"</note> them, it would do no harm to write: **Qare qinte qatis qadrum qintum?** <note>The author demonstrates that the 'u' is redundant and can be omitted without losing the meaning of the words.</note> Thus, those who are used to using redundant letters could perform a better service for foreigners in their own country who study the language in vain, doing so without any loss of integrity or damage to refined culture.

## IX.

### *To expand the internal field of subjects and multiply the number of additions most successfully.*

Once you have worked more efficiently by using a few additions <note>original: "adiectis"; in mnemonic systems, "additions" or "adjuncts" are the images or markers one attaches to a memory location</note> and more strictly with the number of subjects <note>original: "subiectorum"; "subjects" refers to the "loci" or the mental places used to store information</note>, I will add nothing more regarding the multiplication of subjects, except for what pertains to the quality of the things to be multiplied. Consider, I say, which of these retain additions more tenaciously, and which retain them more slowly...

Regarding the multiplication of additions <note>original: "adiectorum"; in Bruno's system, these are the images or "adjuncts" attached to memory locations</note>, you will find a significant opportunity within three days; for by observing this principle from which it flows, you will be able to provide yourself with a useful practice in other matters: for from proportional things we are able to turn to finding other proportional things.

You used to have thirty very lively additions <note>likely representing the 23 letters of the Latin alphabet plus Greek or additional characters used in Bruno's wheel system</note> for the number of elements, which could serve you in brief writing; now it is helpful to expand the page immensely (if there is a need): to multiply the addition of words into an infinite number. Indeed, it turns out poorly if the same additions of the same form are repeated too often, since internal writing <note>original: "internam scripturam"; the process of encoding information into the memory through mental images</note> requires a variety that is not required for external writing <note>writing on paper</note>, as is clear enough to those practicing this method. What then? Just as you had one **Lycaon**, one **Deucalion** <note>In Greek myth, Deucalion and his wife Pyrrha repopulated the world by throwing stones behind them which turned into people; Lycaon was a king turned into a wolf</note>, etc., in number, now imagine for yourself two **Lycaons** and two **Deucalions** and others, so that where thirty were available to you, sixty now come into use. If you triple them, you will have ninety; if you quadruple them, one hundred and twenty.

Force all those whose images you know best to strike your imagination <term>imagination <gloss>referred to as "phantasia," the mental faculty that stores and shapes sensory images for the memory</gloss></term> into the count of thirty root names. For the fact that they have another proper name of their own is no obstacle. For **Philotheus** <note>Philotheus, meaning "Lover of God," is a common pseudonym for Giordano Bruno himself in his dialogues</note> will remain perpetually dyed with the character of Deucalion's name if he is once numbered and enrolled among those "throwing stones" <note>This refers to the aforementioned myth of Deucalion; Bruno is suggesting that any image (even a friend like Philotheus) can be "encoded" as a version of a standard mnemonic agent like Deucalion</note>. How famous this discovery is, will be better [known] through use and...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for expanding a mnemonic system. Having established a basic "alphabet" of 30 images on the previous page, the author now explains how to multiply these into a more complex "combinatory" system by groups of five, increasing the capacity from 30 to 150 mental images.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

...application to other things than those you can comprehend through the surface itself.

->*The second practice, which concerns simple terms, for presenting any composition derived from many combinations of elements.*<-

### I.

Indeed, this is the "great practice" <note>original: "praxis magna"</note> toward which the first practice, once matured, hastens. Where that initial practice granted you the combination of elements <note>the basic building blocks, usually letters or primary symbols</note>, this one—for the sake of explaining complete terms—passes on the composition of those combinations. Thus, for a given number of subjects <note>original: "subiectorum"; the "places" or "loci" in a memory palace</note>, you will be able to attach a number of perfected adjuncts <note>original: "adiectorum"; the mental images or "agents" placed within the memory locations</note> (which are called simple and non-complex terms), and soon you will grasp their total number.

### II.

This is achieved in the following way. Just as you established thirty active agents, actions, insignia, circumstances, and attendants for the number of thirty elements: so now you should arrange one hundred and fifty in an orderly fashion. This is done by leading each of the "attendant" elements through a group of five <note>original: "quinarium"</note> "subsistent" elements. Therefore, let it be understood that we have taught the combinatory system <note>original: "combinatorium"</note> under the same conditions by which we taught the establishment of the elementary system.

<term>praxis</term> (practice or method), <term>combinationem</term> (the act of combining symbols), <term>elementorum</term> (of the elements or base symbols), <term>termini simplices</term> (simple terms), <term>termini incomplexi</term> (non-complex terms), <term>quinarium</term> (a set or grouping of five), <term>combinatorium</term> (the combinatory art or system)

<summary>This page explains the transition from a basic mnemonic alphabet to a "great practice" where images are multiplied. By combining the 30 original elements in groups of five, the practitioner expands their system to 150 complex memory agents.</summary>
<keywords>memory palace, combinatory art, mnemonic agents, elements, adjuncts, group of five, mental images</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for expanding a mnemonic system. Having established a basic "alphabet" of 30 images on the previous page, the author now explains how to multiply these into a more complex "combinatory" system by groups of five, increasing the capacity from 30 to 150 mental images.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<sig>16</sig>

## III.

Through what we have said regarding the expansion of representation <note>original: "graphicæ dilatationem"; the broadening of the visual or symbolic system</note>: most famous names have been brought forth; insofar as it contributes greatly to the variety necessary in this art, and those certain principal and more celebrated names which are to be added by you, you may bring back like centuries <note>original: "centurias"; units of 100 in the Roman military, but here referring to organized groups of names or images</note> under the shadow and wing of thirty banners <note>original: "vexillorum"; standards or flags used as mental markers or headings in a memory palace</note>. Now, for the most extensive operation, as each of the proposed banners is led through a group of five <note>original: "quinarium"</note> of the five subsistent elements <note>the basic building blocks of the system</note>, let us place five other banners beneath them.

## IIII.

Thus, those figures better known to you should be assigned to this number of banners, in such a way that each one occupies that seat which, according to their quality, seems better suited to them. Regarding the banners which are more accustomed to frequent military service <note>referring to symbols or images used more often in the memory practice</note>; let them take larger forces for themselves: for among them are those which are content with only one or two soldiers <note>individual images or data points</note>. Therefore, one must act justly with all according to an equality of proportion, and not merely an equality of number <note>meaning the "weight" or complexity of the image should match its importance, rather than just filling slots</note>.

## V.

You yourself will therefore arrange one hundred and fifty names for yourself, which either from their own naming, or from a customary action, or a specific affection <note>an emotional state or quality associated with the image</note>, according to the order...

<summary>This page explains how to expand a mnemonic system from 30 to 150 items using military metaphors like banners and centuries. It emphasizes organizing these mental images based on their "quality" and "proportion" rather than just filling a list numerically.</summary>

<keywords>mnemonic system, banners, mental images, proportion, organization, military metaphors, centuries, memory art</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing a complex mnemonic system. Following the previous page’s discussion on organizing names into "centuries" under "banners," the author now explains how to map these onto a physical or mental apparatus of five wheels to create a vast number of memory combinations.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>

...whereby the assistants are arranged; and they are formed in an orderly way by the five subsistent elements <note>original: "subsistentibus"; the fundamental underlying components that "stand beneath" or support the memory system</note>. This being done, you should organize these or other subsisting parts and arts (if you have more suitable ones) so that they might be attached to these through actions and into nameable arts. You may do this by reducing them to order, either by placing names known to you in the column opposite the following order; or otherwise, as it may be more convenient for you, by arranging those provided by us, using others to succeed in the place of some, and thus bringing an uncertain series into order. In this way, in relation to the same or different circumstances, they may receive the same or different insignias <term>insignias: visual symbols or attributes, such as a wheel or a lightning bolt, that identify a person or concept</term> and assistants <note>original: "adsistentia"; secondary figures or objects that help define the primary image</note>.

## VI.

Therefore, you shall construct, in the likeness of the three aforementioned wheels: five fixed wheels, each of which consists of one hundred and fifty combinations of two elements. 

The outer and first of these signifies the **agents** under the name of the **inventors** <term>inventors: historical or mythological figures credited with "inventing" certain things, used here as the primary actors in a memory scene</term>. 

The second signifies **actions**. 

The third, **insignias**. 

The fourth, **bystanders** <note>original: "adstantia"; things or figures standing nearby to provide further detail</note>. 

The fifth, **circumstances** <term>circumstances: the specific settings, locations, or conditions that contextualize the memory image</term>. 

These are introduced in such a way that those things which do not serve a specific, proper purpose in one instance may provide help through another universal logic.

<summary>The author describes how to organize a mnemonic system into five "wheels" of 150 combinations each, categorizing images by agents (inventors), actions, insignias, bystanders, and circumstances.</summary>
<keywords>mnemonic wheels, inventors, memory palace, combinations, agents, actions, insignias, circumstances, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the mnemonic apparatus introduced on page 122. Having defined the five categories (Agents, Actions, Insignias, Bystanders, and Assistants), the author now explains the visual and geometric layout of the wheels used to calculate these combinations.</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>

Since it is difficult to compress five wheels <term>wheels: also known as "volvells," these are paper instruments with rotating parts used in the Renaissance for calculation and memory</term> into a modest width, we are providing only one here, after the likeness of which the others are made; and this one is shown not in an expanded form, but in a contracted one. 

Specifically, thirty principal banners <term>banners: major thematic categories or "flags" that organize groups of images</term> are arranged around the circumference; and the five subordinate items of each are arranged into ladders <note>original: "scalas"; these are radial columns or "steps" leading from the outer rim toward the center of the wheel</note> extending from each banner toward the middle.

# IMAGES OF THE FACES
->Images of the signs from Teucer of Babylon <note>An influential astrologer from the 1st century BCE/CE whose descriptions of the decans influenced later Hermetic and Arabic astrology.</note>, which can be very conveniently adapted for the use of the present art.<-

### Aries.

<margin>AA</margin>
In the first face <note>term: "face"; also known as a 'decan.' Each zodiac sign is divided into three 10-degree segments, each represented by a specific symbolic figure.</note> of Aries, there rises a black man of immense stature, with burning eyes and a severe expression, standing wrapped in a white robe <note>original: "palla"; a traditional rectangular mantle worn by ancient Romans.</note>.

<margin>AE</margin>
In the second, a woman not without charm, dressed in a white tunic, and wearing over it a cloak dyed with Tyrian purple <note>term: "Tyrian purple"; an expensive, deep-reddish-purple dye harvested from sea snails, used in antiquity as a symbol of status and divinity.</note>, with her hair loose and crowned with laurel.

<margin>AI</margin>
In the third, a pale man with reddish hair, dressed in red clothes, carrying a golden bracelet in his left hand and an oak staff in his right; he displays a restless and angry countenance, as he can neither obtain nor bestow the goods he desires.

<summary>This page describes the symbolic figures associated with the three decans (faces) of Aries, attributed to the ancient astrologer Teucer of Babylon. These vivid personifications are intended to be used as mental markers in a mnemonic system, categorized by vowel pairs (AA, AE, AI).</summary>

<keywords>Teucer of Babylon, Aries, decans, astrological faces, mnemonic system, memory palace, Tyrian purple, zodiac imagery</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of astrological "faces" (decans) attributed to Teucer of Babylon, following the descriptions for Aries. The organization continues to use the mnemonic vowel-pair sequence—AO, AV, BA, BE, BI, BO—to integrate these celestial images into a structured memory system.</meta>

## Taurus.

<margin>AO</margin> In the first face <note>term: "face"; also known as a 'decan.' Each of the twelve zodiac signs is divided into three 10-degree segments, each with its own symbolic image.</note> of Taurus, there is a naked man plowing, wearing a hat woven of straw, and of a dark complexion; he is followed by another countryman sowing seeds.

<margin>AV</margin> In the second, a naked Key-bearer <note>original: "Clauiger"; literally a bearer of keys, often a title for a gatekeeper or a specific mythological figure like Janus or Hercules.</note>, crowned, wearing a golden belt over his shoulders, and holding a scepter in his left hand.

<margin>BA</margin> In the third, a man carrying a serpent in his left hand and a spear or arrow in his right; before him stands a vessel of fire and a jug of water.

## Gemini.

<margin>BE</margin> In the first face of Gemini, a man prepared for service, holding a rod in his right hand, with a cheerful and pleasant expression.

<margin>BI</margin> In the second, a man digging the earth and laboring; next to him is a flute-player dancing with bare feet and a bare head.

<margin>BO</margin> In the third, a Buffoon <note>original: "Morio"; a professional jester or fool, often characterized in Renaissance texts by whimsical or eccentric behavior.</note> carrying a flute <note>original: "tibia"; an ancient pipe or flute-like instrument.</note> in his right hand and a sparrow in his left; next to him is an angry man grasping a staff.

<summary>This page details the symbolic figures associated with the three decans of Taurus and Gemini. These personifications—ranging from laborers and kings to jesters—serve as vivid mental landmarks for use in an artificial memory system.</summary>

<keywords>Taurus, Gemini, decans, astrological faces, Clauiger, Morio, mnemonics, Teucer of Babylon</keywords>

## Cancer. <note>original: "Cancer"; the sign of the Crab.</note>

<margin>BU</margin> In the first face <note>term: "face"; also known as a 'decan.' Each zodiac sign is divided into three ten-degree sections, each represented by a specific symbolic figure.</note> of Cancer, there is a crowned woman, excellently dressed, carrying an olive branch in her right hand and a wide bowl <note>original: "phialam"; a shallow ceremonial vessel or vial.</note> in her left.

<margin>CA</margin> In the second, a man and a woman sitting at a table and playing <note>This likely refers to games of chance or board games played in a social setting.</note> with wine before them; there are various types of fine garments; before the woman are gold and silver vessels.

<margin>CE</margin> In the third, a man, a hunter, whom dogs precede and follow; he carries a horn and a crossbow <note>original: "balistam"; while originally a large siege engine, in this context it refers to a handheld crossbow.</note>, with a swift and wandering gait.

## Leo. <note>original: "Leo"; the sign of the Lion.</note>

<margin>CI</margin> In the first face of Leo, a man of a reddish <note>original: "ruffus"; often used to describe a ruddy complexion or red hair.</note> color, dressed in saffron-colored clothes and crowned with gold; he carries a rooster in his right hand and is riding a lion.

<margin>CO</margin> In the second, a woman walking toward the heavens with her hands outstretched, and next to her a man who appears ready for vengeance, holding a drawn sword and a shield.

<margin>CU</margin> In the third, a man carrying a pouch or a whip <note>original: "scuticam"; a lash or scourge.</note>; he has a sad, downcast, and unlovely face, and is followed by a young man dressed in white.

18

## Virgo. <note>The Virgin; the sixth sign of the zodiac, associated with the harvest.</note>

<margin>Da</margin>
**In the first face of Virgo:** A girl crowned with flowers, and a man scattering flowers and foliage toward her, dressed in a green garment and ungirded <note>original: "discinctus"; wearing a tunic without a belt, often signifying a relaxed state or a specific style of classical dress.</note>.

<margin>De</margin>
**In the second** a black man clothed in leather, carrying a purse in his hand, having a cloak hanging down from his head.

<margin>Di</margin>
**In the third** an old man leaning on two staffs, with unkempt hair over his forehead, a scattered beard, and dressed in a dark color.

---

## Libra. <note>The Scales; the seventh sign of the zodiac, symbolizing balance and justice.</note>

<margin>Do</margin>
**In the first face of Libra,** there is a man looking into a book, holding a dagger in his right hand or a stylus <note>term: "stylus"; a pointed tool used for writing on wax tablets.</note>. He walks with a grim expression.

<margin>dv</margin> <note>The scribe uses 'v' and 'u' interchangeably; this continues the mnemonic sequence DA, DE, DI, DO, DU.</note>
**In the second** two people quarreling and troubled before a man sitting as a judge <note>original: "pro tribunali"; literally 'at the tribunal' or judgment seat.</note> who has a rod extended toward them in his right hand, and his left hand raised.

<margin>Fa</margin>
**In the third** a fierce <term>Sagittarius</term> <note>The Archer; usually depicted as a centaur. Its appearance here within the sign of Libra represents an "extra-zodiacal" influence or a specific decanic image.</note>, whom a man follows carrying bread in his hand and a cup of wine; and a completely naked man goes before him.

### Scorpio. <note>The Scorpion; the eighth sign of the zodiac, often associated with intensity and transformation.</note>

<margin>Ee</margin>
**In the first face of Scorpio**, a beautiful and very well-dressed woman rises, for whose sake two angry young men tire themselves out by striking one another with blows.

<margin>Ei</margin>
**In the second**, a completely naked woman and two completely naked men; one of them stands by the woman's side, while the other lies on the ground playing with a dog.

<margin>Eo</margin>
**In the third**, a man exposing his back to a woman who is striking him, while he holds both of his feet with both hands.

### Sagittarius. <note>The Archer; the ninth sign of the zodiac, symbolizing wisdom, the hunt, and far-reaching goals.</note>

<margin>Ev</margin>
**The first face of Sagittarius** is occupied by a man fully armed, carrying a small shield <term>parma</term> <note>original: "parmam"; a light, round shield used by Roman soldiers and later appearing in medieval heraldry.</note> in his left hand and a very broad sword in his right; the earth seems to tremble beneath his step.

<margin>Fa</margin>
**The second**, a sad woman dressed in mourning clothes, carrying one boy in her arms and leading another by the hand.

<margin>Fe</margin>
**The third**, a man lying on the ground, aimlessly waving a staff, pale in the face and wearing dirty clothing; a pig stands beside him, rooting up handfuls <note>original: "maniplos"; literally 'maniples' or bundles, here referring to the clumps of soil the pig is digging up.</note> of earth.

# Capricorn. <note>The Sea-Goat; the tenth sign of the zodiac, traditionally associated with winter, structure, and social status.</note>

<margin>F1</margin> **In the first face of Capricorn** <note>A "face" or decan is a ten-degree segment of a zodiac sign, each with its own specific symbolic image used in medieval and Renaissance astrology.</note>, a man in the attire of a merchant with an ugly and sad face, whom a young man follows, dancing and clapping his hands.

<margin>Fo</margin> **In the second**, a man hurling javelins at a flying dove; and two women embracing a single man.

<margin>FV</margin> **In the third**, a maiden dressed in white garments; she is trampling a little fox under her feet and is intently reading a book.

---

# Aquarius. <note>The Water-Bearer; the eleventh sign of the zodiac, often linked to intellect, community, and the pouring out of knowledge.</note>

<margin>GA</margin> **The first face of Aquarius** is held by the father of a family and a matron in the posture of people deep in thought: the former holds counters <note>original: "calculos"; small stones or tokens used for mathematical calculations or accounting.</note> in his hand, and the latter holds a distaff <term>colum</term> <note>original: "colum"; a tool used in spinning wool or flax, a traditional symbol of domestic industry.</note>.

<margin>GE</margin> **In the second**, a man in the attire of a counselor and seated, holding memorial notebooks in his hand; from his chin hangs a long beard, and he appears to be a man of a rather severe countenance.

<margin>G1</margin> **In the third**, an angry young man whose face appears inflamed with rage: his hands are poised as if attacking, and his fingers are twisted.

<meta>This page concludes the catalog of astrological decans with the sign of Pisces. It follows the descriptions of Capricorn and Aquarius from the previous page, continuing the vowel-based indexing system (Go, Gv, Ha) used to categorize these mnemonic images.</meta>

## Pisces. <note>The Fishes; the twelfth and final sign of the zodiac, often associated with transition, spirituality, and the completion of the astrological year.</note>

<margin>Go</margin> **In the first face of Pisces** <note>A "face" or decan is a ten-degree segment of a zodiac sign, each possessing its own unique symbolic image used in Renaissance astrology and magic.</note>, there is the figure of a man transporting his belongings and seeking a new habitation; he is followed by a woman carrying a tripod and a pole.

<margin>Gv</margin> **In the second**, a man girded as if for work, with his garments tucked up and his arms bared, showing an agility of body and a cheerful expression.

<margin>Ha</margin> **In the third**, a young man loving and embracing a girl, and near them are two of Juno’s birds <note>original: "aues Iunoniæ"; typically referring to peacocks, which were sacred to the Roman goddess Juno and symbolized immortality or pride.</note> struggling with one another.

The dress and actions of the aforementioned images, although they do not by themselves contribute to the <term>art of memory</term> <note>original: "artem memoratiuam"; a classical system of mnemonics where information is "placed" within vivid mental images to aid in perfect recall.</note>, they can nevertheless complete the systematic logic of the images.

<summary>This page describes the three symbolic "faces" or decans of Pisces, concluding the zodiacal sequence. The author notes that while these specific attributes might not be strictly necessary for mnemonic techniques, they complete the symbolic framework of the work.</summary>
