<meta>This page begins a new list of astrological descriptions, returning to the start of the zodiacal wheel with Aries. While the previous page focused on the visual "faces" or decans, this page describes the character traits and psychological influences associated with those same ten-degree segments.</meta>

I **The first image of Aries** <note>The Ram; the first sign of the zodiac, marking the spring equinox and associated with beginnings and vital energy.</note> is bold, shameless, and strong in the **imaginative faculty** <term>imaginatiuæ</term> <note>original: "imaginatiuæ"; in Renaissance psychology, this was the mental power that formed and held images in the mind, crucial for both art and memory.</note>.
**The second** is prouder, ambitious, and of a noble spirit.
**The third** is more restless and as if despairing, of a sharper intellect, awaiting joy.

II **The first image of Taurus** <note>The Bull; the second sign of the zodiac, typically associated with stability, earthiness, and material labor.</note> is a patron of the beginnings of works and geometric acts.
**The second** is powerful and noble among a multitude of peoples.
**The third** is needy, miserable, and even servile.

III **The first of Gemini** <note>The Twins; the third sign, associated with communication, intellect, and duality.</note> is wise in Mathematics and other faculties in which there is no utility <note>The author distinguishes here between "pure" theoretical knowledge and practical application, suggesting the first decan of Gemini favors abstract or perhaps even esoteric studies.</note>.
**The second** is shameless, a deceiver, and of a laborious diligence, yet producing no fruit.
**The third** is delirious, forgetful, and of much talkativeness.

IIII **The first of Cancer** <note>The Crab; the fourth sign, marking the summer solstice and associated with emotion and the domestic sphere.</note> is of subtle contemplation, a vigilant intellect, and a will prone to love.
**The second** is of modest intellect, and of much fortune.
**The third** is of very much business but modest follow-through, on account of arduous means.

V **The first of Leo** <note>The Lion; the fifth sign, associated with sovereignty, pride, and the sun.</note> is impetuous, luxuriant, cruel, and implacable.
**The second** is swift to the shedding of blood, treacherous, and suspicious.
**The third** is a companion and friend whom he would rather lose than keep or obtain through disputes. <note>This suggests a temperament that values harmony or pride so highly that it would rather abandon a relationship than suffer the indignity of a quarrel.</note>

<summary>This page catalogs the character traits and psychological profiles associated with the first three "faces" or decans of the signs Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, and Leo. It shifts from purely visual descriptions to the moral and intellectual qualities these celestial positions were thought to instill in humans.</summary>
<keywords>Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, decans, astrology, character traits, imaginative faculty, geometry, mathematics, temperament</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of astrological decans, following the descriptions of the signs from Aries through Leo on the previous pages. This section details the psychological and moral qualities associated with the three subdivisions (decans) of Virgo, Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius, Capricorn, and Aquarius.</meta>

**vi** **The first of Virgo** <note>The Virgin; the sixth sign of the zodiac, often associated with diligence, order, and the harvest.</note> accumulates wealth, yet not through dishonest dealings.
**The second** applies itself more greedily and intently to increasing its property.
**The third** weakens, enfeebles, breaks apart, and consumes.

**vii** **The first of Libra** <note>The Scales; the seventh sign, representing justice, balance, and social harmony.</note> is just, rescuing the poor from the hands of the powerful and the violent.
**The second** is both intolerant of injustice and a peacemaker of unrest.
**The third** is a harlot, an adulteress, and a glutton <note>original: "voratrix"; literally a female devourer, implying excessive consumption or lack of self-restraint.</note>.

**viii** **The first of Scorpio** <note>The Scorpion; the eighth sign, associated with intensity, secrecy, and transformation.</note> is a beautiful and flattering traitress, wicked in nature.
**The second** is likewise faithless and shameful.
**The third** is characterized by open anger, indignation, and violence.

**ix** **The first of Sagittarius** <note>The Archer; the ninth sign, linked to exploration, philosophy, and higher laws.</note> is bold, insane, and detests the yoke of all laws.
**The second** conceives sadness out of fear and gives birth to obedience.
**The third** is hairy <note>original: "capilosa"; in historical physiognomy, thick hair was sometimes interpreted as a sign of a wild or primitive temperament.</note>, quarrelsome, and an enemy of peace.

**x** **The first of Capricorn** <note>The Mountain Goat; the tenth sign, associated with structure, ambition, and material reality.</note> loses its wealth through luxury.
**The second** seeks after impossible things.
**The third** is more focused on increasing its possessions; in its wisdom, it acts foolishly <note>original: "Sapiendo desipit"; a paradoxical phrase suggesting that the individual’s attempts at being clever lead to foolish results.</note>.

**xi** **The first of Aquarius** <note>The Water-Bearer; the eleventh sign, associated with community and intellectual pursuits.</note> thinks and labors for profit <term>quæstum</term> while being burdened by poverty and low status.
**The second** possesses great intelligence and sobriety.
**The third** is of no small impudence and presumption.

<summary>This page describes the moral characters and fortunes associated with the three decans (ten-degree segments) of the zodiac signs from Virgo to Aquarius, often contrasting virtues like justice and intelligence with vices like greed and infidelity.</summary>

### XII

**The first of Pisces** <note>The Fishes; the twelfth and final sign of the zodiac, often associated with endings, spirituality, and the subconscious.</note> is anxious due to a lack of resources.
**The second** is intent upon many and great affairs.
**The third** grows sluggish with much leisure and luxury.

Although these descriptions do not serve as images <note>In the context of Renaissance astrological magic, "images" refers to specific visual figures carved into stones or drawn on parchment to capture a planet's power.</note> themselves, they are nevertheless necessary for determining the forms and qualities of such images, provided they are represented in some sensible appearance.

->*The following are seven images of Saturn from*<-
->*Egyptian and Persian philosophers,*<-
->*which may also be used for specific locations and*<-
->*subjects.*<-

**He** **The first image of Saturn.** A man with the face of a deer sitting atop a dragon, holding an owl in his right hand that is swallowing a serpent. <note>The owl and serpent are traditional symbols of wisdom and time, frequently associated with Saturn's more contemplative and ancient aspects.</note>

**Hi** **The second** is a man holding a sickle <note>original: "falcem"; the sickle is the most common attribute of Saturn/Cronus, symbolizing both the harvest and the "reaping" of time.</note> in his right hand and a fish in his left, and he rides upon a camel.

**Ho** **The third** is a sad and groaning man, having his palms raised toward heaven, dressed in dark garments. <note>Saturn was known as the "Great Malefic" and governed melancholy, sorrow, and somber religious devotion.</note>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of talismanic images of the planets, concluding the seven forms of Saturn and beginning the seven forms of Jupiter. **Hv The Fourth.** A black man having the feet of a camel, sitting upon a winged dragon: in his right hand carrying a branch of cypress <note>original: "Cupressi"; the cypress tree was traditionally associated with mourning and the underworld, fitting for the somber nature of Saturn.</note>.

**IA The Fifth.** Clothed in black garments and with a black face, in whose right hand is a basilisk <note>A legendary serpent king said to cause death with a single glance.</note> with its tail twisting around his arm.

**Ie The Sixth.** An old and lame man leaning on a staff on a high throne atop a chariot; which a mule and a donkey pull. <note>Saturn is often depicted as an old, slow, or "lame" figure because he represents the slowest-moving planet known to the ancients and governs old age.</note>

**Ii The Seventh.** A charioteer atop a chariot which two stags pull; in one hand bearing a fish, in the other a curved sickle <note>The sickle is the primary attribute of Saturn (as the Roman version of the Greek Cronus), symbolizing the harvest and the passage of time that eventually cuts down all living things.</note>.

->Seven images of Jupiter.<-

**Io The first image of Jupiter** is a handsome man atop a chariot drawn by dragons, in his right hand casting an arrow into the head of a dragon. <note>Jupiter is the "Greater Benefic" in astrology, representing justice, order, and the triumph of the intellect over chaotic forces (symbolized here by the dragons).</note>

**Iv The second image of Jupiter** is a man sitting in a chair: whom four winged youths pull, leaning against a leafy beech tree.

**ka The Third** is one having the head of a ram <note>original: "Arietinum"; this is a reference to the syncretism of Jupiter with the Egyptian god Amun-Ra, who was often depicted with a ram's head.</note>, sitting upon a wheel: and carrying a vessel of balsam in his hand. <note>Balsam was a rare and expensive medicinal resin, representing Jupiter's role as a healer and provider of abundance.</note>

**ke The Fourth** is one having the head of a lion and the feet of an eagle,

...and an oak branch in his right hand: before whom two youths of most beautiful appearance clothed in white bow down. <note>The oak tree was sacred to Jupiter (Zeus), representing strength and endurance.</note>

**ki The Fifth** sitting upon an eagle, adorned in emerald garments, having on his head a hyacinthine crown <note>original: "hiacintinam"; this refers to a deep blue or violet color, associated with the hyacinth stone, symbolizing the heavens.</note> and a scepter in his hand. <note>The eagle and scepter are the traditional attributes of Jupiter as king of the gods.</note>

**La The first image of Mars** is an armed man riding a lion: whose helmet a vulture strikes with its beak. He is a man of fierce appearance. <note>The vulture and the lion both represent the predatory and aggressive nature of Mars, the god of war.</note>

**Le The second** an armed man with a broad sword and a spear, on whose helmet is something like a chimera <note>A mythological fire-breathing creature, here signifying the destructive heat and fury of the planet Mars.</note> casting out flaming sparks from its mouth.

**Li The Third** casting sulfurous fires with his right hand, having the neck of a leopard gripped in his left, which he rides though it is not willing <note>original: "non bene consentientem"; this illustrates the Martian theme of conquest and the forceful taming of wild, dangerous beasts.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the descriptions of planetary images used for astrological talismans. **Lo The Fourth** is a man holding a drawn sword in his right hand and a human head dripping with blood: in his left hand, he has a face as if scorched by the sun. <note>original: "sole adustum"; this scorched appearance reflects the traditional association of Mars with intense, destructive heat.</note>

**Lv The Fifth** is a man of tawny color, dressed in red garments: carrying a heavy iron scepter, riding upon a wolf. <note>The wolf was sacred to Mars in Roman mythology, representing the predatory nature of war.</note>

**Ma The Sixth** is the ravisher of a most beautiful virgin who is turning away from him with all her might. Both are drawn in an ivory chariot by two <term>cynocephali</term> <note>From the Greek for "dog-heads," these were a race of mythological dog-headed men often depicted in medieval bestiaries and maps.</note>.

**Me The Seventh** is a leopard and a tiger fighting: on either side are two helmeted men with drawn swords threatening one another.

### Seven images of the Sun.

**Mi The First** image of the Sun is a lovely and crowned woman in a golden chariot, pulled by a <term>quatriugo</term> <note>original: "quatriugo"; a quadriga or four-horse team, the classical vehicle used by the sun god to traverse the heavens.</note> of rising horses.

**Mo The Second** is a most beautiful naked youth having a crown on his head woven from many flowers, embracing a peacock. <note>The peacock, while usually associated with Juno/Hera, here likely represents the "thousand eyes" or the variegated colors of light.</note>

**Mv The Third** is a youth wearing a diadem from whose head a brilliance of rays shines out, carrying a <term>pharetram</term> <note>original: "pharetram"; a quiver for holding arrows, identifying the Sun with the archer-god Apollo.</note> and a bow.

<summary>This page concludes the aggressive and often violent imagery associated with Mars and introduces the first three radiant, regal images of the Sun used in celestial magic.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of planetary images used for creating astrological talismans. It concludes the seven images of the Sun—representing vitality, clarity, and authority—and begins the sequence for Venus, the planet associated with love, beauty, and growth.</meta>

**Na The Fourth** is a woman embracing and kissing a boy who is dressed in a green ankle-length robe <note>original: "veste talari"; a garment reaching the ankles, often worn by scholars or priests.</note> with tawny hair and a beautiful face, holding a mirror in her right hand.

**Ne The Fifth** is a virgin carrying a shield in her left hand and throwing a dart with her right, sitting upon a crocodile. <note>The crocodile was often associated with solar deities in ancient Egyptian traditions, which influenced these later Renaissance magical texts.</note>

**Ni The Sixth** is a man riding a running Lion which breathes out a cloud of darkness from its nostrils; in the man's right hand is a rooster. <note>The rooster is a classic solar symbol, signaling the dawn, while the Lion represents the astrological home of the Sun (the sign of Leo).</note>

**No The Seventh** is a man in the habit of a priest <term>pontiff</term> <note>original: "pontificis"; while this can mean "Pope," in a magical context it refers to a high priest or someone with sacred authority.</note> preceded by two men with bare heads in tawny or saffron garments; he has a raven in his bosom and a golden dog beneath his feet.

### Seven images of Venus.

**Nv The First image of Venus** is a girl crowned with myrtle, naked, having long hair reaching down to her ankles, and before her a little white dog leaping for joy. <note>Myrtle was a plant sacred to Venus; the little dog often symbolizes playfulness or "the sport of love."</note>

**Oa The Second** is a charming boy carrying a basket packed with various flowers in both hands: whom a man follows in the manner of a gardener.

**Oe The Third** is a bust of a naked woman upon which a dove's head seems to be grafted, having the feet of an eagle. <note>The dove is the traditional bird of Venus, representing peace and love, while the eagle's feet may suggest the piercing power or the celestial height of "Venus Urania" (Heavenly Venus).</note>

<summary>This page concludes the symbolic descriptions of the Sun’s talismans and begins those for Venus, focusing on themes of beauty, nature, and devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Sun, Venus, talismans, astrology, planetary magic, iconography, lion, rooster, myrtle, dove</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of planetary images, concluding the sequence for Venus (the planet of love and harmony) and beginning the sequence for Mercury (the planet of intellect, communication, and travel).</meta>

...whom a youth follows, and a man going before seems to flee.

**Oi The Fourth.** A woman riding a bull, combing her hair with her right hand and holding a mirror in her left, by whom stands a younger youth holding a green bird in his hand. <note>The bull represents Taurus, an astrological sign ruled by Venus, while the mirror and green bird are classic symbols of beauty and nature associated with her.</note>

**Oo The Fifth.** A boy holding a silver chain: and next to him a naked girl, crowned with laurel and berries <note>original: "bacchis"; specifically refers to the berries of the laurel or ivy, often associated with festive or Dionysian energy</note>, and dancing.

**Ov The Sixth.** A winged boy having hair more shining than gold, whose feathers are dipped in a thousand colors, throwing fiery darts. <note>This is a classic description of Cupid (Eros), the son of Venus, whose "fiery darts" represent the sudden spark of desire.</note>

**Pa The Seventh.** A youth and a girl wrestling, both naked, struggling so that one might bind the other; of whom the girl has a fine linen <term>byssine</term> <note>original: "bissinam"; a rare and costly fine fabric from antiquity, often called "sea silk."</note> chain, and the youth has a golden chain in his hand.

### Seven images regarding Mercury.

**Qv The Second.** A hooded countryman fishing with a hook.

## Image of the Dragon of the moon.

The "Head" (North Node) was traditionally considered auspicious and associated with growth.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from a catalog of specific astrological images (Houses 1-12) to a theoretical explanation of how to use those images within a mnemonic system. It establishes the relationship between "forms" (the images) and "subjects" (the places or memory locations), emphasizing the need for vividness to engage the "imaginative faculty."</meta>

-> 158 <-

### *The use of the "hand" <note>original: "manus"; likely referring to a mnemonic "hand" diagram where different images are mapped onto the joints and parts of the hand for easy recall</note> of the aforementioned images for the memory of things.*

You will be able to gain great utility and incomparable benefit from the things previously noted. 

i. Let the forms <note>the mnemonic images</note> be perpetually inherent in perpetual subjects <note>the "loci" or fixed memory locations, such as rooms in a building or parts of the hand</note>. 

ii. Let the subjects provide order to the images, or let the images receive their order from the subjects. This indeed is conducive to maintaining the memory of the forms themselves. 

iii. But if it pleases you to turn the added details themselves into the nature and role of the subjects, what is there that could hinder you? 

iv. Therefore, commit the parts of the thing to be remembered to vivid images, symbols, actions, and their circumstances in order: so that each individual part—through an action appropriate to it, or through a <term>passion</term> <note>Latin: "passionem"; here meaning the state of being acted upon or influenced, rather than emotion</note>, an alteration, a change of position, or various other modes of being (either as things that act, or as the tools and objects of those actions)—may be able to describe the individual divisions of the material to be remembered one by one. 

In all of these matters, do not let it escape your notice that you must choose <term>species</term> <note>Latin: "species"; refers to the mental "forms" or appearances of things as perceived by the mind</note> that are not abstract, but are fixed before the eyes of the <term>imaginative faculty</term> <note>original: "phantastica facultas"; the part of the soul/brain believed to process and store sensory images</note>. For indeed, we have shown elsewhere how we ought to capture those things which, by moving the external senses more strongly, have more powerfully crept into the internal senses.

<summary>This page explains the practical application of the previously described images within a memory system, detailing how to link vivid, active "forms" to fixed "subjects" or locations. It emphasizes that memory works best when mental images are active and striking enough to move from the external senses into the internal imaginative faculty.</summary>

<keywords>Art of Memory, mnemonics, imaginative faculty, mental images, loci and imagines, species, cognitive theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for building a memory system. ### **Concerning the combinations in which the underlying element precedes the accompanying element.**

With these things thus arranged toward the multitude of infinite combinations to be constituted from the elements: there is no reason for you to add further orders of primary compositions. For here, just as you have one hundred and fifty simple <term>combinations</term> <note>refers to the pairing of two mnemonic images or symbols to represent a complex idea or sound</note>, in which the <term>accompanying element</term> <note>Latin: "elementum adsistens"; a secondary or mobile component of a mnemonic image</note> (or that which is placed in the role of the accompanying) precedes the <term>underlying element</term> <note>Latin: "elementum subsistens"; the primary or fixed component that acts as the base for the image</note> (or that which is placed in the role of the underlying), so with very easy effort you have one hundred and fifty others in which the accompanying element (or that which is placed for the accompanying) follows the underlying element (or that which is placed for the underlying). 

Therefore, from the difference of straight or curved, clothed or stripped, turned here or there, sitting or standing, or a thousand other modes of having a double state, you will be able to adapt this very thing for yourself. For example, <term>Coroebus the potter</term> <note>original: "Chorebus figulus"; a legendary figure from Greek antiquity credited with the invention of pottery</note> sitting denotes *A M*, who by standing signified *Ma*. <note>The author is demonstrating how a single character can represent different syllables or data points by simply changing their posture (sitting vs. standing), thereby doubling the system's capacity.</note>

<summary>This page explains a combinatorial mnemonic method where the spatial relationship and physical posture of images (underlying and accompanying elements) are used to multiply the amount of information stored. By reversing the order or changing the stance of a figure like Coroebus, the practitioner can represent different phonetic or conceptual data.</summary>

<keywords>combinations, underlying element, accompanying element, Coroebus the potter, mnemonic permutations, memory palace techniques, phonetic encoding</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for a combinatorial memory system. While the previous page discussed the basic 150 combinations of primary and secondary mnemonic elements, this page expands the system to include more complex phonetic structures, specifically "liquid" and "terminal" consonants, allowing the user to encode longer or more complex words into a single mental image.</meta>

### *Concerning the liquid and terminal elements between and after the simple combinations.*

As for the mediating and supervening—or falling and following—elements belonging to each of the five <term>combinations</term> <note>original: "combinationum"; the pairing of mnemonic symbols to represent sounds or ideas</note>, you, O friend, will be able to provide for these through your own study; for we have indeed paved a wide path for you where we taught how to form any combinations increasing up to a <term>quinary</term> <note>original: "quinarium"; a group of five elements</note> number of elements. 

You shall therefore give to the <term>agent</term> <note>original: "agenti"; the person or figure performing an action in the mnemonic scene</note> eleven "differences" by which the final elements are signified: C, G, L, M, N, P, NS, NT, R, S, T. <note>These represent terminal consonant sounds in Latin</note> And you shall give three differences by which certain middle <term>liquid</term> <note>original: "liquescentia"; consonants like L, N, and R that "flow" into other sounds</note> elements are revealed: L, N, R. The first set, if you please, shall be placed on its head; the second on its back, or vice-versa. 

In the same way, you will assign eleven differences of operations to signify the finals, and three for the middle ones: of which the first (if you please) will be located in the material subject to the action, and the second in the arms or hands of the operator. 

Similarly, you will distinguish twelve and three in the <term>bystander</term> <note>original: "circumstante"; a secondary figure within the memory palace or scene</note>. In the <term>assistant</term> <note>original: "adstante"; another term for a supporting figure</note>, you may use the same differences as you use for the operator, since they are of the same kind. We have communicated these things so familiarly, therefore, that very little seems left to your own industry <note>A common Renaissance trope where the author praises the reader's intelligence while claiming the instructions are now perfectly clear</note>.

-> 21 <-

Elements, combinations, quinary, liquids, of operations, bystander

By placing symbols on a figure's head, back, or hands, the user can encode complex phonetic variations into a single mental image.</summary>

<meta>This page follows a detailed technical explanation of how to represent complex phonetic endings (terminal consonants and liquid sounds) within a mnemonic system. Having established the mechanics of these "combinations," the author now pivots to celebrate the revolutionary efficiency of his method compared to older, more cumbersome systems.</meta>

### *Concerning two excellent inventions in this art,*
### *and a praise of the art itself.*

There are, therefore, two things that we have discovered and completed in this art, both of which seemed impossible to achieve to all the ancients. First, regarding the **first <term>practice</term>** <note>original: "praxim"; the practical application or method of the mnemonic system</note>, we have made it so that any combination of sounds or ideas, no matter what kind, can be assigned to be presented by only a single **<term>subject</term>** <note>original: "subiecto"; in the "Art of Memory," a subject is a mental image or a specific location (locus) used to store information</note>. Second, regarding the **second practice**—which is an almost divine invention and pregnant with further discoveries, provided one knows how to apply it—we have made it so that we can conveniently attach any **<term>term</term>** <note>original: "terminum"; a full word or a distinct concept, rather than just a single letter or syllable</note> to be referred back to a single and individual subject of this art. 

Our invention, therefore, serves to perfect **internal writing** <note>original: "intimam scripturam"; the process of "writing" information onto the memory using mental images</note> far more quickly and efficiently, and to bring the fruit of one’s practice to maturity. For where others used to "bleat" <note>original: "balabant"; a mocking term suggesting that older methods were as slow and repetitive as a sheep's cry</note> while gathering letters into syllables before they were—much later—promoted to more complete acts of memory, we now teach how to immediately gather whole terms into speech. 

Therefore, as soon as the student of this art has mastered my **<term>catalog</term>** <note>original: "cathalogum"; a systematic list of mnemonic images or rules</note> of the first or second practice, he will be able to record things read or heard by adding them to his ordered mental subjects. Then, through use, he will become ready and will perform everything with the greatest promptness. For this reason, this art does away with all others that preceded it in this field, and for the time being, it does not fear any other that might follow it. For we believe we have brought the matter to its very peak: because while others cast their eyes about to catch, or rather hunt after, individual elements, we run through whole terms toward the completion of language and **<term>discourse</term>** <note>original: "orationem"; formal speech or the structured flow of thought</note>.

<summary>The author claims to have perfected the art of memory by inventing a method to store complex data and entire words within single mental images. This is presented as a revolutionary leap over older systems that could only handle information syllable-by-syllable.</summary>
<keywords>art of memory, mnemonics, mental imagery, internal writing, pedagogical efficiency, linguistic terms, cognitive shortcuts</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general praise of the mnemonic art to the specific practical application of how to categorize different types of "things" (concepts or objects) to be remembered. ## ON THE PRACTICE OF
### simple terms that can be
### signified.

Let us now approach that **practice** <note>original: "praxim"; the practical application or method of the mnemonic system</note> which handles the memory of things. Of those things that exist, some are signified without complexity, while others are signified with complexity. Of those which are grasped within this grouping, some are **substances** <note>original: "substantiæ"; in Aristotelian logic, a "substance" is something that exists in itself, like a person or a tree</note>, while others are **accidents** <note>original: "accidentia"; attributes or qualities that exist only within a substance, such as a color or a weight</note>. Of those which are substances, some are **sensible** <note>original: "sensibilia"; things perceived by the physical senses</note>, while others are **intelligible** <note>original: "intelligibilia"; things understood only by the mind or intellect, such as abstract concepts</note>. Of the sensible things, some are perceptible through themselves, while others are perceptible by their accidents. Of those which are accidents, some are within the substance—such as quantity and intrinsic quality—while others adhere to the substance as extrinsic qualities, such as colors and the shapes that surround them.

Some things, however, are in a certain way "within" and in a certain way "present," such as **dispositions** <note>original: "habitudines"; the way a thing is related to itself or others</note>, some of which are taken from what a thing is, and others from what is said about it. Certain things consist within substances, such as those that act upon things receiving the action, and the receivers of action in relation to those that act <note>This refers to the categories of "Action" and "Passion" (being acted upon)</note>. Certain things surround the substance: some of these surround it as things to which the substance is applied, such as **position** <note>original: "situs"; the physical posture or arrangement of a thing</note>, while others are surrounded as things applied to the substance—namely those things said to be "had" from the outside. Of these, some are joined to the subject through the addition of the thing itself, whereby someone is said to be **wearing a ring**, **booted**, **clothed**, or **married** <note>original: "annulatus, ocreatus, indutus, vxoratus"; these are classical examples of the category of "Habit" or "Having" (Habitus)</note>. Others are adjuncts, such as a house, an estate, or a woman. Certain things in a way...

<summary>This page introduces the practical method for categorizing information into Aristotelian "Categories" (substance, quality, quantity, relation, etc.) to make them easier to store in the memory. It distinguishes between things that exist independently and the various attributes or external circumstances—like clothing or property—that define them.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelian categories, substance, accidents, mnemonics, sensible things, intelligible things, habitus, classification</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how different categories of "things"—specifically those relating to time, place, and Aristotelian accidents—are perceived and, more importantly, how they must be "figured" or turned into mental images to be remembered. It picks up from the previous page's discussion of how certain qualities exist within or around substances.</meta>

...are within and in a way surrounding, such as the concept of "when" <note>original: "quando"; one of Aristotle's ten categories, referring to time</note>. For time is both outside and above the thing as something single and general, and yet it is also in the thing itself, as something specific to each individual. This is true of both time and place. For **Time** is one "subjectively" <note>original: "subiective"; in this context, meaning the underlying reality</note> in the heavens, but it is multiple subjectively in each of those things that are called temporal. **Place**, likewise—if we follow the logical philosophers <note>referring to those who define things by strict Aristotelian logic</note>—is said to be the "surface of the container" and is something that surrounds a thing.

If we follow common speech, place is called the "containing body," and it is still considered something surrounding. But if, according to the natural philosophers <note>original: "physicos"; those who study the physical nature of the world</note>, it is called space, or a dimension of more stable matter, or a receptacle for all dimensions, then it is "in the thing" and "with the thing" in its own way, which is obvious to anyone who considers it.

For the remembrance of all these things to occur, it is necessary that a **figuration** <note>original: "figurationem"; the process of creating a mental image or "figure" to represent an abstract concept</note> has come before. Therefore, **simple intelligible substances** <note>abstract concepts or spiritual beings like angels that cannot be seen</note> are represented by the "figuration" of sensible substances—just as was done with the **wheels and fires of Ezekiel** <note>original: "rotis & ignibus Ezechielis"; referring to the vivid, physical imagery in the biblical Book of Ezekiel used to represent complex divine truths</note>. **Simple sensible substances** <note>physical things like people or animals</note> are represented through their "accidents" <note>their traits or positions</note>, such as the upright arrangement of a human's limbs, or the various ways animals are prone toward the earth.

Things that are perceptible by "accident" <note>perceived indirectly</note> are represented through those things which are the direct objects of the senses. **Accidents that are within** <note>intrinsic qualities like size</note> are represented by the things in which they reside; for instance, a specific **quantity** is represented by the object that allows for that specific "continuous" or "discrete" amount <note>continuous quantity refers to things like length; discrete quantity refers to things that can be counted, like a group of people</note>.

A specific **quality** is represented through the very thing in which it properly resides. In this way, a **substance** is represented through those things which we imagine the "accidents" to stand under. **Accidents that are inherent** are represented by the things in which they inhere, just as they are represented through these inherent traits by the very things in which they... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at "in-", likely continuing "insunt" (are within) on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page explains how abstract concepts like time, place, and qualities are categorized and emphasizes that they must be converted into visual "figures" (like Ezekiel's vision) to be stored in memory.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelian categories, mnemonics, figuration, Ezekiel, time and place, substance and accidents, mental imagery</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "figuration"—the process of turning abstract concepts into mental images—specifically focusing on how "accidents" (properties like time, place, or quality) are linked to substances to make them memorable. It then transitions into the practical application of these concepts in the art of mental inscription.</meta>

...cling. **Accidents** <note>original: "accidentia"; in Aristotelian logic, these are non-essential qualities of a thing, such as its color, size, or location</note> that are present are represented, in turn, by those things in which they reside. Accidents that exist alongside things are represented interchangeably with those with which they exist. Circumstantial accidents <note>properties relating to the surrounding environment or context</note>, since they cannot be represented as mental figures by themselves, are "figured" through those things of which they are the circumstances or those things they are placed around. Once they have been turned into figures, they are also able to bring back to mind the meaning of those things through which they were first represented. 

Thus, when all these things have been turned into figures—by means of those things required for "subjecting" and "adding" <note>original: "subiectionem & adiectionem"; the logical process of assigning a property to a subject</note>—they are rendered memorable and remain within the halls <note>original: "atris," likely a shorthand for "atriis"; a metaphor comparing the memory to the courtyards or halls of a great house</note> of the internal senses.

## ON THE PRACTICE OF THINGS
## conceivable through complex terms.

Just as a "complex" <note>original: "complexio"; the joining of two or more simple ideas to form a full thought or proposition</note> is created in the mind and in speech when, through a connecting verb <note>original: "copulam substantiui"; literally the "substantive copula," referring to the verb "to be"</note>, one simple concept is joined with another, or one is divided from the other; so it is in <term>engraphia</term> <note>from the Greek for "inner writing"; the art of inscribing images onto the "wax tablet" of the mind</note>. 

This art of <term>engraphia</term>, which follows and designates such complexes, first forms a complex out of simple parts when it joins substance to substance, relates one substance to another, or adapts one accident to another. It connects what is "within" to that in which it exists, what is "beside" to that which it assists, and what is "around" to that which it surrounds. This process of complexing contributes primarily to forming the images, figures, and signs of **definitions**—speaking in a general sense. (I set aside the fact that for other logical purposes, definitions are counted among "simple" concepts; for in <term>engraphia</term>, without this complexing, they do not con-...)

<summary>This page explains how abstract qualities (accidents) are turned into mental images by linking them to the objects they belong to. It introduces "engraphia," the practice of mentally "writing" or joining these images together to form complex definitions in the memory.</summary>
<keywords>accidents, substances, figuration, internal senses, engraphia, mnemonics, logic, definitions</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how "complex" or compound thoughts are structured in the mind. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how simple concepts must be established before they can be combined into larger logical propositions.</meta>

...[con]ceived; here, they cannot be expressed without the **simplicity of simpler terms** <note>original: "simplicitate terminorum simpliciorum"; the idea that complex ideas must be broken down into their most basic building blocks to be understood or memorized</note>). 

**Second**, this applies to forming or completing **propositions** <note>original: "enuntiationes"; statements that affirm or deny something</note>, which occurs when one or more terms are spoken of or by one thing, or of or by many things. 

**Third**, it applies to the ordering of **discourses** <note>original: "discursus"; the process of reasoning from one thought to another</note> when a **complex** <note>original: "complexio"; a compound thought or logical structure</note> is created from other complexes as if from its own limbs—that is, when composite parts are joined together into an even greater composition.

Just as it is said by **natural philosophers** <note>original: "physicis"; those who study the laws of nature and the physical world</note> that, first, "form" comes to "matter" to create a **simple body**; then, that simple body goes into the making of an "imperfect mixture." All simple things together lead to the production, on one hand, of "imperfect sinew," on the other of "perfect bone," and on another of "perfect flesh"—which are called **homogeneous parts** <note>parts that are uniform in structure, like tissue or wood</note>. All these parts then come together to constitute this or that **heterogeneous member** <note>original: "etherogeneum membrum"; an organ or limb made of different types of tissues, such as a hand or an eye</note>, such as the head and the arms. Finally, all the members meet together to encompass the whole of a single **living body**.

It happens no differently with mental forms and **additions** <note>original: "adiectis"; properties or qualities added to a subject to make it more specific</note>. After the simple formation, we proceed to the first level of complexity, from this to the second, from this to the third, and from there to making other combinations according to the capacity of the subjects. 

But if a single **subject** <note>the primary image or "placeholder" in the memory palace</note> cannot contain such a great and complete combination, let there first be a combination of one subject with another, or a connection of many subjects with others; in this way, the application of those things to which an addition is made is followed by the application of those things which are being added. 

Thus, while ships are joined to ships in succession to form a bridge, you shall cross the **Hellespont** <note>The narrow strait in northwestern Turkey; the author refers to the famous bridge of boats built by the Persian King Xerxes to march his army into Greece</note> with dry feet.

<summary>This page explains the hierarchy of mental "composition," comparing the way simple thoughts build into complex arguments to the way matter forms tissues, then organs, and finally a whole body. It concludes with an analogy of a bridge of boats to show how linking memory images allows one to navigate complex information.</summary>

<keywords>logic, propositions, natural philosophy, anatomy, homogeneous parts, heterogeneous members, Hellespont, memory techniques, complexio</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the introductory "Enigma and Paradigm" dedicated to King Henry III. ## A Certain Explanation of the Enigma.

From here (most serene king), **historians** will be able, with very little effort, to arrange those whose memory they seek in an orderly fashion (as they are distinguished throughout volumes, books, and chapters). By placing them thus, they can lead them out, as if from their own houses, through **courtyards** <note>original: "atria"; in the Art of Memory, these are "loci" or mental places used to store information</note>; in these, various dealings and the various successes of lives and actions will be placed in order before the eyes, to be grasped in an infallible and brief interval of time.

For the solemn and most famous history of all others <note>Referring to Sacred Scripture/the Bible</note>, it is necessary to have established a common house for **Adam, Abel, Cain, Noah, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Joseph, Moses,** and the rest, distinct with proper small receptacles for each individual. For soon, all of them—either traversing the same path successively or different individuals taking different paths—are led through a courtyard (either one common to all or several unique to each); they will then cause the order of all **accidents** <note>original: "accidentium"; a philosophical term referring to the non-essential qualities or events associated with a subject</note> to be retained under the order of the subjects of history.

In this way, **orators** will be able to place the articles of their speeches under the various members <note>divisions</note> of those same speeches. **Legal scholars** can arrange titles under books; laws under titles; sections <note>original: "§"</note> under laws; and under these, even the intended meanings of the verses. **Physicians** will be able to arrange sections and chapters, parts of chapters, and aphorisms with all their meanings under books, no less orderly than they are found in the book itself.

It will be permitted to adapt the materials of one's own profession in no different a manner for all others (to a degree greater than could be believed by those ignorant of this present industry).

As a fruitful **Model** <note>original: "Typo"</note> for this art, you have the following...

## ENIGMA AND PARADIGM
->**OF GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA.**<-

*Lest the mind stray like a stranger from the clear light,*
*Nor the running sense fall behind the things it seeks:*
*And lest the deep water, sought to flee burning thirst,*
*Deceive you—a man sent into the profound depths of Tartarus* <note>original: "Tartara"; in classical mythology, the deepest abyss of the underworld. Bruno warns that without this "Art," the mind sinks into a chaotic void of forgetting</note>:
*Powerful Circe* <note>The sorceress from the Odyssey who transformed men into animals; here she represents the "Art" itself, which transforms and categorizes raw data</note>, *gather your juices into seven halls,*

*Transfer these wicked things into the field of the aged parent* <note>original: "annosi parentis"; a reference to Saturn, the god of time and the slowest-moving planet, representing the first "hall" or category</note>
*Let these have Jove* <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods</note> *together with Ganymede* <note>The cupbearer to the gods; this line identifies the second planetary hall</note>.

*Sweetly breathing Venus, by whom you conquer all living creatures,*
*Take your place, Goddess, in a secluded spot.*

*Trust the female to the female sea, and the male to the male.*

*(Lest your gifts be violated).*

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the poetic "Enigma" of the previous section into a prose explanation of how this Mnemonic Art is applied across various professional disciplines. Bruno moves from the theoretical and mythological to the practical utility of his memory system.</meta>

This art reveals itself too much by its very chant, especially to those who have attained knowledge of the **rotation of the wheels** <note>original: "conuersionis rotarum"; Bruno uses paper "volvelles" or revolving wheels to generate combinations of ideas and images</note>. In a case where a leader of an army wishes to refer centurions—known by sight personally or through a deputy—to their own proper ranks, he ought to have viewed them with the internal eye of the **imagination** <note>original: "phantasiæ"; in Renaissance psychology, the "phantasy" was the faculty that created and manipulated mental images</note> under various and fitting actions, or passions, or circumstances, in seats multiplied according to the number of the ranks. 

From this, the medical <term>Circe</term> <note>referring to the art of healing as a form of "sorcery" or transformation of nature; Circe represents the power to categorize the qualities of natural things</note> has the qualities of all **simples** <note>medicinal herbs or unmixed drugs</note> and the degrees of those qualities inscribed and fixed in the memory with very short and easy study. From this, the <term>Grammarian</term> will be able to advance a boy, in the space of a single day, to the point of retaining the genders of all nouns (once it is understood what is properly signified by a noun). 

And pay attention to what we say regarding "what it properly signifies," because the common opinion is that the same thing is signified by many and diverse names in the same language, which are articulated under different genders. To this opinion, of course, we have for a long time not subscribed: for different words always carry from within themselves some different **emphasis** (if I may say so): so that we do not understand exactly the same thing by *tunic*, *garment*, and *clothing* <note>original: "tunicam, vestem, & indumentum"; Bruno argues that since no two words are perfectly identical in meaning, they must each be assigned a unique mental image</note>. Therefore, excluding <term>Synonymy</term>, we shall suffer no difficulty at all in this and other similar businesses. 

From this, the <term>astrologer</term> will be able to see the **forty-eight images of the sky** <note>the 48 constellations of the Ptolemaic system</note> related to the four parts of the heaven, with the logic of their sites, positions, and their furthest parts (which are the stars of considered magnitude), gathered in the same order in four halls. From this, finally, all other sciences, arts, and exercises, whether already ordered or yet to be ordered, will be able to refer things to their own decades, centuries, or myriads.

You have this art materially figured in the following **schema of the twelve houses**. <note>original: "duodecim domorum schemate"; referring to the twelve divisions of the zodiac used as a framework for organizing memory loci</note>

<summary>Bruno explains the practical applications of his mnemonic system for soldiers, doctors, grammarians, and astrologers, emphasizing that every distinct word or object must have its own unique mental place.</summary>
<keywords>mnemonic art, memory wheels, military organization, pharmacology, grammar, synonyms, astrology, constellations, twelve houses, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous astrological diagram—which mapped human concerns like life, death, and religion onto the houses of the zodiac—into a poetic and philosophical foundation for the Art of Memory. Having provided the "map" of the cosmos, Bruno now introduces the underlying metaphysical principles: the "Monad" (the One) and the shifting forms of nature (Proteus), establishing that memory is possible because a single divine order underlies the infinite variety of the world.</meta>

# GIORDANO BRUNO
## OF NOLA

### ANOTHER SHORT ART
#### for the memory of words and 
#### things.

-> An enigma involving the most skillful selection of subjects and forms serving the art. <-

*In motion, the mover, driven by that same impulse,*
*Gives motion, flourishing in eternal immobility.* <note>Bruno refers to the "Unmoved Mover," a concept from Aristotle describing a divine being that causes all motion in the universe without moving itself.</note>
*As one center is recognized by the circle carried around it*
*In a circuit, which it is never able to leave:*
*And as a moving wheel rotates on its fixed axle,*
*Nor does it complete its path with straight-line motion:*
*The wise nature of works will not vary her face:*
*Unless both principles remain forever.*
*Proteus, in the vast Carpathion whirlpool of Neptune,* <note>In mythology, Proteus was a sea-god who could change into any shape. He lived in the "Carpathion" sea near Egypt.</note>
*Driven after the shameful herd by his two-footed horses,* <note>original: "bipedes equos"; these are "hippocamps," mythical sea-creatures depicted as horses with fish tails, possessing only two legs.</note>
*Remains the same even while he assumes all forms,*
*And performs great, eternal divine acts within the deep.*
*Not otherwise in all things which you govern by nature,*
*Therefore the <term>Monad</term> stands before, lasts through, and underlies all.* <note>term: Monad; from the Greek 'monas' (unity). In Bruno's philosophy, it represents the irreducible, divine center of all existence.</note>
*And let that Monad which you see underlying deny itself no action,*
*By which it may come more dutifully to all.*
*Turn Chaos toward Anaxagoras; and let the atoms have* <note>Anaxagoras was an ancient philosopher who believed that 'Mind' (Nous) organized the primordial chaos into the universe.</note>
*Democritus as their father; and <term>Matter</term> its Plato.* <note>original: "Sylua"; literally "forest," but used in Latin translations of Plato to mean "hyle" or raw, primordial matter.</note>
*The seeds which it receives, it preserves, cherishes, orders, and increases.*
*The mother is prudent, unless the father is without mind.*
*It is pleasing to extend this into the series of the microcosms* <note>original: "τῶν μικροκοσμῶν"; Greek for "of the microcosms" (the "small worlds" or individual humans).</note>
*Which the greatest world holds enclosed within.* <note>The "greatest world" is the macrocosm or the universe as a whole.</note>

<summary>This page presents a philosophical poem that establishes the metaphysical basis for Bruno's mnemonic system, using the figure of Proteus to illustrate how a single substance (the Monad) can take on infinite forms. It argues that the human mind (the microcosm) reflects the structure of the entire universe (the macrocosm).</summary>
<keywords>Monad, Proteus, Chaos, Anaxagoras, Democritus, Plato, Microcosm, Macrocosm, Metaphysics, Mnemonic Theory</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page follows the introduction of the "Monad" and "Proteus" as metaphysical foundations for memory. Having established that the universe is a singular substance taking infinite forms, Bruno now moves into the technical application of these principles: the specific mechanics of his memory system, shifting from philosophy to "The Explanation of the Art."</meta>

## EXPLANATION OF THE ART.

Let there be animate subjects <note>original: "animata subiecta"; in Bruno's system, these are "agents" or human figures who perform actions to represent information.</note> within inanimate subjects <note>original: "inanimata subiecta"; these are the "loci" or memory places, such as rooms, buildings, or specific spots in a landscape.</note>: and let these be fixed so that they do not move from place to place.

Let the inanimate subjects have five local distinctions <note>These "five differences" typically refer to the four corners and the center of a memory room.</note> from which the mute animals may emit a sound.

Let there be present for each animate subject twenty-four or thirty types of <term>organs</term> <note>term: organs; from the Latin "organa," meaning tools, instruments, or body parts. Here, they refer to the physical attributes or objects a memory figure holds or uses to represent specific letters or concepts.</note>, by which—directed toward the five mentioned local distinctions—it may express what needs to be expressed according to need and necessity.

Let it suffice for you, however, that each emits a single sound. For we have found through experience that for the stability of things placed [in the memory], it is better and more advisable to work boldly with many subjects than hesitantly and with fear with only a few.

Have four kinds of organs. First, those which are positioned aptly to the right and to the left.

Second, those which are upward. Third, those which are downward. Fourth, those in plain sight, through which the <term>rational animal</term> <note>term: rational animal; a standard Scholastic definition of a human being.</note> performs what is to be done.

I believe it will be advantageous, therefore, and accomplished with greater skill, if each individual thing has a permanent organ for the five mentioned distinctions, which is moved [or activated] when new species <note>original: "speciebus"; in this context, it refers to the mental "images" or "forms" of things one wishes to remember.</note> arrive.

To designate the <term>mediating elements</term> <note>term: mediating elements; these are sounds or letters (like L, R, N) that occur in the middle of words, used here as a phonetic shorthand in Bruno's memory system.</note>, let forms shine forth from the region toward the practitioner that signify L, R, and N.

To designate the other <term>consequents</term> <note>term: consequents; refers to the letters or syllables that follow the initial ones.</note>, let there be things that represent for you the <term>terminations</term> <note>term: terminations; the grammatical endings of words.</note> in the Latin, Greek, Italian, Spanish, and French languages.

-> B. P. M. L. R. S. N. C. T. G. D. F. A. E. I. Y. Ѻ. V. <-

<summary>Bruno explains the technical structure of his memory system, using animate "agents" placed in fixed "loci" (rooms) who use "organs" (tools) to represent phonetic sounds and linguistic endings.</summary>
<keywords>memory art, mnemonic agents, loci, memory organs, phonetic system, linguistics, translation of sounds</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the mnemonic system, specifically detailing how the "organs" (tools/attributes) are used by "animate subjects" (memory agents) to encode information. It transitions from the physical placement of these organs to the conceptual flexibility of the "Art."</meta>

Therefore, let other arts command that the living figures wander while the instruments remain fixed: this art, however, finds satisfaction in doing the opposite <note>original: "ista sané sibi magis oppositum inhibendo complacet." Bruno is distinguishing his method from the Classical Art of Memory, where figures are typically imagined moving through fixed rooms. In Bruno’s system, the "organs" or attributes are often what provide the primary structure.</note>.

Furthermore, if animate subjects are better known to you than inanimate ones—which is necessarily the case—then what, I ask, prevents you from assigning many things to the same living figure in the mind (provided they are separated by location), or from assigning a different region to those same figures when placed in the same order? In this way, as you pass through different <term>climates</term> <note>term: climates; from the Latin "climata," referring here to specific zones, wheels, or divisions within Bruno's complex memory palace system.</note>, the same <term>attributes</term> <note>term: attributes; from "adiectibilia," literally "things that can be added." These are the secondary images or symbols attached to a primary figure to encode specific pieces of data.</note> can be conveniently applied for the memory of things rather than merely for the memory of words.

## ANOTHER METHOD OF THE ART.

If it pleases you to assign frequent and perpetual actions to the animate subjects, you might imagine that a hundred <term>organs</term> <note>term: organs; "organa," meaning the tools, instruments, or objects used by the memory figures to signify different letters or concepts.</note> are added to them as the opportunity to explain things arises. These might include things that follow from them, or accompany them, or things that move, disturb, turn away, or suddenly arrive; if you do this, perhaps you will succeed happily.

<summary>Bruno argues that because humans remember living beings better than static places, mnemonic agents should be layered with numerous tools and actions to increase their information-carrying capacity. He emphasizes a flexible system where many attributes (organs) can be attached to a single figure across different mental "climates."</summary>
<keywords>memory agents, mnemonic organs, memory climates, mental attributes, Giordano Bruno, artificial memory, animata subiecta</keywords>

> It roars something different if it looks toward the glowing sunrise;
> First its face must be shown where the horses of the sun appear. <note>original: "rutilum... ortum." This refers to the Eastern orientation of the memory figure.</note>
> It does not cry out in the same way when, late in the day,
> the last four-legged beasts of your chariot, Phoebus, show their backs. <note>original: "Phæbe." While usually referring to the moon, in this context of the sun's chariot horses, it refers to the solar deity Phoebus Apollo. This indicates the Western orientation (sunset).</note>
> Its resonance is not the same while watching watchful Boötes, <note>The constellation Boötes, located in the northern sky.</note>

> But if a rocky cave should catch its voice,
> the air is bent back from there, restoring the sound;
> Either the voice resounds back to you, or if practiced use strives for it,
> the reversed <term>elements</term> <note>term: elements; "elementa," often referring specifically to the letters of the alphabet or the basic components of a memory palace.</note> will return a reversed mark. <note>A description of a mnemonic "echo" or reflection, where a sequence can be retrieved in reverse order.</note>

<meta>This page contains the formal dedication of Giordano Bruno’s "Cantus Circaeus" (Circe’s Song), published in 1582. It follows the title page (which was noted as blank/missing in the previous metadata) and establishes the book's patronage. The "Jean Regnault" mentioned here was a secretary to Henry d’Angoulême and played a key role in bringing Bruno’s work to the French nobility.</meta>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND MOST EXALTED PRINCE HENRY D’ANGOU-
# LÊME,
## Grand Prior of France, Governor of Provence, and Lieutenant General, and Admiral of the whole Eastern Sea for the Royal Majesty, from Jean Regnault, Secretary and Councilor to the same most illustrious Prince.

*When a double dialogue concerning Circe’s Song and its application to the Art of Memory came into my hands, Most Exalted Prince <note>Henry d’Angoulême (1551–1586) was the illegitimate son of King Henry II of France and a significant political and military figure</note>, and since the excellence and the utility of this art are well known to me, I judged it most worthy to be shared with noble and generous minds. For it contains matter that is by no means trivial, but concerns things that are most highly desired. I will pass over the fact that, beyond the title and the basic elements of the art, it has nothing in common with other works published until now; indeed, it possesses its own unique characteristics, which will testify that Giordano <note>original: "Iordanum," referring to the author Giordano Bruno</note> is not only the author but also a fruitful inventor. Furthermore, the publication of this art is necessary for its reputation and its defense; for it has happened that, by some turn of events, copies of it have been corrupted and circulated in a defiled state, to such an extent that the author is made suspect to the common people and the Art itself is rendered less commendable. Therefore, I deemed it wise to take that very art—having for its title a preface <term>prohemium: a formal introduction or preliminary remarks setting out the goals of a book</term> (in which the things that can be promised are set forth) along with its progression—of which I possessed a copy, recorded most faithfully just as it had once been dictated to certain individuals. This was done so that, alongside the dialogue of Circe and its applications, it might serve as an enhancement and no small ornament—* <note>The sentence and the page end here, continuing on the following leaf.</note>

<summary>This page features a dedication by Jean Regnault to Henry d’Angoulême, introducing Giordano Bruno's work on memory. Regnault explains that he is publishing this "faithful" version to protect Bruno's reputation from corrupted copies circulating at the time.</summary>

<keywords>Henry d'Angoulême, Jean Regnault, Giordano Bruno, Art of Memory, patronage, 16th-century publishing, Circe's Song, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Jean Regnault to Henry d’Angoulême. It transitions from discussing the corruption of earlier manuscripts to the author's (Giordano Bruno's) agreement to let Regnault oversee this corrected edition. It also establishes the relationship between this work and Bruno's previous book, "De umbris idearum" (On the Shadows of Ideas).</meta>

...correction and clarity, and was afterwards shaped by Giordano <note>original: "Ior.", an abbreviation for Iordanus (Giordano Bruno)</note> to be brought into the light <note>Meaning: to be published</note>. To this friendly advice and opinion, Giordano most willingly agreed, as was fitting, and asked me—since he is himself occupied with weightier business—that I should take up that very care and complete it. 

Therefore, after another art published by him and dedicated to the Most Christian King <note>King Henry III of France</note>, which is titled **On the Shadows of Ideas** <note>original: "De umbris idearum", Bruno's foundational work on mnemonics published earlier in 1582</note>, I have undertaken to publish this work. It possesses a particular and unique quality: while other arts struggled to achieve the *memory of words* <term>memoria verborum: the practice of memorizing exact vocabulary as opposed to "memoria rerum," the memory of general topics</term> through thousands of substituted images and a hundred thousand mental locations <term>loci: "places" or mental architecture used to store mnemonic images</term>, this system will most easily perfect that goal using only one hundred and twenty principal forms (as he calls them) to be subjectivized <note>Bruno's technical term for internalizing mental structures so they become part of the "subject" or the mind itself</note>. 

This is done according to the number of terms and sentences, with determined operations, insignias, circumstances, and assistants. By these means, he ensures that this art requires far less labor, industry, and practice for the memory of words than all other systems that can be read. So much so, that three or four months will offer more to those walking this path, and with more certainty, than three or four years could provide to those following the path of others. 

To one capable of comparing this art to others, there can truly be no doubt of this. Furthermore, as far as it pertains to the memory of things and sentences, it is clear enough that once the instructions of the art are heard, anyone can use them according to their own capacity; and no one of sound judgment is excluded from making progress in it. 

Therefore, since I have recognized the unique qualities within this work, I judged it worthy that it should go forth under the splendor of your name; so that even the obedience of perpetual service, to which I am devoted to you from my whole heart, might also be marked by this sign...

<summary>Jean Regnault explains how he came to publish this work with Giordano Bruno's permission, highlighting that this new mnemonic system is vastly more efficient than traditional methods, allowing a student to learn in months what usually takes years.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Art of Memory, memory of words, De umbris idearum, Jean Regnault, Henry d’Angoulême, mnemonic techniques, loci</keywords>

*you might be able to receive: so that if any reap fruit from the rules <term>canonibus: the technical laws or instructions governing the mnemonic system</term> of this art, they may attribute it in part to you, under whose favorable auspices <note>auspices: support or patronage; originally a Roman term for omens observed by priests to determine the favor of the gods</note> it was published. --- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page marks a transition from Jean Regnault’s introductory dedication to the inclusion of a poetic excerpt by the famous philosopher and memory theorist Giordano Bruno. The poem serves as an allegorical invitation into the "Incantations of Circe," a work Bruno published in 1582 which links memory techniques with hermetic philosophy.</meta>

## GIORDANO BRUNO, FROM HIS BOOK. <note>original: "IORDANVS LIBRO." Referring to Giordano Bruno (1548–1600), the Italian philosopher and occultist. This poem is the opening "incantation" from his work *Cantus Circaeus* (The Incantation of Circe).</note>

About to see the sorceress, daughter of the great Sun, <note>Referring to Circe, the mythological enchantress and daughter of Helios, who represents the power of transformation.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Proceeding from these dark hiding places,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You shall go freely into Circe’s hospitality,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Hardly enclosed by narrow boundaries.
Bleating sheep, and lowing oxen,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The wanton <note>original: "Crissantes" – used here to describe the rhythmic or lustful movements of goats.</note> fathers of the goats,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You shall see, and the cattle of every field,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And all the beasts of the forest.
In various harmony the birds of heaven shall wander,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On land, in water, and in the air.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And the fish of the sea shall leave you unharmed,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In their natural silence.
At last, take heed when you approach the house,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;About to find the domestic things:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For before the doors, and the entrance to the hall,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Presenting itself all covered in filth,
A pig shall meet you; if by chance you cling to it—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With mud, with teeth, and with feet—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It will bite, defile, and trample you,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And deafen you with its grunting.
In the very doorways, and the entrance to the hall,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Lingers the breed of barkers:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They will become a nuisance with their many-fold barking,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And terrible with their jaws.
Unless you are foolish, and unless the dog is foolish,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Through fear of the tooth and the staff,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It will not bite you, nor will you strike it;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You will proceed, and it will not hinder you.
When you have escaped these things with skillful industry,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And are entering the inner rooms:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The solar bird <term>solaris volucer: the rooster, whose crowing announces the sun and who was sacred to the solar gods</term> will receive you,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Committing you to the care of the Sun’s daughter.

<summary>This page contains a poetic excerpt from Giordano Bruno’s "Cantus Circaeus," using the allegory of Circe’s palace to describe the challenges of attaining wisdom and mastering the art of memory. The poem warns the seeker of the distractions and animalistic impulses (represented by the pig and the dog) that must be avoided before meeting the "daughter of the Sun."</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Circe, solar bird, allegory, mnemonics, hermeticism, Cantus Circaeus, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page begins the core text of Giordano Bruno’s "Cantus Circaeus" (1582). It follows the introductory poem from the previous page, moving into the first dialogue between the sorceress Circe and her assistant Moeris. Circe opens the work with a majestic Hermetic prayer to the Sun (Apollo), reflecting Bruno's philosophical belief that the physical sun is a primary gateway for divine influence and the "World Soul."</meta>

# THE CIRCEAN INCANTATION
# OF PHILOTHEUS GIORDANO
# BRUNO OF NOLA
## Arranged for the practice 
## of Memory.

# FIRST DIALOGUE

->*Interlocutors:* CIRCE and MOERIS.<-

->CIRCE.<-

Sun, who alone illuminates all things. Apollo, author of song, quiver-bearer, bow-wielder, mighty in arrows, Pythian, laurel-bearer, teller of fates, shepherd, seer, augur, and physician. <note>original: "pharetrate, arcitenens, sagitti-potens... fatiloque." Circe begins with an "hymnic" invocation, listing the many titles and attributes of Apollo, the sun god, who in the Renaissance was also associated with the divine intellect.</note> Phoebus, rosy one, long-haired, beautiful-haired, golden, bright, peaceful, lyre-player, singer, and truth-teller. Titan, Milesian, Palatine, Cyrrhean, Thymbraean, Delian, Delphic, Leucadian, Tegean, Capitoline, Sminthean, Ismenian, and Latian. <note>These names refer to various ancient cult sites and local titles of Apollo across the Greco-Roman world, emphasizing his universal reach.</note> You who impart wondrous natures to the elements: by whose governing the seas swell and are calmed; the air and the heavens are disturbed and made serene; and the vivid force and power of fires is both intensified and restrained. By your ministry the framework of this entire universe flourishes, leading down the inscrutable powers of things from the <term>Ideas</term> <note>In Bruno’s Neoplatonic philosophy, "Ideas" are the eternal, divine archetypes from which all physical things derive their form and power.</note> through the principles of the <term>World Soul</term> <note>original: "anima mundi." The animating spirit that connects the divine mind to the physical world, acting as a medium for spiritual influence.</note> down to us and further below, from which spring the various and manifold virtues of herbs, other plants, and stones, which are powerful enough to draw the <term>Worldly Spirit</term> <note>original: "spiritum mundanum." A subtle, ethereal substance believed to permeate the universe; in Hermetic magic, certain objects can "attract" this spirit to produce miraculous effects.</note> to themselves through the rays of the stars.

<summary>The sorceress Circe begins a ritual invocation to the Sun/Apollo, describing how divine power descends from eternal Ideas through the World Soul into the physical world. This establishes the philosophical foundation for the "memory magic" that follows in the dialogue.</summary>

<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Circe, Apollo, Sun worship, Hermeticism, World Soul, Anima Mundi, Memory Techniques, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening ritual of Giordano Bruno’s "Cantus Circaeus." Having invoked the many names of Apollo on the previous page, Circe now performs the specific ritual actions—incense, fumigation, and "bindings"—required to focus the solar influence. The dialogue transitions from the formal invocation to a philosophical lament regarding the corruption of the human soul, which Circe perceives as "beastly" despite its human form.</meta>

Be present at the sacred prayers of your daughter Circe. If I approach you with an intent and pure mind, if I present myself with rites worthy of my ability. Behold, we have built for you compliant altars. Here is your fragrant incense, and the smoke of red sandalwood. Behold, for the third time I have whispered foreign and secret incantations. <note>original: "barbara & arcana carmina." In Renaissance magic, "barbarous names" or foreign chants were believed to hold more power because they were untainted by common usage.</note> The purifications are complete. We have prepared seven types of fumigations for the seven rulers of the world. <note>Referring to the seven planetary deities (Sun, Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn) who govern the material realm.</note> The loosenings and bindings <note>original: "Solutiones & ligamenta." Technical terms in Hermetic magic: "ligatures" are spells that bind or constrain a spirit or force, while "solutions" release them.</note> have been performed according to custom. We have sealed everything. Only one thing remains: that we bring forth the desired results of the prayers which ought to have preceded, and which have been repeated according to their proper rhythms. Moeris, check the line <note>referring to a sundial or astronomical instrument</note> and see if the sun still holds the heights of heaven.

->MOERIS.<-

Nothing is lacking. <note>meaning: the sun is at its zenith, the perfect time for the ritual.</note>

->CIRCE.<-

I turn therefore to you, midday sun, by the wondrous power through which you, being one, perform so many things. By the course of your swift-driven horses, you who reveal all things and traverse the two hidden hemispheres. What limit, I ask, is there to things? Behold, beneath a human shell <note>original: "humano cortice," literally "human bark" or "human skin."</note> there are beastly souls. Is it fitting for a human body, as a blind and deceptive dwelling, to be inhabited by a bestial soul? Where are the laws of things? Where is the right and wrong of nature? If Astraea <note>The goddess of Justice who, according to myth, fled the earth as humanity became corrupt.</note> has returned to heaven, so that the earth sees not even her footprint: why does she not at least appear from heaven? Behold, we have entered a Chaos that is hardly hidden. Why are the seas not mixed with fires, and the clear stars with the dark earth, if on the earth itself and in its governance there is nothing that shows its true face? Does Mother Nature herself deceive us? Should I call her mother or stepmother? Nothing ought to be more hateful to Truth than falsehood; nothing more troublesome to Goodness than malice itself. It is not, it is certainly no small matter, O brightest lamp of the world, that of things both visible and non...

<summary>Circe completes a complex Hermetic ritual to the Sun and begins a philosophical lament, questioning why human beings possess the souls of beasts and why natural justice (Astraea) has abandoned the world.</summary>
<keywords>Circe, Moeris, solar ritual, fumigation, bindings, Astraea, human nature, bestiality, Hermetic magic, Chaos</keywords>
