<meta>This page continues the opening ritual of Giordano Bruno’s "Cantus Circaeus." On the previous page, Circe prepared the fumigations and bindings, turning to the midday sun to begin her Great Work: stripping away the deceptive human masks of those who possess the souls of beasts.</meta>

...lest we be deceived by the cleverness of these supposedly rational beings. Why then should we have to experience such hypocrisy in nature itself? If so very few human souls are actually fashioned, why, I ask, are so many human bodies formed? <note>Bruno is expressing a core philosophical tenet: that many who look like humans are actually "beasts" internally, possessing only the outward shape of a man without the true rational soul.</note> Turn therefore to your duties, O Sun, and avenge this great insult to nature and your own dignity. Grant your Circe her mark, you and the other most powerful gods, so that she may possess the power to command the ministerial spirits <term>ministerial spirits: intermediate entities or "daemons" that carry out the divine will in the material world</term> and those nearby shapers of these physical bodies.

I adjure you by these lying faces, these ministers of error, and by the high power of the presiding forces of nature: strip away the human face from every individual of these brutish species, and make their own true, outward shapes emerge. If rivers must ever run their repressed courses; if fires must ever seize their heights; if nothing violent can be eternal; if, finally, all things must reach their proper ends—then let this be done. Is anything changed, Moeris?

->MOERIS.<-

Nothing at all.

->CIRCE.<-

I adjure you again! Why do you tremble? Why do you hesitate, you carriers of forms, you falsifiers of nature's seals? <note>original: "sygillorum naturæ falsificatores." In Hermetic philosophy, a "seal" or "sigil" is the spiritual blueprint that determines an object's form. Circe accuses these spirits of "forging" human shapes for non-human souls.</note> Truthful Jupiter, whose majesty has been insulted by your actions, commands you. The Father of Men compels you; by his power I bind you three and four times over. I also command you by the other gods who hold authority over the various kinds of living creatures: remove the deceptive mask of men and do not prevent the true figures of each individual from coming forth to be seen in the light. Look, Moeris!

->MOERIS.<-

Nothing new appears.

->CIRCE.<-

I shall therefore increase the weight of my third adjuration. <note>original: "adiurationem aggrauabo." A ritual escalation where the sorceress uses more forceful names or threats to compel the spirits.</note>

<summary>Circe commands the spirits of nature to strip away the "hypocritical" human masks from those with beastly souls, but her first two attempts fail to produce a visible change.</summary>
<keywords>Circe, Moeris, Jupiter, ministerial spirits, hypocrisy of nature, ritual magic, seals of nature, transformation, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Circe's ritual incantation. Having previously commanded the "ministers of error" to reveal the true bestial natures hidden beneath human appearances, she now turns her direct invocation to the two great luminaries of the heavens—the Sun and the Moon—calling upon the vast array of creatures and divine names associated with their cosmic influence.</meta>

Again I stretch out my hands to you, O Sun. Behold, I stand here entirely before you. I ask you, unfold your lions, your lynxes, goats, baboons <note>original: "cynocephalos," literally "dog-headed ones," often referring to baboons associated with the Egyptian god Thoth</note>, gulls, calves, serpents, elephants, and the rest of the genera of those animals belonging to you. Kingfishers, swallows, quails, ravens, crows, she-goats, cicadas, and scarab beetles, and the other flying things of your kind. The turtle, the pholis <note>a type of small, scaleless fish</note>, the tuna, the ray, whales, and others of that kind of yours. 

You who are called Ubian, the Averter of Evil <note>original: "Alexicacus," a Greek epithet typically for Apollo</note>, Phanes <note>a primeval deity of procreation and light in Orphic tradition</note>, Horus, Apollo of the day, Dionysus of the night, and Father of Days <note>original: "Diespiter," usually an archaic name for Jupiter, but here used to emphasize the Sun’s role as the source of daylight</note>. You whose power gold, hyacinth, ruby, and carbuncle <note>a historic term for any deep red gemstone, usually a garnet</note> supply to me as your substitutes. You who are revered in the midst of the governance of the planets, foretelling and demonstrating the course of all things: leading forth, producing, and ripening the universe; lord of rulers and counselors, most celebrated with your shining rays. If you are the prince of the world, the eye of heaven, the mirror of nature, the architecture of the world-soul <term>world-soul: the "Anima Mundi," an intrinsic spirit believed by many ancient and Renaissance philosophers to animate all nature</term>, and the seal of the high architect. 

I call upon you also, O Moon. Behold, I stand here before you as well. Bring forth (I ask you) your divers, cranes, buzzards, storks, jackdaws, ducks, geese, and other aquatic birds. Snails, goldfinches, web-footed creatures, salpa-fish, spiders, weasels <note>original: "ictices"</note>, <unclear>iuerse</unclear>, lizards, and all those of that kind: the venomous toad, the frog, crabs, slugs, bone-fish <note>original: "ossolas"</note>, and the rest of your swimming creatures. 

I call upon you: whom we call Hecate, Latona, Diana, Phoebe, Lucina, Trivia, the Three-fold, and the Triform Goddess. If you are agile, all-wandering, most beautiful, bright, white, chaste, unwed, modest, pious, merciful, and undefiled. The archer, the honorable and spirited huntress, queen of heaven, governess of the spirits of the dead <note>original: "manium," referring to the Manes or souls of the departed</note>, goddess of night, ruler of the elements, nurse of the earth, suckler of living beings, mistress of the sea, mother of the dew,

...nurse of the air, guardian of the groves, mistress of the woods, tamer of the underworld <note>original: "tartari," referring to Tartarus</note>, most powerful hunter of ghosts <note>original: "laruarum," malevolent spirits of the dead</note>, consort of Apollo. Bring forth (I ask of your power) your donkeys, oxen, camels, stags, moles, hares, mice, swine, basilisks, cats, monkeys, hyenas, catfish, mice, toads, wild goats <note>original: "origes," likely oryxes</note>, and the rest of your kind of land animals. The eel, the octopus, the cuttlefish, the sponge, and the remaining aquatic things of your kind. Master of the scythe <note>original: "Falcipotens," referring to Saturn's role as a god of harvest and time</note>, ancient one, mature, slow, late, venerable, scythe-bearer, mournful, wise and judicious, profound, penetrator, searcher, examiner, thoughtful, and contemplator. Lord of the ages, cultivator of the fields, inventor of the scythe, governor of the rudders of the seasons, servant of flowing eternity, measurer of traversed spaces, equaling by your duration the impassable sempiternity <note>everlastingness</note>. Father of the father of the gods <note>Saturn was the father of Jupiter, who eventually overthrew him</note>, bringing and carrying away all things under devouring time, weaver of those things that are made, preserver of those things that endure, and consumer of those things that perish. From whom I have so often borrowed the chariot drawn by dragons. You who fathered the gods: Jupiter for the fiery and ethereal heaven, Juno for the air, Neptune for the sea, and Pluto for the underworld. Be present, father of the Golden Age <term>Golden Age: a primordial era of peace and prosperity when Saturn ruled before his exile</term>. Leucadian, Cretan, Italian, Latian, Aventine.

To your tribunal also I turn, O Jupiter; bring forth (I entreat you) your eagles, vultures <note>original: "percnopteros," literally 'dark-winged ones'</note>, white-tailed eagles <note>original: "pygargos"</note>, partridges, pelicans, storks, yellow-wagtails <note>original: "anthos"</note>, thrushes <note>original: "iliades"</note>, blackbirds, bees, and the rest of the birds of your kind. Elephants, hunters <note>original: "subulones," possibly referring to a specific role or a horned animal</note>, stags, satherians <note>an obscure animal classification, possibly mythical</note>, oxen, chameleons, and others of [your] kind...

...most penetrating <note>original: 'penetratissimo', continuing from the previous page</note>, you unjust destroyers, most impudent, impious, and stubborn ones, you shall not escape me. Let the human faces withdraw—let them withdraw from the beasts even against their will <note>Circe implies that their outer human appearance was a lie, and she is forcing their true animal nature to the surface</note>. I command you powerfully in the sight of this sun, by high-thundering Jove, and by all the gods who will avenge your sluggishness and evasion. Do you believe the gods do not care for these things? Behold the sacred letters of the gods, which I show on this tablet. Behold the characters <term>characteres</term> <note>magical symbols or astrological signs</note> which I spread into the air. Behold the trace of the great seal <term>sygillum</term>. Moeris, unroll the parchment on which are the most powerful marks, the mysteries <term>misteria</term> of which are hidden from all mortals. These are the means by which we believe ourselves able to change the laws of nature themselves; why shall it not be permitted through them to restore those same laws when they have been impiously profaned? Add incense to the fires, and the other types of fumigations; and while I murmur these things to myself, look out from the window to see what becomes of the crowd:

**MOERIS.** A wonder to behold, Circe, a wonder! Of so many men as we saw, only three or four remain, who flee trembling to safety. All the others—of whom some retreat into nearby caverns, others fly up into the branches of trees, others cast themselves headlong into the nearby sea, and others, more domestic, hasten to our doors—I see transformed into living creatures of diverse kinds.

**CIRCE.** Nay, they have revealed their own true forms. It shall come to pass that I am blamed, Moeris: unwise men will call the beneficent Circe a "maleficent" one <note>original: 'maleficam', meaning a sorceress or one who works evil magic</note>. Those who still persist as they were are the true men: our song neither wishes to nor is able to touch them.

**MOERIS.** I am shaken with terror, my goddess and queen, for beasts terrible to behold are threatening us.

**CIRCE.** Were you afraid just a moment ago?

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Moeris and the sorceress Circe following the transformation of a crowd of men into various animals. Moeris is expressing fear at the sight of the newly formed beasts, while Circe explains the philosophical and physical nature of the change.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>

**MOERIS.** Not in the least, indeed.

**CIRCE.** Now, therefore, you are given less reason for fear.

**MOERIS.** Why is that?

**CIRCE.** For these creatures you see now—these brutes <term>bruta</term> and beasts <term>bestias</term> <note>In this period, 'brutes' usually referred to irrational animals, while 'beasts' could imply a more wild or predatory nature</note>—do not differ (as you yourself know) from those men you saw just a moment ago, except for the fact that they now openly display the claws, teeth, stings, and horns that were previously hidden. Indeed, I want you to be aware of this: because they now lack that organ which is most effective at wounding the innermost parts of the soul, they have been rendered far less harmful and formidable.

**MOERIS.** What organ is that?

**CIRCE.** The tongue. <note>Circe argues that human speech and deceit are more damaging to the soul than the physical weapons of animals.</note>

**MOERIS.** Gods help me, I fear what they might do more than what they might have said.

**CIRCE.** You are less wise for thinking so. Furthermore, I will show you why you should be less anxious regarding the specific kind of terror you are feeling. For these very creatures—some of whom you see as horned, others stinging, others so many-toothed, others with lethal claws—were previously, as individual men, terrifying with horn, sting, tooth, and claw all at once. Now they have obtained separate and singular weapons with which to defend themselves and harm others, whereas before they possessed them all.

**MOERIS.** By what means will you convince me of that?

**CIRCE.** Do you not know that he who is armed with a hand is more armed than everyone else? Do you not know that the hand lacks any single weapon precisely so that it may be most powerful with *all* weapons? <note>Circe is referencing a classic philosophical idea, often attributed to Aristotle and Galen, that the human hand is the "instrument of instruments" because it can use any tool or weapon.</note> Are you unaware that by adapting stings, poisons, horns, and teeth for itself, the hand fears the attacks of no beast? Is it not by that single instrument that man has become accustomed to ruling over all visible living things? Therefore, rashly and impru- <note>original: 'impru-', likely continuing as 'imprudenter' (imprudently) on the next page</note>

<summary>Circe comforts Moeris by arguing that the men she transformed are actually less dangerous as animals than they were as humans, because they have lost the "weapon" of the tongue and the versatility of the human hand.</summary>
<keywords>Circe, Moeris, metamorphosis, human nature, the hand, the tongue, weapons of nature, animal transformation</keywords>

In the preceding text, Circe argued that men transformed into animals are actually less dangerous than they were as humans because they have lost the "weapon" of the deceitful tongue. ...evidently you have been made more fearful. Therefore, drive all dread from your soul, dispel all ambiguity, and proceed with me to the examination of these creatures.

**MOERIS.** I cannot help but fear the horns present here, fitted and turned against us—unarmed and weak as we are—and the other instruments of death that I see.

**CIRCE.** Hasten safely; with a simple song <term>carmine</term> <note>original: "carmine"; while this can mean a poem or song, in Bruno's magical context it refers to a powerful incantation or spell.</note> we shall overcome all things.

**MOERIS.** If you, a powerful goddess, promise this, I proceed without any hesitation.

**CIRCE.** First, let us track these domestic animals. Look, those pigs <term>porcos</term> closest to us who have fled toward the houses; you would have recognized them most easily of all beneath their human husk <term>cortice</term> <note>original: "cortice"; literally meaning "bark" or "rind," Bruno uses this to describe the external human form as merely a shell covering the animalistic nature within.</note>.

**MOERIS.** Most easily indeed.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Circe and Moeris. Having identified the pigs as the first group of transformed humans to examine, Circe now provides a mnemonic "primer" of their vices, organized alphabetically. This reflects Giordano Bruno’s interest in the "Art of Memory," where lists of characteristics are attached to specific symbols to aid in recall and moral meditation.</meta>

<page-num>196</page-num>

**CIRCE.** For the pig is an animal that is:
**A**, avaricious.
**B**, barbaric.
**C**, caked in filth <note>original: "coenosum"; referring to one who dwells in mud or mire.</note>.
**D**, durable <note>original: "durum"; can also imply being hard-hearted or insensitive.</note>.
**E**, erring.
**F**, foul-smelling.
**G**, gluttonous.
**H**, heavy-headed <note>original: "hebes"; meaning dull, blunt, or stupid.</note>.
**K**, knotty-headed <note>original: "Kapitosum"; a Latinization of a term for being stubborn or headstrong.</note>.
**L**, lascivious.
**M**, meddlesome.
**N**, nefarious.
**O**, over-idle.
**P**, persistent.
**Q**, quarrelsome.
**R**, rustic.
**S**, stupid.
**T**, tumid <note>original: "turgidum"; swollen with pride or excess.</note>.
**V**, vile.
**X**, lunatic.
**Y**, wide-eared.
**Z**, zig-zagging <note>original: "mutabile"; literally "changeable" or "fickle," placed at Z to signify a wandering or unstable nature.</note>, and, good for nothing unless dead.

**MOERIS.** When you were establishing this porcine primer <term>elementarium</term> <note>An "elementarium" was a basic schoolbook for learning the elements of the alphabet; here, it refers to the basic "elements" of a pig's character.</note>, Circe, you overlooked one element of the greatest necessity.

**CIRCE.** That was not done without consideration, because that very element is both implicit in all the other elements, and it appears to be the "element of the elements" itself. Therefore, let all traits be brought forth in this one, just as this one was brought forth in all the others.
**A** ungrateful.
**B** unclean.
**C** unconsidered <note>original: "inconsultum"; acting without judgment or rashly.</note>.
**D** unfaithful.
**E** unstable.
**F** impatient.
**G** undiscerning.
**H** uncivilized.
**I** impudent.
**K** impetuous.
**L** incautious.
**M** ill-omened.
**N** inept.
**O** iniquitous.
**P** inhuman.
**Q** implacable.
**R** irreverent.
**S** inquiet.
**T** insane.
**V** intemperate.
**X** ignoble.
**Y** uncultivated.
**Z** inhospitable.
And unmindful <note>The second list uses the prefix "in-" (or its variants "im-", "ig-") for every entry, emphasizing the pig as a creature of "negation" or lack of human virtue.</note>.

**MOERIS.** And I shall consider the creature by the numbers of its natural features.
1. Having small eyes, and these serving nothing but gluttony.
2. It has sharp ears.
3. Very wide jaws.
4. Filth placed near all its nostrils.
5. Destructive teeth.
6. A narrow—I mean to say a "straitened"—forehead.
7. A rather fatty brain.
8. A tail that is always moving, always knotting itself, but never actually tied in a knot; as if always busy, yet never accomplishing anything.
9. It has a single, rather large belly.
10. It never loses its teeth.
11. Inside its bones, nothing...

<summary>Circe and Moeris categorize the vices of the pig using alphabetical mnemonics and physical observations, characterizing the animal as the embodiment of greed, filth, and mindlessness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the list of characteristics defining the "porcine" nature in humans, concluding the twenty-four points of the pig before moving on to the next transformation: the dog. Moeris and Circe discuss how these animalistic traits reveal the true character of people, even when they appear in human form.</meta>

...or you will find but a small amount of marrow <note>original: "modicum reperies medullæ"; referring to a lack of inner substance or spirit.</note>. 
12. Of all four-legged animals, it changes or loses its hair with difficulty. 
13. It has its own familiar kind of lice. 
14. It has a specific voice for mating. 
15. Its female is louder than the male himself. 
16. It rages most fiercely during the time of mating. 
17. It is the most fertile of many animals. 
18. It is not consistent in eating only one food. 
19. It easily grows accustomed to all kinds of food. 
20. It rejoices greatly in the change and variety of fodder. 
21. The meat of a pig that has been fed on acorns tastes stronger and better. 
22. it is divided into the domestic and the wild kind. 
23. It lives more delightfully wherever it finds mud. 
24. It is altogether beastly.

Since the pig has so many identifying signs, who would not easily recognize one (no matter how much it lies hidden under a human form)? If it seems right to you, mistress, let us pursue the rest by a single and more suitable sign; for it seems better to touch upon many things lightly than to attempt to fully grasp two, or even one.

**CIRCE.** It shall be done so.

### THE FIRST QUESTION

**MOERIS.** Regarding the several types of dogs I see—omitting the others, such as this biting and specifically Cynic <term>Cynica</term> <note>The word "Cynic" comes from the Greek word for dog (*kyon*). Moeris refers here to both the animal and the "Cynic" philosophers known for their biting criticism of society.</note> kind, which is no less famous than the pig itself—how could I have recognized that cowardly kind of dog under a human figure?

**CIRCE.** It is that very race of barbarians which condemns and carps at whatever it does not understand; just as cowardly dogs, recognizable by their very appearance, bark at strangers.

<summary>Circe concludes her list of twenty-four porcine traits and begins describing the "Cynic" dog as a metaphor for ignorant people who criticize what they cannot comprehend.</summary>

<page-num>198</page-num>

...all those who are even beneficial, and gentler toward even those known to be lost and most wicked.

## QUESTION II

**MOERIS.** For now, I shall pass over the donkeys: for they will be considered more seriously and more maturely at another time. These sons of donkeys—namely the mules—by what mark could I have recognized them?

**CIRCE.** These are the ones who would be held as philosophers and eloquent speakers, yet were neither philosophers nor eloquent. They boast of being poets and orators, yet are neither. Under the title of lawyers <term>legistarum</term> <note>Specifically those who study or practice civil law.</note> and scholars <term>scholasticorum</term>, they would be considered as neither the one nor the other. As grammarians and debaters, they would fail in both duties. As merchants and nobles, they would fall into a more secret kind of lowliness. As men of the gown <note>original: "togati"; referring to the civil or legal profession.</note> and men of arms, they would be unskilled in both letters and war. As courtiers and religious men, they would present themselves as an animal of an irregular kind <note>original: "etherocliti generis"; from the Greek *heteroklitos*, meaning "differently leaned" or "irregular," usually referring to grammatical exceptions.</note>. As the beautiful and the terrible, they would produce neither woman nor man. Just as when they are born from a horse for a mother and a donkey for a father, they are neither horses nor donkeys, and they possess a bray mixed with a neigh.

## QUESTION III

**MOERIS.** What would the goats signify to me?

**CIRCE.** Either the "goatish" odor <note>A common metaphor for lust or the stench of vice.</note>, or the fact that as long as they live, they continue to mate. Or perhaps this: that they rejoice when they see a companion mating with their own female; then they wiggle <note>original: "crissant"; a specific term for the rhythmic movement of the hips during intercourse.</note> for joy and leap about like rams.

<summary>Circe explains how to identify 'mules'—people who are mediocre hybrids of different professions without mastering any—and 'goats,' who represent constant lust and voyeuristic joy.</summary>
<keywords>mules, goats, mediocrity, hypocrisy, lawyers, scholars, lust, animal metaphors</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Moeris and Circe from page 198. Having previously categorized humans who resemble donkeys, mules, and goats, the speakers now turn to three distinct classes of "monkeys"—symbolizing those who are poor imitators of excellence, social parasites, or over-indulgent parents.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>

### QUESTION IV

**MOERIS.** How could I have recognized the monkeys?

**CIRCE.** Either by the very shape of their nose, or by the fact that although they strove for all the finest things—such as the best poetry, wisdom, oratory, or history—they nonetheless conducted themselves most unsuccessfully in all of them. I say this because, by reaching for the best, they fell into the worst. Just as you see now, because they imitate man (the most beautiful of animals), they become by that very act the most deformed of all.

**MOERIS.** Notwithstanding the saying that to a monkey, a monkey is beautiful <note>original: "simius simio pulcher"; a common Latin proverb suggesting that even the flawed or ugly find beauty in their own kind.</note>.

### QUESTION V

**MOERIS.** How would I have distinguished that other kind of monkeys from the first?

**CIRCE.** Those were useless in serious and weighty matters; they pleased the great lords by flattering, by acting <note>original: "hystrionando"; referring to the histriones or stage-players of the ancient world</note>, and by playing the <term>parasite</term> <note>In this context, a "parasite" is a social hanger-on who trades flattery and entertainment for free meals.</note>. Just as they are now not strong enough to carry burdens with the donkeys, to go to war with the horses, to plow with the oxen, or to feed others after death like the pigs: they only serve to provoke laughter.

### QUESTION VI

**MOERIS.** There is also a third kind of monkeys set apart; look toward the banks of the river—what did that indicate?

**CIRCE.** It was to see a barbaric kind of parents, raising unrefined, uncivilized, and ill-born children, while through immoderate and irrational affection they indulge their habits... <note>The text ends mid-word at "indul-".</note>

<summary>Circe describes three types of 'monkeys' in human form: those who fail at intellectual imitation, those who serve as useless social parasites, and those who spoil their children through irrational affection.</summary>
<keywords>monkeys, imitation, flattery, parasites, education, social hierarchy, deformity, Circe, Moeris</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Moeris and Circe. It completes the discussion regarding the third class of "monkeys" (foolish parents) and moves into a series of questions identifying other animals that represent specific human vices: the camel (the corrupter of wisdom), the giraffe (the hypocritical or clumsy scholar), and the hyena (the deceitful flatterer).</meta>

...doing; so that you now see them in their own form killing their own offspring by embracing them too tightly <note>original: "catulos suos strictius amplexando necare"; this refers to the folklore that mother monkeys accidentally crush the infants they love most through over-affection.</note>.

## QUESTION VII

**MOERIS.** By what mark would you have distinguished the camels?
**CIRCE.** I shall tell you. While they were hiding under a human appearance, they were not in the least delighted by pure things, but rather approved of everything defiled according to their own custom. Among their kind are those who prefer to receive—or to be thought of as receiving—the monuments of the wise when they have been infected with childish and filthy additions. Just as you see the camel now, they do not enjoy a drink unless the water has been muddied by the trampling of feet <note>A reference to the ancient belief, recorded by Pliny the Elder, that camels prefer to drink dirty, stirred-up water rather than clear streams.</note>.

## QUESTION VIII

**MOERIS.** I do not recognize this next kind, which is very similar in its head. For a horse’s neck is joined to a camel’s head, and with its back stained with spots it resembles a tiger, while its feet are like those of an ox.
**CIRCE.** They call these <term>Cameleopards</term> <note>original: "Cameleopardales"; the archaic name for a giraffe, which ancient observers believed was a hybrid of a camel and a leopard (or "tiger" as Moeris suggests here).</note>. You could have identified them because by ritual they were worshippers of the gods, but by their voice they were executioners; in their lives they were unclean with diverse kinds of vices, and in the records of their writings they were "cowherds" <note>original: "bubulci"; here used metaphorically to describe someone who writes in a crude, rustic, or unrefined manner, despite handling "sacred" or "wise" subjects.</note>.

## QUESTION IX

**MOERIS.** What were the gestures of the hyena?
**CIRCE.** They used to flatter with obedience so that they might lead others to destruction; just as they now know how to mimic the human voice <note>According to medieval bestiaries, the hyena was said to imitate the sound of a human calling a name to lure people out of their homes and devour them.</note>,

<summary>Circe explains how specific animals reflect human moral failings, including camels who prefer "muddied" wisdom, giraffes who represent hypocritical ritualists, and hyenas who use flattery to destroy.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, camels, giraffes, hyenas, vice, corruption of texts, hypocrisy, flattery, animal symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Moeris and Circe, a philosophical exploration of how human vices transform the soul into animal shapes. The previous page ended with a description of the "cameleopard" (giraffe) as a symbol of religious hypocrisy; this page concludes the discussion of the hyena (representing those who seek out human filth and dregs) and introduces the stag (representing selective hearing in courtiers) and the elephant.</meta>

...and to call men by the very names they have heard, so that they may tear them to pieces <note>A reference to the ancient belief, mentioned by Pliny the Elder, that hyenas could mimic human speech and would call out a person's name to lure them out into the woods to be devoured.</note>.

## QUESTION X

**MOERIS.** There appears to stand here another kind of hyena.
**CIRCE.** You would have recognized the very same creature under different actions. See how they run toward human waste; if it were hung higher (so that they could not reach it), they would die exhausted by the stretching of their bodies. When these creatures were humans, whatever was most foul was considered the sweetest, and from the best things they were accustomed to take only the worst. It is just as it appears in the bags through which wine is strained: for they, while pouring out the wine, keep only the dregs <note>original: "feces"; in this context, the dregs or sediment of the wine.</note> for themselves. <note>This allegory describes those who ignore the virtues or "good wine" of others and instead focus only on their sins and failures, much like a strainer that catches only the waste.</note>

## QUESTION XI

**MOERIS.** Is that the race of **stags** <note>original: "ceruorum"</note> so famously horned?
**CIRCE.** These people were hiding under the cover of certain princes; if something pleased them, they perceived it from a distance, but if something was less pleasing, no matter how much you shouted, they did not understand it. Just as now: if they prick up their ears, they are capable of the sharpest hearing; if they lower them, they are stone deaf. <note>The stag here represents the fickle courtier or counselor who practices selective hearing, listening only to what brings them profit or favor while ignoring the cries of the needy or the truth of a difficult situation.</note>

## QUESTION XII

**MOERIS.** What would have brought us to the sight of **elephants**?
**CIRCE.** This: that they had nostrils in place of a hand, or... <note>The text refers to the elephant's trunk, which ancient writers often described as a "hand" (manus) because of its dexterity.</note>

<summary>Circe explains the moral symbolism of the hyena, representing those who dwell on human faults, and the stag, representing the selective hearing of courtiers. The page concludes as they begin to discuss the nature of the elephant.</summary>

<keywords>hyena, stag, elephant, court life, selective hearing, moral allegory, Circe, Moeris, vice, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Moeris and Circe. The previous page discussed the nature of those who act like "stags" at court—individuals who selectively hear only what pleases them. This page concludes that thought by describing their judgmental nature before moving on to the symbolic transformations of bears, lions, and porcupines.</meta>

...because lacking hands, they used their noses: for since they knew how to do nothing themselves, they occupied themselves solely with judging the deeds of others. <note>This concludes the previous section regarding critics who, having no creative talents of their own, "sniff out" and judge the work of others.</note>

## QUESTION XIII

**MOERIS.** Who would have scented out these **bears**?
**CIRCE.** Anyone who had experienced their stubborn, barbaric, and savage nature—those whom the bear brought forth by giving birth, fostering, nourishing, and licking. For no matter how much this animal is shaped by the tongue and other "soothings" of the mouth from an unformed and raw birth, it still grows up to be a wild, harsh, and mountain-dwelling beast. <note>Circe refers to the ancient folklore belief, popularized by writers like Pliny the Elder, that bear cubs were born as formless lumps of flesh and that the mother bear literally "licked them into shape" (original: "lambendo"). Here, it serves as a metaphor for a nature so fundamentally wild that even "shaping" cannot civilize it.</note>

## QUESTION XIIII

**MOERIS.** Who would have recognized the **lions**?
**CIRCE.** He who considered that although they were princes, they had to fear the insults of those with the least power. Just as now, by their very nature, they are driven to fear the crest and the crowing of a rooster. <note>Ancient naturalists claimed that the lion, despite its status as the king of beasts, was inexplicably terrified by the sight of a rooster's comb and the sound of its voice.</note>
**MOERIS.** Was it for that reason they were changed into wild beasts?
**CIRCE.** Not at all; rather, they were already lions, only with an added madness.

## QUESTION XV

**MOERIS.** I leave those other greater matters to be considered at another time. By what mark could you have pointed out the **porcupine** <note>original: "istricem"</note> to me?
**CIRCE.** Do you not see that it does not hurl its quills and spines unless it is provoked, summoned, incited, and compelled?
**MOERIS.** I understand. <note>The porcupine is presented here as a symbol of a defensive temperament; a person who is prickly and dangerous to touch, but who only attacks when forced into a confrontation.</note>

<summary>Circe explains the symbolic vices associated with bears (innate wildness), lions (the anxiety of power and its strange fears), and porcupines (defensive aggression).</summary>
<keywords>transformation, bears, lions, porcupines, folklore, animal symbolism, moral philosophy, Circe, Moeris</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sorceress Circe and Moeris. On the previous page, they discussed the transformation of people into bears and lions based on their stubborn or fearful temperaments. Here, they transition to those who have become hedgehogs and turtles—specifically those whose behavior at the courts of princes led to their current animal states.</meta>

## QUESTION XVI

**MOERIS.** I would have easily recognized the **hedgehogs** <note>original: "Echinos." In Latin, *echinus* can refer to either a sea urchin or a land-dwelling hedgehog/porcupine. Given they "walk about," the land hedgehog is intended here.</note>, because just as they now walk about covered on all sides with spines, so formerly they maintained a bitterness in all their affairs, since they kept a harsh spirit within.
**CIRCE.** Truly indeed.

## QUESTION XVII

**MOERIS.** From what source would I have guessed those were **turtles**? And what kind of turtles (leaving aside the others) is this?
**CIRCE.** These were men enticed by magnificent expectations to the courts of princes. They were able to take delight in the luxuries of those courts to such an extent that, afterward, they were not permitted to retreat freely into their own leisure and peace. Just as you see them now—having laid aside their human veil and appearing covered in their own shells—they take pleasure in the heat of the midday sun and expose their whole backs to be warmed by it. The power of the sun has so dried out their shells that they are pinned down by them as if by floating cork; they are unable to swim back to safer and deeper recesses. Consequently, they can now be captured by hunters with no effort at all. This kind of turtle they call "Indian." <note>Circe uses the "Indian turtle" as an allegory for courtiers who become so accustomed to the "warmth" and luxury of a prince's favor that they lose their independence. Their high status becomes a heavy shell that prevents them from returning to a simple, private life, making them easy targets for those who wish to exploit them.</note>

## QUESTION XVIII

**MOERIS.** What do you say about those **crabs** which they call "pinnoteras"? <note>The *pinnoteres* or "pea crab" was famous in ancient natural history for its symbiotic relationship with the *pinna* (a large bivalve mollusk). Aristotle and Pliny described the crab living inside the shell to warn the mollusk of prey, sharing the food in return.</note>

<summary>Circe explains how bitter-spirited men become hedgehogs and how courtiers, trapped by the very luxuries they sought, are transformed into turtles unable to return to their former lives.</summary>
<keywords>hedgehogs, Indian turtles, courtiers, princes, pinnoteras, moral transformation, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Circe and Moeris regarding the moral failings that lead to animal transformations. It concludes the discussion on courtiers who hide behind the titles of their superiors (likened to mollusks and turtles) before moving on to crocodiles, asps, and chameleons.</meta>

**CIRCE.** Do you not see how they hide themselves under the empty shells of mollusks <note>original: "concharum testis"</note>: the smaller ones under smaller shells, but the larger ones under more spacious ones? Even then, one could see many who, since they were worth nothing by themselves and distrusted their own sufficiency, protected themselves with the titles of their superiors and masters.

## QUESTION XIX.

**MOERIS.** Would you have seen the **crocodiles**?
**CIRCE.** They were fierce against those who tried to appease them <note>original: "plaudentes." Literally "those applauding," here implying those who submissively flatter out of fear.</note>, but they yielded to those who despised and resisted them; so that, just as they are now, they are terrifying to those who flee, but they flee from those who are terrifying. <note>This reflects a common Renaissance belief that the crocodile is a "cowardly tyrant" that pursues the fearful but runs from the brave.</note>

## QUESTION XX.

**MOERIS.** And the **asps** <note>original: "Aspides." While we often think of the Egyptian cobra, in this context it refers generally to venomous snakes associated with betrayal.</note> as well?
**CIRCE.** These were insulting, ungrateful, and murderous toward parents, teachers, and benefactors: just as now the young kill their mothers with their bites. <note>Circe is referencing the ancient biological myth—found in writers like Pliny the Elder—that baby vipers were born by eating their way through their mother's side, killing her. This made the snake a symbol of ungrateful children.</note>

## QUESTION XXI.

**MOERIS.** In what way would those ones have shone forth as **chameleons**?
**CIRCE.** Either because they were flatterers and imitators of everything except those things which are seen to be honorable and bright: just as now they imitate all colors except for red and white. <note>In ancient natural history, it was believed the chameleon could not turn white (the color of purity) or red (the color of modesty/shame), thus representing the flatterer who cannot emulate true virtue.</note> Or because the "popular breeze" <note>original: "aura popularis." A classical metaphor for the fickle nature of public opinion or fame.</note> nourished them, and they did not hunt for anything other than human praise and glory...

<summary>Circe explains how human vices like cowardice, ingratitude, and empty flattery manifest in the forms of crocodiles, asps, and chameleons. The dialogue highlights how these animals' legendary traits—like the asp killing its mother—serve as allegories for specific moral failings.</summary>

<keywords>allegory, Circe, Moeris, crocodiles, asps, chameleons, flattery, ingratitude, transformation, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Circe and Moeris. The theme remains the moral correspondence between human vices and animal traits.</meta>

...they were fed. Look at them always gaping with their mouths open, as if they feed on nothing but air. <note>This refers to the ancient belief that chameleons lived on air alone.</note> Or perhaps because they possess nothing inside that very large and most capacious lung of theirs; it is as if the soul-anatomists <term>notomistæ</term> <note>original: "notomistæ," a Renaissance Latin coinage for "anatomists."</note> could have contemplated nothing in those men besides windy boastfulness.

## QUESTION XXII.

**MOERIS.** In a single word, who could have proven by their appearance that those men were actually those aquatic creatures?

**CIRCE.** I will tell you. Anyone who had observed them as willing listeners and imitators of evil and shameful things would have discovered the **lampreys** <note>original: "Sycias." In this context, it refers to "sucker-fish" or lampreys, which were thought to draw out only the bad humors or "filth" from a body.</note>, which powerfully attract only that which is worst and most unpleasant in the body. They would have recognized the **octopuses** <term>Polypos</term> <note>From the Greek "poly-pous" (many-footed). In classical literature, the octopus was the ultimate symbol of the hypocrite or flatterer because it changes its color to match its surroundings.</note> if they had seen how they were skilled at increasing their household wealth through pretense—adapting themselves to the habits of different people and faking the voices of various kinds of animals (as birdcatchers are accustomed to do)—just as they now hunt by changing their color.

## QUESTION XXIII.

**MOERIS.** Leaving aside the species of those creatures, with others to be considered at another time: let us lift our eyes, Circe, to those birds flying toward the nearby woods and high places. Who were the **swallows** <term>hyrundines</term> that build their nests in the very rooftops? 

**CIRCE.** It is said that they represent well enough by their own behavior those who were present for their friends while fortune was shining; but when fortune raged and clouded its fickle face, they turned their backs: just as they are present with us now in the springtime, but retreat when winter presses in. <note>The swallow was a common emblem for the "fair-weather friend" who disappears when times get difficult.</note>

<summary>Circe explains how chameleons represent boastfulness, octopuses represent hypocritical flatterers, and swallows represent fair-weather friends who abandon others in winter or misfortune.</summary>
<keywords>chameleon, octopus, lamprey, swallow, fair-weather friends, flattery, moral allegory, Renaissance bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Circe and Moeris. Following the previous page's discussion on aquatic creatures and swallows, this section concludes a thought on mouse-like humans and transitions to peacocks and nightingales, representing vanity and empty eloquence.</meta>

**CIRCE.** Well said. But they could also be recognized by this: even when they lived among real men under a human disguise and visited human homelands and houses, sustaining themselves on discarded straw and chaff, they could never truly grow accustomed to them. In the same way, mice are unable to live in harmony with men, no matter how much they dwell and live under the same roof.

## QUESTION XXIV.

**MOERIS.** I would have easily recognized the **peacocks** <note>original: "Pauones."</note> as well, for I saw those boastful ones walking with high steps <note>The Latin "altigrados" suggests a pompous, strutting gait typical of those who think highly of themselves.</note>, displaying their feathers and colors, and swelling with puffed-up chests.

**CIRCE.** Certainly. But you should have also understood them to be peacocks because they did nothing unless they were praised; just as now, when someone praises them, they make themselves conspicuous by spreading their feathers.

## QUESTION XXV.

**MOERIS.** I would not have been ignorant of the **nightingales** <note>original: "Luscinias."</note> either. They were a certain kind of talkative people who spoke at length so they might appear to possess great wisdom. Indeed, it was the high opinion of fools that sustained them; to the wise, they are as contemptible as the fools themselves, for the wise well know the saying: *AN EMPTY VESSEL MAKES MUCH NOISE.* <note>original: "VAS VACVVM MVLTVM SONAT." This ancient proverb suggests that those with the least knowledge or substance are often the loudest.</note>

**CIRCE.** Excellent. There was also a certain talkative type of poets who were full of empty melodies.

<summary>In this passage, Circe and Moeris discuss how certain human vices manifest as animals: mice represent parasitic social outcasts, peacocks symbolize those driven by vanity and praise, and nightingales represent talkative people and poets who lack true substance.</summary>
<keywords>Circe, Moeris, peacocks, nightingales, vanity, eloquence, proverbs, animal metaphors, transformation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Circe and Moeris regarding the moral significance of various animals. Having previously discussed peacocks and nightingales as symbols of vanity and empty talk, the speakers now turn to smaller birds and the eagle, exploring themes of pretense, social parasitism, and the nature of power.</meta>

## QUESTION XXVI.

**MOERIS.** Who does the tiny bird represent that imitates the voice of an ox?
**CIRCE.** You would have recognized them as those who were worthless and puny subjects; though they were insufficient in spirit and nature, they magnified their voice by speaking and passing judgment on great matters. <note>This refers to people of low social or intellectual standing who attempt to appear important by using a deep, authoritative tone, much like the "bittern" or certain small birds known for surprisingly loud, low-pitched calls.</note>

## QUESTION XXVII

**MOERIS.** I recognized the **cuckoos** <note>original: "Coccices," a variant spelling of *cuculi*.</note> when they made the wives of others the mothers of their own children—just as we see them now, placing their eggs in the nests of others.
**CIRCE.** You have judged correctly. <note>In the Renaissance, the cuckoo was a common symbol for adultery and the subversion of the family unit, as the bird notoriously lays its eggs in the nests of other species to be raised by them.</note>

## QUESTION XXVIII.

**MOERIS.** Who would not have recognized the **eagle** <note>original: "Aquilam."</note>, which they call the royal bird? It declares itself clearly enough with its hooked and predatory claws. Who would not have heard it screaming while in human form?
**CIRCE.** It is certain that many eagles hide behind the faces of certain powerful men. All eagles are powerful, yet not all powerful men are eagles. Indeed, you would not call your Circe an eagle in this regard, since you assert she is a goddess and a queen. <note>Circe draws a distinction between raw power (which can be predatory and animalistic) and the divine or legitimate authority of a queen or goddess.</note>
**MOERIS.** Not at all; but what harm would there be? Did not **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iuppiter." The king of the Roman gods in mythology.</note> himself, the father of the gods, hide under the form of an eagle?
**CIRCE.** You wander from the point; for now we speak of those eagles who [dwell] under... <note>The text cuts off here as Moeris and Circe begin to distinguish between divine transformations and the moral failings of men.</note>

<summary>Moeris and Circe interpret the symbolic behaviors of the cuckoo and the eagle, equating them to human adultery and the complexities of political power. The dialogue concludes with a debate on whether the eagle is a fit symbol for divinity or merely for predatory human rulers.</summary>
<keywords>eagle, cuckoo, Jupiter, allegory, power, animal symbolism, Moeris, Circe</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Moeris and Circe. ...were hiding in the man we are speaking of, not about those men and gods who are accustomed to hide under the forms of eagles and other living creatures.

## QUESTION XXIX.

**MOERIS.** I do not recognize that bird which seems to be attacking the eagle.
**CIRCE.** They call it the **cybindis**. <note>original: "Cybindum." The *cybindis* (also called the *chalcis*) is a nocturnal bird mentioned by Aristotle and Pliny as a mortal enemy of the eagle; it was often identified in the Renaissance as a large owl or a goatsucker.</note> The eagle and the cybindis were hiding under the appearance of princes; while they were fighting amongst themselves, they were being overthrown by a third invader. Just as they now cling together, tearing at each other with claws and beak, they are brought down to the lower parts of the earth by their mutual conflict, only to be snatched away by others. <note>Circe uses this as a political warning: when powerful leaders consume themselves in personal rivalries, they leave themselves vulnerable to outside conquest.</note>

## QUESTION XXX.

**MOERIS.** Good gods! I never remember having seen so great a bird.
**CIRCE.** That is the **ostrich**, <note>original: "strutio camelus"</note> the largest and most foolish of birds. When it has hidden its neck in a bush, it believes itself to be hidden. Such are the **pheasants**, and such are the **mullets** <note>original: "mugiles"</note> in the waters. You could have seen the bird's likeness when a man was presented who was burdened with a massive body and great weight, but possessing the very least amount of judgment. <note>In ancient and medieval bestiaries, the ostrich, pheasant, and mullet fish were all legendary for their "head in the sand" behavior—believing that if they could not see their pursuer, they themselves could not be seen.</note>

## QUESTION XXXI.

**MOERIS.** Since it is well known that greed and the eating of flesh are common to many flying creatures, where then, Circe, do the **vultures** <note>original: "vultures"</note>—if indeed they are vultures that I see, for because of the darkness that has fallen I cannot well [distinguish] them...

<summary>Circe identifies the cybindis and eagle as symbols of warring princes who destroy one another, and describes the ostrich as a metaphor for physically imposing but intellectually dim-witted people who ignore obvious dangers.</summary>
<keywords>cybindis, eagle, ostrich, political conflict, foolishness, vultures, animal allegory, moral philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Moeris and Circe as evening falls. Having discussed the ostrich and other large birds, they now turn to scavengers and the onset of night, shifting the conversation toward metaphorical "lights" and domestic birds.</meta>

...how would I have recognized those black birds I see there, distinguishing them by a sign separate from the others?

**CIRCE.** Those are the **vultures**. <note>original: "vultures." In ancient and medieval natural history, vultures were often depicted as prophetic scavengers. Circe refers here to the belief that they could sense a coming death or a future battlefield from a great distance.</note> They have been looming for a long time now. Back then, they were grasping at deaths which they had scented many years before they happened; just as now they fly toward carcasses which they sense three days before they exist. But we did not foresee our own coming hunger, and being too intent on those winged creatures, we only noticed the shadows at the very end of the twilight. Now that the sight of the stars has been returned to us, they have distracted us from gazing at those animals. Therefore, let us return to the house to dine.

**MOERIS.** If it pleases you, let us dedicate the whole of tomorrow to inspecting the rest of the sights.

**CIRCE.** So be it, provided I am not drawn away by more urgent matters.

### QUESTION XXXII.

**MOERIS.** But these **night-shiners** <note>original: "noctilucas." While this can refer to bioluminescent creatures like glow-worms, Circe uses it here as a metaphor for brilliant individuals who stand out in "dark" or ignorant times.</note> of a different kind—I pray you, Lady Circe, do not delay in explaining to me what faces they might have been hiding under?

**CIRCE.** Those are the learned, the wise, and the illustrious, placed among the ignorant, the dullards, and the obscure.

### QUESTION XXXIII.

**MOERIS.** What was this so charming, affable, humane, sociable, and dutiful kind of animal, which flew to the domestic perches before us as night pressed on? How would I have recognized these **roosters**, <note>original: "Gallos." In the symbolic language of the time, the rooster was a "solar" creature, representing the sun, vigilance, and the awakening of the mind.</note> as I call them?

**CIRCE.** Since the rooster is a most beautiful, musical, noble, generous, high-spirited, solar, and command... <note>The text cuts off mid-word; "impe-" likely continues as "imperiosum," meaning commanding or powerful.</note>

...glorious and almost **divine** <note>original: "diuinum." In classical mythology, the rooster was often sacred to sun deities like Apollo, marking him as "divine" for his role in announcing the light.</note>; yet he misuses his own nature, and for this reason alone is stripped of a more noble form. For he, alongside a companion of his own kind, most often fights to the death over worthless and idle **hens** <note>original: "gallinis."</note>. The one who emerges as the **victor** <note>original: "victor."</note> provides amusement for the other **spectators** <note>original: "spectatoribus."</note>, and through his song, he proclaims his superiority. You would have seen this creature hiding within those men who are accustomed to wearing each other down through mutual **discord** <note>original: "dissidiis."</note>, and who, appearing ridiculous to everyone else, would boast of the shameful **deeds** <note>original: "facinora."</note> they committed against their own kin.

<meta>This page marks the transition from the first dialogue of the "Cantus Circaeus" (the Song of Circe), which dealt with the mythological transformation of men into beasts, to the second dialogue. This new section shifts focus toward the practical "Art of Memory," a system of mnemonics for which Giordano Bruno was famous.</meta>

# PHILOTHEUS OF
# GIORDANO BRUNO

## THE NOLAN'S SECOND DIALOGUE.

->applied to the Art<-
->of Memory.<-

---

->*Interlocutors:* **ALBERICUS**<-
->**BORISTA.**<-

### ALBERICUS.

**FRIEND** Borista, I cannot be weary <note>original: "metedere," likely a transcription of "me taedere."</note> of having spent part of the little time I have available in reading the **Song of Circe** <note>original: "Circæi cantus." This refers to the first part of Bruno's work, where the goddess Circe reveals the animalistic nature of humans.</note>, and its fictional outcomes. There, I behold no small **variety** <note>original: "varietatem."</note> of things; there, I see many meanings unfolded on the very **surface** <note>original: "cortice," literally "bark" or "outer shell."</note> of the words. I suspect also that the intentions folded deep within the **core** <note>original: "medullitus," literally "to the marrow."</note> are innumerable—of all which, I confess that I am ignorant of what is serious and most important.
**BOR.** Nor will you understand it easily.
**ALB.** However, regarding those things which I might be able to grasp, there is only one that I desire with a **burning soul**.
**BOR.** What is that?
**ALB.** That very **variety** which is on the face of the dialogue—

<summary>This page introduces the second dialogue of the work, where the characters Albericus and Borista begin to discuss the deeper, hidden meanings of the "Song of Circe" and its application to the Art of Memory.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Art of Memory, Song of Circe, Albericus, Borista, mnemonics, philosophy, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Albericus and Borista. Albericus is explaining his desire to master the Art of Memory to retain the complex "variety" of knowledge he encountered in the Song of Circe. Borista responds by defending the complexity of the Art, arguing that mastery requires deep contemplation and the "forging" of mental combinations.</meta>

... [varie]ty <note>original: "varietatem," continuing from the previous page.</note>, so that I might be able (as far as I hear can be done through art) to retain it fixed in my memory in the same order it was acquired, without the burden of great labor. For because of my lack of patience for work and my naturally slippery faculty for retention, I otherwise despair of being able to do it. I hear that from Jordan's <note>Giordano Bruno often referred to himself as "Jordanus."</note> discoveries you possess a skill—not some theatrical one such as others boast of—but an art expressed from reflections on the **shadows of ideas** <note>original: "vmbris idearum." This refers to Bruno's famous work *De umbris idearum* (1582), which suggests that human ideas are not the absolute truth, but "shadows" of the divine archetypes that we can use to organize our memory.</note>. Many judge this art to be very difficult and inaccessible through private study. Some of those who appear more learned than the common people—lest their own inadequacy be exposed, and because they are ashamed to admit their poverty of mind—attribute what they do not understand to the art itself, claiming it is confused.

**Bor.** I would easily grant that the art is inaccessible, but only if one lacks a ladder <note>original: "schala," a Latinization of the Greek "scala," meaning a step-by-step method or guide.</note>. I also admit it is difficult, but it is not therefore worthy of blame: for it is hidden from no one that all the best things (whether we wish it or not) are placed by the decree of the gods in high, difficult places. The fact that many learned men do not understand it by themselves should not be attributed to their ignorance, nor to the confusion of the art: for they can be most learned indeed without understanding this art. As far as they are concerned, the cause is that, being busy with other affairs, they read the principles of this art with very little attention. For it is not enough that they understand the members and all the parts of the **intentions** <term>intentio: in Renaissance logic, an "intention" is a mental concept or the way the mind "stretches toward" an object of thought.</term>; it is also necessary that they deign to reflect upon and contemplate them, and further, that they relate some intentions to others, and compare those relationships with others, so that from simple intentions they might forge **combinations** <note>original: "combinationes." This refers to the art of combining concepts to generate new knowledge, a method Bruno adapted from the medieval philosopher Ramon Llull.</note> of terms. Thus, by their own effort, they might achieve those things which are proposed regarding discovery, judgment, and memory...

<summary>Albericus expresses his desire to learn the Art of Memory to overcome his natural forgetfulness, while Borista explains that the Art's difficulty stems from the need for deep mental focus and the active combination of concepts rather than a lack of clarity in the method itself.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Art of Memory, Shadows of Ideas, mnemonics, philosophy of mind, mental combinations, logic</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Borista and Albericus. Borista is explaining that while the Art of Memory is difficult for some, a simplified version exists for friends. They are about to begin reading the "proœmium" or preface of this specific work by Giordano Bruno.</meta>

...let those who are able to deduce, deduce them. For those, however, who are least able to undergo such labor and reach such perfection, and who only grasp at some kind of practical application <term>praxis: the practical application of a theory</term>; there exists a certain version <term>editio: a specific published or written version</term> of it, shared with a few friends, which is easy to understand for those who are experienced in the study of this kind of art. There, you will be able to see easily for yourself what he <note>Referring to "Jordanus" (Giordano Bruno), the author of these mnemonic systems.</note> added to the inventions of others, and what he invented entirely himself.

**ALB.** The gods are favorable to me: I will seem to have achieved through you the thing I most desired, if the deeds match the words.

**BOR.** Look, I am opening the copy <term>exemplar: a physical copy of a book or manuscript</term>; pay attention with your mind, and consider what you hear, and if you do not understand something, ask. The booklet has its own preface <term>proœmium: an introductory part of a discourse or treatise</term> as its title.

**ALB.** Read.

<summary>Borista introduces a simplified version of Bruno's mnemonic art intended for friends and prepares to read the preface of the text to Albericus. This marks the transition from the philosophical discussion of the art's difficulty to the presentation of the method itself.</summary>

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the formal treatise following a blank transition page. Borista, a character often appearing in Giordano Bruno’s dialogues, begins to outline the objectives of the work, emphasizing a new, streamlined approach to the "Art of Memory."</meta>

## THE INTENTION OF THE AUTHOR.

### BORISTA.

*Our intention is, by the favor of the divine power, to follow a skillful and methodical path: for correcting defects, strengthening weakness, and boosting the power of* <term>natural memory</term> <note>original: "memoriae naturalis"; the innate ability to remember things without the use of specialized mnemonic techniques.</note>*: so that anyone (provided they possess reason and a moderate share of* <term>judgment</term> <note>In this context, "judgment" (iudicium) refers to the intellectual capacity to evaluate, categorize, and use information effectively.</note>*) may be able to profit from it, to the extent that no one meeting these conditions should be excluded from acquiring this* <term>art</term> <note>The "Art of Memory" was a Renaissance system of mnemonics that used mental imagery and "memory palaces" to store vast amounts of knowledge.</note>*. This is something the art does not possess by itself, nor from the labor of those who came before us, by whose inventions we were inspired: we have been moved by long-term reflection to add to them, both in matters that make the task easier and more certain, and also in those that provide brevity. For whereas things following the path of others required lengthy practice, intense focus, and a kind of abandonment of other studies—so much so that more gifted minds disdained such study—now, through us by the grace of the Most High, a task is proposed that is so easy, illustrious, and worthy that no one of sound mind should merely love the art, but should also apply themselves to its study with all their energy and strength. Since this art assists all others, and shows the way, and opens the door to many other discoveries. Indeed, it will function in such a way for memory that it will even contribute greatly to judgment. But why do I linger on the praise and importance of this art? Success will prove the matter itself...*

<summary>In this opening statement, the character Borista explains that the author’s goal is to provide a more efficient and accessible "Art of Memory" that improves both recall and intellectual judgment.</summary>
<keywords>Art of Memory, mnemonics, natural memory, judgment, methodology, Giordano Bruno, Borista</keywords>

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues Borista's introductory speech from page 215, concluding the philosophical justification for the work's difficulty and transitioning into the practical application of the Art of Memory.</meta>

*...proves. There is only one thing that is difficult: that someone might be able to understand these very things by themselves. Under a teacher, everyone will understand. This indeed does not happen because we strive for difficulty, but from the novelty of the subject and the* <term>technicality of the terms</term> <note>original: "celebritate terminorum"; this likely refers to the specialized or "famous" vocabulary used in mnemonic systems which, while precise, can be overwhelming to a beginner.</note>*. Moreover, Plato in the* <note>The reference is to Plato's dialogue *Euthydemus* (304b), where Socrates remarks that "the rare is precious" and that water, though best, is cheapest because it is common.</note> *Euthydemus urges that the most renowned and* <term>secret</term> <note>original: "archanae"; referring to esoteric or "hermetic" knowledge—information intended only for a small circle of initiates.</note> *matters be kept by philosophers among themselves and shared only with a few who are worthy. For water, he says, is bought at the lowest price, even though it is the most precious of all things. We do the same for our friends, and even more intensely: for we are led by a greater occasion than Plato was led by in that purpose. We advise the same to all those into whose hands these things may come: that they do not abuse the grace and gift bestowed upon them. And let them consider what is symbolized in* <note>In Greek mythology, the Titan Prometheus stole fire from Mount Olympus to give to humanity, an act of "divine theft" that resulted in his agonizing punishment. Bruno often uses this myth to represent the philosopher who brings "light" to the world at great personal risk.</note> *Prometheus, who, when he had presented the fire of the gods to men, incurred their indignation. The rest of what is usually proposed in prefaces and* <term>bombastic introductions</term> <note>original: "ampullatis exordiis"; literally "swollen" or "flask-like" beginnings, a classical rhetorical term for prose that is overly grand, pompous, or filled with "hot air."</note>*, we pass over. For it is enough to bring forward the necessary things and those which lead to the completion of the present intention.*

<summary>The author justifies the initial difficulty of the text as a result of its novelty rather than intentional obscurity, invoking Plato's warnings about the value of secret knowledge. He cautions readers to treat the "fire" of this new art with respect, alluding to the myth of Prometheus, before moving directly to the core subject matter.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Euthydemus, Prometheus, secrecy of knowledge, Art of Memory, technical terminology, philosophical prefaces</keywords>

## DIVISION OF THE BOOK.

« The present work is divided into <term>Theory</term> <note>original: "Theoriam"; here meaning the underlying principles and philosophical framework of the art.</note> and <term>Practice</term> <note>original: "Praxim"; the actual application and exercises for improving memory.</note>—namely, into the logic of the art and its principles, and those instructions from which the actual work most directly proceeds.

« Theory has three parts: one concerning the method of investigating the art by governing the <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "phantasia"; in Renaissance psychology, this was the faculty that transformed sensory input into mental images.</note> and the <term>thinking power</term> <note>original: "cogitatiua"; the faculty that processes and judges mental images.</note>, which are the gateways of memory; another concerning the nature of "subjects" or <term>places</term> <note>original: "locorum"; these are the mental "rooms" or locations where information is stored in the mind's eye.</note>; and another concerning the nature of "adjuncts" or <term>images</term> <note>original: "imaginum"; the visual symbols placed within the "places" to represent specific ideas or words.</note>.

« Practice, however, has two parts: one regarding the memory of things <note>referring to memorizing concepts, arguments, or the "gist" of a subject.</note>; and the other regarding the memory of words <note>referring to verbatim memorization of specific phrasing.</note>.

> *for the purpose of preparing and building the memory.*

**§ I** *The order of the powers and the organs.*

« It is well known and generally agreed that there are four <term>cells</term> <note>original: "cellulas"; referring to the "Cell Doctrine," a medieval belief that the brain was divided into physical chambers or ventricles, each housing a specific internal sense like common sense, imagination, or memory.</note> for the four internal senses, through which...

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the internal senses, detailing the four-fold division of the brain's faculties and the sequential path information must take to reach permanent memory.</meta>

« ...of the senses: The first is called the <term>common sense</term> <note>original: "sensus communis"; in pre-modern psychology, this was the faculty that collected data from the five outer senses (sight, sound, etc.) into a single unified perception.</note>, located in the front part of the brain. The second, reaching toward the middle of the brain, is named the home of the <term>phantasy</term> <note>original: "phantasiæ"; the faculty that forms mental images from the data provided by the common sense.</note>. The third, which touches upon that one, is called the house of the <term>cogitative power</term> <note>original: "cogitatiuæ"; the faculty that processes, judges, or thinks about the images formed by the phantasy.</note>. The fourth, indeed, is the <term>memorative power</term> <note>original: "memoratiua"; the faculty of storing and recalling information.</note>. This distinction is of little importance as far as our present consideration is concerned, except for this reason: that we must understand the order of the operations of these powers in order to perfect the operation of memory.

### *§ II. The order of operations or acts.*

« These operations are therefore ordered in such a way that we cannot progress from one to the next, all the way to the final <term>chamber</term> <note>original: "cubile"; literally a bedchamber or resting place, here used metaphorically for the storage area of the brain.</note> of memory, except by proceeding successively from one into the other. They follow the same order in which they were instituted by Mother Nature through their organs and homes, and arranged according to their physical location. For they should be imagined as four rooms or chambers—not indeed separated from one another, but placed one inside the other, so that entry to the fourth lies open through the third; into the third through the second; and into the second through the first. I say for our purpose that nothing enters the memory except through the hall of the cogitative faculty; nothing enters the cogitative faculty except through the hall of the phantasy; and nothing enters the phantasy except through the hall of the common sense.

### *Comparison § III.*

« Therefore, in this art, the same logic must be held as that which we perceive nature itself to possess: so that...

For the <term>imaginative power</term> perfects images along with their internal rationales, while the <term>phantasy</term> especially and properly perfects the locations and seats of those images.

## CHAPTER II.

->*Concerning the conditions of the subjects.*<-

->§ I.<-

« Therefore, **sensible** and **material** subjects—in the judgment of all who have hitherto written well on this art—must first be chosen according to their substance so that they are perceptible to the eye. Of these, some are **natural**, such as stones, trees, and similar things. Others are **artificial**, such as halls, columns, corners, statues, and the like. Still others exist in both ways, such as those that consist partly of nature and partly of art.

->§ II.<-

« Regarding their <term>continuous quantity</term> <note>original: "quantitatem continuam"; a philosophical term referring to the physical extension or size of an object in space.</note>, the proper subjects should not be excessively large, lest they blunt and disperse the vision, nor excessively small, lest they escape the vision. Instead, they should be **moderate**, according to the size of a human being: specifically, a height equal to a man with arms raised and a width equal to a man with arms outstretched. <note>This "Vitruvian" measurement suggests that the ideal mental space for a memory image is about the size of a person to ensure it is neither too overwhelming nor too tiny to see clearly in the mind's eye.</note>

## § III.

Regarding **discrete quantity** <note>original: "quantitatem discretam"; in Aristotelian logic, this refers to things that can be counted as individual units, like the number of rooms in a memory palace, rather than their physical size.</note>, let there be as many subjects as there are principal types of things to be remembered. A moderate and few number of subjects suffice for the **memory of things and ideas** <note>original: "rerum & sententiarum memoriam"; the technique of remembering the core meaning or "gist" of a speech or text.</note>, but a great many are required for the **memory of words** <note>original: "memoriam verborum"; verbatim recall, which requires a much higher density of mental "slots" to store every specific term.</note>.

As for the abundance of subjects—since those that can be selected from a single house or building are often not enough—one must resort to a method similar to what writers do for the physical eye. When a single page is not enough for them to express an entire matter, they join and "sew" page to page, so that what a single tablet cannot hold, a book can. We are advised to do much the same in our current practice: through our own mental arrangement, we should connect **commonplaces** <term>commonplaces</term> <note>original: "loca communia"; the standard mental "seats" or locations used in the Art of Memory to store information.</note> to other commonplaces. By the work of our thought and **imagination** <note>original: "phantasiæ"; the mental faculty of forming images.</note>, those things which in reality are divided, disjointed, and distant from one another may be united, joined, and brought close together.

Let it be done in this way: let the beginning of one subject adhere to the end and boundary of another, or at least be understood to adhere. For nothing prevents you from placing at the end and boundary of your house (which may be in one part of the city) the beginning of another building that is in a completely different part of the city. Likewise, nothing prevents you from attaching the first of the **Parisian** locations to the end of the **Roman** locations, provided that...

<summary>This page explains that while a few mental locations suffice for remembering concepts, verbatim memory requires many more. It introduces the technique of "sewing" distant physical locations (like a house in Rome and one in Paris) together in the mind to create a larger, continuous memory palace.</summary>

<keywords>discrete quantity, memory of things, verbatim memory, commonplaces, imagination, memory palace, linking locations, Rome, Paris, mnemonics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing a "memory palace." It moves from the quantity of locations to their specific qualities—lighting, distinctiveness, and how they interact with the mnemonic images placed within them.</meta>

« ...let it be fixed and established in your mind, that you always understand such a beginning to follow from such an end <note>The author is concluding the previous section on connecting sequences of locations: when you reach the "end" of one mental room or building, the "beginning" of the next should feel like a natural continuation so the memory chain is never broken.</note>.

## § IV.

Regarding **Quality**. All authorities agree <note>original: "vt volunt omnes"; referring to the consensus of classical rhetoricians like Cicero and the anonymous author of the *Rhetorica ad Herennium*.</note> that **subjects** <term>subjects</term> <note>original: "subiecta"; in this context, these are the mental "seats" or backgrounds where a person "places" the things they want to remember.</note> ought not to be too illustrious <note>too bright or glaring</note> nor too obscure. Rather, they should be of such a nature that they are not perceived to disturb the inner vision by their excessive brilliance, nor fail to stimulate the vision by their dullness.

## § V.

Regarding **Difference**. They <note>the ancient masters of mnemonics</note> advise that one must flee, as if from fire, from a plurality of identical places; instead, in every choice, **variety** is to be commended. Therefore, they say to pass over many identical spaces between columns <note>original: "inter columnia"; the architectural gaps in a portico or colonnade.</note> or identical windows, and leave behind empty, featureless spaces. If you still wish to place something in such areas, you can establish some kind of "receptacle," such as an **altar**, a **table**, a **throne**, and other such items <note>The logic here is that the human mind forgets repetitive environments. By adding a unique piece of furniture like a "throne" to an empty corner, you create a distinct "hook" for a memory.</note>.

## § VI.

Regarding **Relation**. Subjects should be understood as being formed, moved, and altered by the arrival of **images** <note>original: "imaginum"; the mental pictures representing the specific words or ideas one wishes to memorize.</note>, so that the subjects may conveniently represent those images. Let them be understood, I say, as being **affected**, just as a page is physically changed by a letter being written upon it; or, if possible and even better, as **wax** is affected by the impression of a new seal.

<summary>This page outlines the qualitative requirements for mental memory locations, emphasizing that they should have moderate lighting, high visual variety to avoid confusion, and a reactive nature that "receives" mnemonic images like ink on a page or a seal on wax.</summary>
<keywords>memory palace, subjects, quality of locations, variety in mnemonics, architectural memory, wax tablet metaphor, mental images</keywords>
