### § VII.

Regarding **Order** <term>Order</term> <note>original: "Ordinationem." This refers to the logical sequence of the "memory palace."</note>. According to the consensus of all authorities, locations are not to be taken up without careful selection, but in an orderly fashion: just as the parts and members of buildings follow one another in a specific sequence.

### § VIII.

Regarding **Position** <term>Position</term> <note>original: "Situm." This refers to the spatial distance between the mental markers.</note>. They should be neither too close together nor too far apart, but set aside at a convenient interval. Otherwise, they will confuse the gaze and the insight of the imagination; just as in physical writing, letters written over other letters, or letters clinging too closely to letters, cause confusion. Similarly, letters that are separated from each other by more than a moderate amount also cause disturbance (though not as much).

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues Borista’s explanation of "vivifying" memory locations. He is distinguishing between the fixed architectural backgrounds (substantive subjects) and the movable objects placed within them (adjective subjects) to make the memory more dynamic and easier to recall.</meta>

...[certain things] which can be added to the aforementioned places, a difference from their **substantives** <term>substantives</term> <note>original: "substantiuis." In this mnemonic system, a "substantive" is the fixed, unchanging part of a memory location, like the floor or a corner of a room.</note> in this: that those remain perpetually the same and immovable. These [additions], however, although they ought to remain and do remain there perpetually, are nevertheless moved, altered, and taken up for various and diverse uses according to the occasion of the arriving **forms** <term>forms</term> <note>The mental images or "actors" placed in the scene to represent specific information.</note> and images, provided that through them something is done, or they are inserted into the action itself in some way. 

These additions are surely able to add as much to the power of the locations as the soul adds to the body—so much so that without them, the locations are considered dead, but with them, they are living. Add, therefore, a hat to the corner, a dagger to the seat, a cup to the window, and other such tools and movable things to other places. For nothing is so small that it cannot have innumerable relations for innumerable uses. 

Now, in the corner while Albert is being beaten, the hat is used for the duty of a shield. Now, while Ideus <note>Likely a name used as an example of a painter.</note> paints, the hat serves for the containment of colors. Now, while the peasant digs, the hat is struck by the shovel; and so, successively, as others are acting and doing various things, the hat is inserted into the operations in different ways. 

Hence, if it should happen at some time that what was placed in a location does not occur to you, you will be able to be made more certain of the thing placed there by the **adjective subject** <term>adjective subject</term> <note>original: "adiectiuo subiecto." These are the movable props (like the hat or dagger) that "modify" the fixed location, helping the memory "stick."</note> itself, as if from a faithful revealer, when you ask: "What was done to you, hat? What was done to you, dagger?"

**ALB.** By heaven <note>original: "Dij me ament," literally "May the gods love me," a common classical exclamation of surprise or emphasis.</note>, Borista, I judge the industry of this instruction to be all the more praiseworthy, and I am persuaded of it, the more I consider how this caution and "vivification" of locations was indeed touched upon by the most celebrated of the ancients, but was by no means fully understood.

<summary>Borista explains how to "vivify" memory locations by adding movable objects (adjective subjects) like hats or daggers, which interact with the mental images to make recall easier. Albert praises this technique as a significant improvement over ancient mnemonic methods.</summary>

# CHAPTER IV.

## § I.

->*On the nature of semimathematical subjects.*<-

« Purely mathematical subjects cannot come into use, because they are abstract and, by their abstraction, they cannot strike or move the **imagination** <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "phantasia." BORISTA.

# THIRD PART OF THE THEORY CONCERNING
## THE SYSTEM OF ADJUNCTS OR
### concerning forms.

## CHAPTER I.

->*What form is, and its various types.*<-

« Form, as used in this context, is not understood according to the **Platonic metaphysical** <term>Platonic metaphysical</term> <note>original: "intentionem Methaphisicam Platonicam." This refers to Plato's Theory of Forms, where "Forms" are the perfect, abstract archetypes or "Ideas" of all things.</note> sense, as if it were an **Idea**. Nor is it used according to the **Peripatetic metaphysical** <term>Peripatetic metaphysical</term> <note>original: "intentionem Methaphisicam Peripateticam." Referring to the followers of Aristotle (the Peripatetics), who defined form as the **essence** <note>original: "essentia." The "whatness" or fundamental nature of a thing.</note> of a thing.</note> sense, namely as essence. Nor is it taken according to **physical reasoning** <note>original: "rationem physicam," the logic of the natural world.</note>, as a **substantial or accidental form** <term>substantial or accidental form</term> <note>original: "forma substantiali vel accidentali." In traditional philosophy, a substantial form makes a thing what it is (e.g., the soul of a human), while an accidental form is a non-essential quality (e.g., being tan or sitting down) that "informs" or modifies a subject.</note> that gives shape to matter or a subject. Nor is it understood in the **technical sense** <note>original: "intentionem technicam," the perspective of a craftsman or artist.</note>, as an **artificial form** <note>original: "artificialem."</note> added to existing physical things which it presupposes. Rather, it is used according to **logical reasoning** <note>original: "rationem logicam."</note>—not purely rational, but **imaginative** <term>imaginative</term> <note>original: "phantasticam." From the Greek "phantasia," this refers to the mental faculty that produces images or "phantasms" in the mind's eye.</note> (insofar as the term "logic" is taken in its broader sense)—corresponding to the **conception of the subject** <note>original: "intentioni subiecti."</note> which we previously defined by dividing and distinguishing it from other things.

<summary>Borista begins a theoretical explanation of "forms," clarifying that in this mnemonic system, a form is not a Platonic idea or an Aristotelian essence, but a "logical-imaginative" construct used to represent information.</summary>
<keywords>theory of forms, Platonic ideas, Peripatetic philosophy, Aristotelianism, substantial form, accidental form, imagination, phantastica, logical reasoning, mnemonics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical definition of "form" in the context of memory systems, moving from what form is *not* (metaphysical or physical essence) to what it *is* in the practice of mnemonics. It bridges the gap between the external world perceived by the senses and the internal world of the mind.</meta>

## § II.

« Form, in this context, is therefore something thought or capable of being thought by the work of the **phantasy** <term>phantasy</term> <note>original: "phantasiæ." In Renaissance psychology, the phantasy (or imagination) was the faculty that processed sensory images and stored them for the mind to work with.</note> and the **cogitative power** <term>cogitative power</term> <note>original: "cogitatiuæ." This is the faculty that links sensory perceptions together to form judgments or more complex thoughts.</note>. It is added to places or subjects according to the threefold difference established above, for the purpose of representing something and retaining it, so as to inform and perfect the faculty of memory.

## § III.

« Of forms, some are natural and others are **positive** <term>positive</term> <note>original: "positiuæ." In this context, "positive" means forms that are "posited" or created by human will and artifice, rather than occurring naturally.</note>. Intrinsic natural forms do not come into use here, because (as is evident) they cannot be imagined. However, among the extrinsic forms that present themselves to the senses, not all can be used, but only those which enter the internal senses through sight and hearing; of these, visible forms are the most select. For taste perceives objects from their very interior; touch perceives what is clinging to the exterior; smell perceives things at a slight distance; hearing at a greater distance; but sight perceives objects at the greatest distance from the very images of the world. Therefore, sight is the most spiritual and divine of all, just as it naturally excels the others. These define the external visible forms in our present purpose; though these are not the actual forms we are speaking of, they are nonetheless the springs from which those forms flow and the mothers that give birth to them. Hence, if the exterior forms are called **traces** <term>traces</term> <note>original: "vestigia." This refers to the physical "footprints" or marks left by divine Ideas in the material world.</note> of the ideas, the interior forms are called **shadows** <term>shadows</term> <note>original: "vmbræ."</note> of those same ideas by us in our book titled *On the Shadows of Ideas* <note>original: "De umbris idearum." This is one of Giordano Bruno's most famous works on mnemonics and hermetic philosophy, published in 1582.</note>.

<summary>This page defines mnemonic "forms" as mental images created by the imagination and explains that sight is the primary sense used to generate these forms. It establishes a hierarchy of the senses and introduces the concept that mental images are "shadows" of higher, universal Ideas.</summary>
<keywords>phantasy, cogitative power, memory faculty, natural forms, positive forms, five senses, sight, traces, shadows of ideas, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

### § IIII.

« But other forms which are internal—the streams and daughters of external things—which have introduced themselves into the **phantastic faculty** <term>phantastic faculty</term> <note>original: "phantasticam facultatem." In the psychology of this era, the phantasy was the mental "workshop" that received images from the five senses and held them for the intellect to use.</note> through the vehicles and channels of the external senses, are the subject of our current purpose. And these can be taken in two ways: in one way, according to their nature, nudity, and purity, and they are of this kind when they first encounter the internal sense itself. In the other way, they are altered, changed, disordered, and mixed; and this happens in two ways: either by intention, or by the disturbance of other natural **accidents** <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "accidentium." In philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential qualities (like color or mood) that can change without changing the essence of the thing itself.</note>. In the first way <note>that is, by intention</note> they can be **artificial** <term>artificial</term> <note>original: "artificiales." Here, it means "created by art" or deliberately constructed by the practitioner of memory, rather than occurring naturally.</note>, but in the second way <note>by disturbance</note>, not at all.

### § V.

« Therefore, there are two types of images. For some are similar to external things in their entirety or as a whole, such as the image of **Socrates** <note>Socrates (c. 470–399 BC) was a Greek philosopher; he is frequently used in logic and memory texts as a standard example of an individual man.</note> or **Plato** <note>Plato (c. 427–347 BC), the student of Socrates, another standard example of a specific human form.</note>, the image of a horse, or a bull. But others are similar to external things according to their parts, but not according to the whole: such as the image of a golden mountain, a **centaur** <note>A mythical creature with the upper body of a human and the lower body of a horse.</note>, a **harpy** <note>A mythical monster with the face of a woman and the wings and claws of a bird.</note>, and similar things. Both types are useful for the practice of the present **art** <term>art</term> <note>original: "artificij." This refers to the "Art of Memory," the specific system of mnemonic techniques being taught in this manuscript.</note>, or even necessary.

<summary>This page defines internal mental images as "daughters" of external sensory input and categorizes them into two types: direct representations of real objects (like a specific person) and composite "artificial" images (like a centaur) created by the imagination.</summary>

# CHAPTER II.

## *On the Conditions of forms or images.*

### § I.

« Truly, as far as forms pertain to essence and substance, they must be of that kind which are most able to strike the **phantasy** <term>phantasy</term> <note>original: "phantasiam." In early modern psychology, this was the "mental workshop" where images from the senses were stored and visualized.</note> and excite the **cogitative faculty** <term>cogitative faculty</term> <note>original: "cogitatiuam." The part of the mind responsible for active thinking, comparing, and organizing the images provided by the phantasy.</note>. Indeed, their kind was made manifest in what was said just before.

### § II.

« As far as **discrete quantity** <note>original: "quantitatem discretam." This refers to things that can be counted as individual units, such as the number of images used.</note> is concerned, they are to be multiplied according to the number of **locations** <note>In the "Method of Loci," a practitioner memorizes a series of physical places (like rooms in a house) to "store" mental images.</note>: and they must be distinguished for the ease of introducing things—I mean that many images should not run together at once, for instance, in the same location. For from this it happens that some confuse others, as occurs with overlapping letters. It can, however, happen successfully that many are placed in the same location so that some touch others as consequences, which were contained in the preceding things, just as **inferences** <term>inferences</term> <note>original: "illationes." In logic, a conclusion or "illation" is already "virtually" present within the starting premises; the author suggests memory images can be nested in a similar way.</note> are virtually present in the premises.

### § III.

« But as far as **continuous quantity** <note>original: "quantitatem continuam." This refers to physical size, extension, or scale.</note> is concerned, beware of small images, and of excessive ones. For the former <note>small images</note> do not excite the sense; while the latter <note>excessive ones</note>, by their extension... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the necessary qualities of effective memory images, emphasizing that they must be vivid enough to "strike" the mind and properly spaced to avoid the mental equivalent of overlapping or messy handwriting.</summary>
<keywords>mnemonics, phantasy, cogitative faculty, discrete quantity, continuous quantity, memory locations, mental images, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 247 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "quantity" or scale of mental images from the previous chapter and moves into Section IV regarding the "quality" or emotional impact of these images. It references the author's other works on the Art of Memory to explain how to scale images for better mental "visibility."</meta>

...are, and they scatter the internal gaze. Indeed, a fly does not move the external eye—or only moves it slowly—but a giant depicted on a large wall forces its form upon the sight only with difficulty. Therefore, let larger things be contracted to a middle size, and tiny things be extended. For this reason, it is said in the book *The Great Key* <note>original: "clauis magnæ." This refers to Giordano Bruno's own mnemonic system and his (now lost or incorporated) treatise *Clavis Magna*.</note>: *Either modest, or modified. Either magnificent, or magnified.* From this, one may gather that something which is itself modest, and powerless to move the mind, can be made great and powerful by the benefit of an accompanying thing. Thus, a shield and a corpse will represent a fly <note>Small objects are hard to remember alone; by associating a fly with a large "background" like a corpse or a shield, the memory is triggered more effectively.</note>, an archer an arrow, a cobbler a needle, and a writer a pen.

### § IIII.

Regarding quality, it must be recalled that those forms are to be chosen which are able to summon wonder, fear, love, hope, loathing, and other similar **affections** <term>affections</term> <note>original: "affectus." In this context, it refers to strong emotions or "moves" of the soul that fix an image in the mind.</note>. But if an image does not provide this by its own nature, let it do so by your own arrangement, purpose, and application according to its kind. In this way, you will be able to **vivify** <term>vivify</term> <note>original: "viuificare." Literally "to bring to life." This is the process of turning a static mental concept into a vivid, dramatic, or moving scene.</note> a dead image (unless you are of a particularly dull wit). 

Hence, if you take from the human race those who are better known to you—the famous, the monstrous, the beautiful, the beloved, or the hated—they will serve your purpose better. And since there are two kinds of forms, namely **animate** (living) and **inanimate** (lifeless), the first are superior to the second. Likewise, since there are two species of living things, **rational** <note>original: "rationalia." Referring specifically to human beings.</note> and **irrational** <note>original: "irrationalia." Referring to animals.</note>, the first precedes all others in every way. For to them, every action, every passion, every motion, and every possible use can be attributed.

<summary>This page explains that memory images should be scaled to a medium size to be visible to the "internal eye" and emphasizes that living, rational human figures are the most effective tools for memory because they can express complex emotions and actions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the Art of Memory, moving from the emotional "quality" of images discussed on the previous page to the practical "relationship" between the mental image and its location (the locus). For whatever all others are able to bear, these alone can do so, and even more. For this reason, it is permitted to call them "worlds" <note>This likely refers to the "theaters" or "worlds" of memory where images are stored, suggesting these specific emotional forms are vast enough to contain entire systems of knowledge.</note>.

## § V.

> "Regarding the relationship, in turn, which is said of these things, just as was said above concerning the subjects <term>subjects</term> <note>original: "subiectis." In mnemonic theory, the "subject" or "underlying thing" usually refers to the *locus*—the fixed architectural or mental location where an image is placed.</note>.
> 
> "I say: do not be deceived at any time by failing to place an image while thinking you are placing it, or by not attaching it while thinking you are attaching it. This is because it sometimes happens (indeed, quite often) that through insufficient application or focus, you commit to your **natural memory** what you believe you are committing to a specific **location**. That is to say, it is as if you are pouring a devised form into the empty air, failing to make it adhere to the subject <note>The author warns against "daydreaming" the image without firmly locking it into a mental room or background; without this anchor, the image will be lost when the natural memory fades.</note> at all.
> 
> "Against this delusion, the remedy is as follows: Accustom yourself to always have the true form of the place before your eyes, alongside the authentic form of the thing to be placed. For example, in the image of an old man, you should vividly see the white hair, the stooped torso, the trembling hands, and other features of this kind.
> 
> "And along with all these details, observe its application to the place and the relation of the place to the object, as if you were saying: 'Look where that man is!' and 'Look what that place holds!' One must judge similarly concerning other forms. Furthermore, I will pass over what was said above regarding **adjective places** <term>adjective places</term> <note>original: "locis adiectiuis." These are secondary locations or modifiers used to provide more detail to a primary memory image.</note>."

## § VI.

> "Regarding action and passion <term>passion</term> <note>original: "passio." In the philosophical and rhetorical tradition, "passion" refers to the state of being acted upon or receiving an impression, the direct opposite of "action."</note>:
> 
> "Let it be understood that the form acts upon the place in some way, or suffers some effect from the place—be it fitting or unfitting, pleasant or sad."

<summary>This page warns the practitioner against the "delusion" of thinking a memory is fixed when it has only been committed to the fleeting natural memory, emphasizing the need to "anchor" vivid images (like an old man) to specific mental locations. It introduces the concept of "action and passion," where the memory image and its location must interact to remain memorable.</summary>

# CHAPTER III.

->*Several ways of using images for the representation of things and words.*<-

## § I.

< Several methods and ways exist by which both names and things themselves can be represented <note>original: "figurari." Literally "to be shaped" or "to be given a figure."</note> with a single image. First, a distinction must be made regarding these modes in general in this manner: of those things from which a sign of the thing to be represented can be received, some retain a likeness according to the **thing**, while others according to the **word** <note>original: "secundum dictionem." This refers to puns or phonetic similarities where the image represents a sound rather than the object's meaning.</note>.

## § II.

1. < We therefore sometimes place the thing itself, if it can be represented by the **imagination** <term>phantasia</term> <note>original: "phantasia." In this period, the "phantasy" was the specific faculty of the mind that created and stored mental images.</note>, such as a bench for a bench, or a horse for a horse.
2. < But at other times [we place] something similar in sound for a similar thing; for example, we place a representable thing which, by its name, triggers the memory of an unrepresentable thing, whose...

« with the **antecedent**: just as you name the preceding **fire** by its **smoke**, 
« and through **fire** we recall the **smoke** that follows it.

« **honeycomb** being placed, we recall **sweetness**; from a **lion**,

BORISTA

# THE FIRST PART OF PRACTICE.

## CHAPTER I. SECTION I.

**ALB.** Certainly nothing more was required for **practice** <term>praxis</term> <note>original: "praxis." ## § III.

### *For the representation of things.*

<margin><</margin> **With the subjects prepared**, arranged, ordered, determined, and
<margin><</margin> confirmed: it is easy to apply the forms themselves.
<margin><</margin> For things perceived by the senses <term>sensibilia</term> <note>original: "sensibilium." In Bruno’s system, these are objects that can be seen, heard, or touched.</note>, one uses sensible forms;
<margin><</margin> for things not perceived by the senses, one also uses sensible forms.
<margin><</margin> Furthermore, sensible forms related to sensible objects
<margin><</margin> take on the true nature of images and models.
<margin><</margin> But when related to objects not perceived by the senses,
<margin><</margin> they take on the nature of signs, marks, and indicators.
<margin><</margin> We have already demonstrated, to a great extent,
<margin><</margin> how anything can be signified, designated, and fashioned—
<margin><</margin> more so than has ever been demonstrated before us.
<margin><</margin> Only one task remains: to assist with those terms
<margin><</margin> that cannot be represented through the previously mentioned
<margin><</margin> methods of images. This occurs when we cannot adapt
<margin><</margin> any of those **thirty modes** <note>The "thirty modes" (triginta modi) refer to Bruno's earlier systematic list of ways to represent ideas through mnemonic images.</note> to the purpose at hand—
<margin><</margin> whether the difficulty arises from our own limitations
<margin><</margin> or from the nature of the thing itself that must be remembered
<margin><</margin> and for which we seek a suitable presentation.
<margin><</margin> Therefore, a solution must be provided in the method that
<margin><</margin> follows. This method indeed excludes nothing that can be
<margin><</margin> remembered, whether it is an understood term,
<margin><</margin> a term not understood, or a signifying term—
<margin><</margin> whatever it may be, provided it is articulate <note>The word "articulate" here means that the term can be broken down into distinct sounds or syllables, which can then be assigned to specific visual markers.</note>.

<summary>Bruno explains that both physical objects and abstract concepts must be represented by "sensible" (visual) forms in the memory, using signs and indicators when direct images are impossible. He introduces a more flexible mnemonic method for "articulate" terms that do not fit his primary thirty categories.</summary>
<keywords>sensibilia, mnemonic images, signs and indicators, thirty modes, articulate terms, Giordano Bruno, art of memory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion on the "thirty modes" of representation. Having established that some things are "impossible to represent" directly as images, Bruno now begins the practical instruction on "word memory"—a technique for memorizing specific vocabulary and abstract terms by breaking them down into alphabetical components represented by people and objects.</meta>

<page-num>249</page-num>

# THE SECOND PART OF THE PRACTICE.

## CHAPTER I.

->*On the practice of the memory of words.*<-

### § I.

When, therefore, we cannot arrange the things themselves because they are impossible to represent as figures <note>original: "infigurabiles." This refers to abstract concepts or specific words that do not have an obvious visual shape.</note>, let us adapt for ourselves a notation of names and of any words whatsoever, to be maintained in the following way. First, let there be men, distinguished according to the number of letters <note>original: "elementorum." In this context, "elements" refers to the letters of the alphabet.</note>, some of whom designate one letter and others another for you, whether by convention <note>original: "ex institutione." This means assigning a letter to a person arbitrarily for the sake of the system.</note>—such as by assignment—or by truth—such as by their own name. For example, let there be for you several Aristarchuses to designate the letter **A**; several Bacchi for the letter **B**; and several Caesars for **C**. Once these figures are placed in a specific location, they will represent these letters for you.

### § II.

Accordingly, let there be other inanimate objects which can be used by the aforementioned men: so that a cupboard <term>Armarium</term> <note>These objects are chosen because their Latin names begin with the letter they represent.</note>, a pillow <term>auriculare</term>, a bow <term>arcus</term>, and similar things signify the letter **A** to you. A staff <term>Baculus</term> for **B**. A basket <term>Corbis</term> for **C**. Likewise, let other instruments and equipment designate other letters for you. And thus, both several men and also several other things and actions performed by men... <note>The text continues on the next page, likely explaining how to combine these people and objects to spell out complex words.</note>

32 <note>This number is a signature mark, used by early printers to keep the folded sheets of a book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page introduces a phonetic mnemonic system where letters of the alphabet are represented by famous people (like Caesar for 'C') and objects (like a basket for 'C') to help memorize abstract words.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the "memory of words" (memoria verborum). On the previous page, Bruno began explaining how to represent letters using people (like Aristarchus for 'A') and objects (like an Armarium for 'A'). Here, he explains how to combine these figures to form whole words within a single mental "location."</meta>

<page-num>250</page-num>

...you will arrange, by which you will indeed be able to commit an entire word <note>original: "dictionem." In mnemonic theory, a "diction" is a specific word or phrase being memorized.</note> to that same location.

## § III.

« But if the word is quite long, you can commit it to and extend it across two or three locations <note>A "location" or "place" refers to the "Method of Loci," a technique where one visualizes a familiar building and places mental images inside it to remember information.</note>; we advise this when things read or recited off-hand must be repeated.

## § IIII.

« Where, however, you have arranged your own material for yourself, you can commit a word of any length to a single location by placing two or more men in order: who, by representing letters through their instruments, signs, and proper names, may present any word at all to you.

**BORISTA.** <note>Borista is a character in this dialogue who often provides commentary or asks clarifying questions about the mnemonic system.</note> As far as the memory of words is concerned: it seems to relieve us in a wonderful way from the labor into which the older arts <note>This refers to the classical mnemonic systems of antiquity, such as those described by Cicero or Quintilian, which Bruno found cumbersome.</note> drive us. To those who consider these things only slightly, it may seem to add little; however, to those weighing the matter more carefully, it will appear quite differently. For the elements <note>original: "elementa." In this context, "elements" refers specifically to the letters of the alphabet.</note> which, through the teachings of the ancients, we were taught to represent only individually: now, for the completion of syllables and any words whatsoever composed of them, we are taught to place the entire term itself in any single location. For the equipment and actions are now established as neither idle, nor light, nor wandering; nor do men simply stand near men and things near other things just to stimulate the memory...

<summary>This page concludes the rules for arranging mnemonic figures to form words, explaining that long words can span multiple mental locations or be condensed into one using multiple characters. The character Borista praises this system for being more efficient and integrated than the traditional mnemonic methods of the ancients.</summary>

<keywords>memory of words, Method of Loci, mnemonic figures, Borista, alphabet system, syllables, classical mnemonics</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "memory of words." Bruno is contrasting his own mnemonic system—which uses a small, versatile set of images to represent any word—with the classical Greek systems that attempted to create a unique image for every single word, a method Cicero famously criticized as impractical.</meta>

<page-num>251</page-num>

...we are thoroughly taught that they ought to be applied: but that we might receive all things laden with many gifts, so that we may see how easily and conveniently we have obtained what seemed impossible to the ancients, is not difficult to see. Listen to what Cicero <note>original: "Tullius." Marcus Tullius Cicero was the most famous Roman orator and was traditionally credited in the Renaissance with the authorship of the "Rhetorica ad Herennium," a foundational text on the art of memory.</note> thought in his writings *To Herennius*:

*I know that most of the Greeks who have written about memory have made a point of writing down images for many words, so that those who wished to learn them would have them ready, lest they consume effort in searching for them. I disapprove of their method for several reasons, one of which is that among the multitude of innumerable words, it would be ridiculous to prepare a thousand images for words: for how much value can these have when, from an infinite supply of words, we must remember now one word and then another?*

From these words it is indeed clear that Cicero considered that task impossible to do; yet it is found to be not only very easy in itself, but it also creates ease for the entire business. As to why he mocks the industry of those Greeks in this way, the reason is that he thought it could not be done in any other way than by assigning individual images to individual words. To attempt that is indeed ridiculous.

We, however, are so far from multiplying the number of images that we arrange complete, few, determined, and famous images to be perfected and filled in individual locations for the expression of infinite terms—whether they have meaning or not <note>Bruno means his system can encode both real words and nonsense syllables or foreign sounds.</note>. Therefore, you have the ability by which you may be able to affix to mental locations both the things themselves (or the "intentions" of things) placed in the <term>Circaean Song</term> <note>original: "Circæo cantu." This refers to Bruno's specific mnemonic system, named after the enchantress Circe, which uses a series of concentric wheels to generate combinations of images.</note>, as well as the very words by which they are explained. People like you should be exercised in these...

<summary>Bruno defends his mnemonic system against Cicero's ancient criticisms, arguing that his method of using a few "determined images" to represent infinite words is far more efficient than the Greek 1:1 word-to-image ratio.</summary>
<keywords>Cicero, Ad Herennium, mnemonics, Circaean Song, memory of words, mental images, Greek memory systems</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that an efficient memory system should use a small, versatile set of images rather than thousands of individual ones. The speaker (Borellus) is now specifically praising the "brief path" developed by "Jordanus" (Giordano Bruno) as being superior to older, more cumbersome methods that could actually dull a student's mind.</meta>

<page-num>252</page-num>

[...unfavorable] things I would not approve. For they seem to lead toward a vain and childish boasting; for as far as the necessity of representing the terms <term>terms (terminorum)</term><note>In this context, "terms" refers to the specific words or concepts a student wishes to memorize.</note> that occur requires: by using the skill of **thirty configurations** <note>original: "triginta configurationum." This refers to Bruno's specific mnemonic system involving thirty letter-combinations or wheels used to generate memory images.</note> you will be able to help yourself. 

In boys and studious youths, however, I would praise no other way besides this brief path and those others which **Jordan** <note>original: "Iordanus." The author, Giordano Bruno, often refers to himself in the third person in his Latin dialogues.</note> established. For the heavy care and long-lasting attention, and the distraction from serious studies (for which they are perhaps rendered unfit by these very acts—for I have found those who are quick and very capable in these exercises to be otherwise dull) are by no means compensated by the harvest of fruit that can be gathered at a slow and sluggish end. 

Through these methods of ours, however, study is not hindered, the **natural memory** <term>natural memory (memoria naturalis)</term><note>The innate memory capacity one is born with, as opposed to "artificial memory" (the trained system).</note> does not grow dull or weak, but the intellect is able to progress. In the meantime, I hope there will be those who will multiply the seeds of this invention. 

Furthermore, although this consideration is so great if compared with the ancients—without prejudice to the gratitude we owe the first inventors and those who showed the way—it is nonetheless nothing compared to other things of which this man <note>Meaning Jordanus/Bruno.</note> appears to be the sole inventor. One of these, contained in short form, I will soon explain to you. But let these words about the art, dictated to thirteen friends, be sufficient.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**ALBERTUS.** I ask you, please bring forth the other art.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**BORELLUS.** Most gladly.

<summary>Borellus argues that Bruno’s "thirty configurations" are superior to older mnemonic methods because they do not distract students from serious study or dull their natural intellect. The page concludes with Albertus asking Borellus to reveal another aspect of this mnemonic art.</summary>

<keywords>Jordanus, thirty configurations, natural memory, artificial memory, education of youth, Albertus, Borellus, mnemonic art</keywords>

<page-num>253</page-num>

# GIORDANO BRUNO OF NOLA

## ANOTHER ART, SHORTER AND SURER,

### and more efficient for the memory
### of words.

Assuming those things which were brought forward in the supplement to the common art regarding the system of **places** <term>places (loci)</term><note>A foundational mnemonic technique where a person visualizes a familiar building and "places" items to be remembered in specific rooms.</note> and **images** <term>images (imagines)</term><note>The mental pictures used to represent ideas, words, or objects within the memory palace.</note>: we now bring forth another art for the benefit of some, which belongs entirely to our own invention. We express this art perfectly in the following riddle.

->*Let twice twelve bodies, set apart, take their place,*<-
<note>original: "Bis duodena... Corpora." This refers to the 24 letters of the Latin alphabet used in Bruno’s system.</note>
->*Which an efficient hand has shaped into an angle.*<-
->*Then let each have five spirits standing by,*<-
<note>original: "spiritus... quinos." These represent the five vowels (A, E, I, O, U) which, when combined with consonants, allow for the "fivefold sound."</note>
->*Through which they can produce a fivefold sound.*<-
->*Then let two elements provide five consistencies,*<-
->*Which you, being skillful, will place in the middle and at the end.*<-
->*What it is, what it does, what it has, what it receives, and what*<-
<note>These five questions correspond to the categories or "adjuncts" Bruno uses to provide detail to a memory image, making it more distinct and easier to recall.</note>
->*Stands near—let these five positions present them to you.*<-
->*Let these establish a permanent seat for themselves in the body,*<-
->*From which even a conjurer cannot cast them out.*<-
->*Meanwhile, they can wander through various regions,*<-
->*And by remaining in no single spot, they can exist in a thousand places.*<-
<note>This refers to the versatility of the system: once the "alphabet" of images is mastered, the same mental "bodies" can be reused to store an infinite variety of new information.</note>

<summary>Bruno introduces a "riddle" that describes his proprietary mnemonic system, which uses a 24-letter alphabet and 5 vowels to create a versatile mental grid for memorizing words.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Art of Memory, Mnemonics, alphabet, vowels, memory wheels, memory palace, Renaissance education</keywords>

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Albertus and Boraso regarding the mnemonic system visualized on the previous page. Having shown the "Tree of B," Bruno now expands on the twenty-four "subjects" (consonants) and the specific physical attributes or "assistants" used to encode complex data into mental images.</meta>

*Albertus.* What do you think he means by this riddle? <note>original: "Quid credis ipsum sibi velle per hoc ænigma?"</note>

*Boraso.* I shall explain as well as I can. You should have twenty-four subjects set apart from one another, which are not adjacent, do not cling together, and do not touch. Instead, let them be situated—or understood to be situated—as free and solitary. You may choose these for yourself according to your own judgment; however, they ought to be of this kind: 

**B.** Tree. **C.** Column. **D.** Well. **F.** Altar. **G.** Gallows <note>original: *patibulum*</note>. **H.** Table. **K.** Bed. **L.** Statue. **M.** Tribunal <note>A raised platform or seat of judgment</note>. **N.** Chair. **P.** Furnace. **Q.** Hearth. **R.** Anvil. **S.** Chest <note>original: *archa*</note>. **T.** Rock. **V.** Pyramid. **X.** Clock. **Y.** Pit. **Z.** Tomb. **A.** Bier <note>A stand for a coffin or corpse</note>. **E.** Sanctuary. **I.** Fire. **O.** Heap of stones. **V.** Fountain. <note>In Bruno’s Latin alphabet, V and U are the same letter; he provides twenty-four distinct objects to correspond to twenty-four letters/sounds.</note>

These twenty-four shall designate the <term>elements</term> <note>In this context, elements refers to the letters of the alphabet</note> for you. 

Therefore, to establish their primary combinations, you should assign five "assistants" to each of these subjects, which will demonstrate the preceding or following mark to you by a twofold difference. There are five **cardinal differences**: West, East, North, South, and Middle. There are another five **situational differences**: standing forth, bending, sitting, reclining, and lying down. There are another five **local differences**: in front, behind, above, below, and in the middle. By these three types of differences, you will be able to quintuple the subject, the sign, and the operation in harmony.

The five assistants, through various acts multiplied to twenty-four differences, can add a sounding element and a consonant. This number of differences may be completed in various classes, so that it may come into convenient use.

For the <term>liquid</term> <note>In phonetics, "liquid" refers to sounds like L and R that flow into other consonants</note> mediating elements, for these and others, one forms the first combination from two, namely...

<summary>Boraso explains a mnemonic system where twenty-four objects (representing letters) are combined with five spatial, situational, and local "assistants" to create unique mental images. This system allows a practitioner to store and recall phonetic information by placing these objects in specific "memory places."</summary>
<keywords>mnemonic system, alphabet, memory palace, cardinal directions, situational memory, Giordano Bruno, phonetic encoding</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Albertus and Borista (previously referred to as Boraso) concerning the mnemonic system. It transitions from the physical arrangement of memory "assistants" (the five cardinal and five situational differences) toward the practical application of these methods to Bruno's earlier work, "The Song of Circe."</meta>

...likewise, you will provide for those following through several differences. <note>The text continues a technical instruction on how to distinguish between the "assistants" or markers that follow a primary memory image.</note>

Once these things are arranged, determined, and firmly fixed in the mind, you will be able to promptly fashion any combinations from the borrowed <term>insignia</term> <note>Insignia: symbolic marks or physical attributes assigned to a memory image to encode data</note> and those other attributes which were once their own.

Now you see how the signs of the twenty-four elements <note>The letters of the alphabet used as primary memory hooks</note> are derived through a <term>five-fold set</term> <note>original: *quinarium*. This refers to Bruno's system of organizing data into groups of five, often corresponding to the four directions and a center point.</note>. If you consider this industrious method, you will be able to advance into many others by your own effort.

**ALBERTUS.** I seem to understand the purpose well enough (unless I am deceived). But I ask you, please deign to grant something more direct regarding the <term>Song of Circe</term> <note>original: *cantus Circæus*. A reference to Bruno's 1582 book, where he uses the mythological sorceress Circe to represent the power of memory and the transformation of the mind.</note>.

**BORISTA.** I shall do so.

## A FERTILE APPLICATION <note>original: *Applicatio Prægnans*</note>

In the first dialogue of *The Song of Circe*, you have two most general forms: one which includes the song, and the other which includes the manifold effect of the song. You should refer one of these to one most general subject, and the other to the second.

Second, in the first of these most general forms, you have seven <term>hypostases</term> <note>original: *ypostases*; here meaning divine manifestations or personifications, specifically the seven planetary deities used as memory archetypes.</note> of the gods. And in the second, you have three genera <note>classes or kinds</note> of living beings. You should refer those general subjects under the seven most general ones; and these others to the general subjects under their own three highest ones.

Third, under each of the seven hypostases, you have three species of terms: two of which are simple <note>original: *incomplexæ*; referring to single concepts or nouns</note>, while the third is composite <note>original: *complexa*; referring to combinations or descriptive phrases</note>. You also have, under each of the three genera of living beings, several lower terms for the com— <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with "combinations" or "composites" on the following page.</note>

<summary>Borista explains how the 24-letter mnemonic system can be applied to the structures found in Bruno's earlier work, "The Song of Circe," organizing data through seven divine figures and three classes of living things.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Circe, mnemonic system, five-fold set, hypostases, simple and composite terms, memory architecture</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between Borista and Albertus regarding the practical application of the mnemonic system. It provides the final instructions on how to organize information hierarchically—nesting specific details within general categories—to master the content of Bruno's earlier work, "The Song of Circe," and any other subject matter.</meta>

...the <term>species</term> <note>original: *species*. In Bruno's mnemonic system, these are the specific mental images or "appearances" used to represent information.</note> just enumerated. Let those be multiplied into a triple set within their own specific <term>subjects</term> <note>original: *subiectis*. These are the "places" or mental locations—often rooms or architectural features—where memory images are "seated" or stored.</note>. These others, however, should be set out in their own respective subjects in the same way.

Fourthly, under these and those categories of the three terms, you will place the individual items belonging to each species, positioning both these and those in their respective individual subjects.

Thus, the most general <term>form</term> <note>original: *forma*. This refers to the structural arrangement or the conceptual image that gives shape to the information.</note> should follow the most general subject; the general, the general; the special, the special; and the individual, the individual. This is done so that one subject includes and contains another, and one form includes and contains another. In this way, you will acquire an easy memory not only of the *Song of Circe* <note>original: *cantus Circæi*. A reference to Bruno's 1582 work on the art of memory and the transformation of the soul.</note>, but of everything that is proposed to you for memorization.

**ALBERTUS.** I shall try it.

->THE END.<-

This systematic nesting allows the practitioner to memorize complex works like "The Song of Circe" and other vast bodies of knowledge.</summary>
